  \item \textbf{Chernoff.} With the exact cumulant generating function and the best~$s$, the Chernoff bound exceeded the tail by a factor between $4$ and $12$, so it stayed within about one order of magnitude.
    With only the mean and the width of the range (the mean-and-support bound), it exceeded the tail of the three continuous laws by a factor between $1000$ and $3200$.
  \item \textbf{Cantelli.} The Cantelli bound exceeded the tail by a factor between $330$ and $710$, more than two orders of magnitude, for both values of~$k$.
  \item \textbf{Bernstein.} With one support constant $b = 1$, Bernstein's bound exceeded the tail by a factor between $15$ and $560$.
    It was tighter than Cantelli in nine of the ten cases; the exception was $\mathrm{Beta}(0.5,5)$ with $k = 30$, whose increments are small compared with the width~$1$.
    It became tighter as $k$ grew: for the uniform law its factor fell from $232$ to $83$.
  \item \textbf{Hoeffding and Markov.} With width~$1$, Hoeffding's bound exceeded the tail by a factor between $8$ and $6500$.
    It was close to Chernoff only for the two-point law with $\Pr(1) = 0.5$, whose variance is the largest that the width allows; in the other eight cases it was looser than Cantelli.
    Markov's bound exceeded the tail by a factor between $2200$ and $8600$.\footnote{This is a numerical illustration, not a proof.
    The script \texttt{tail\_experiment.py} and its output are in the folder \texttt{merge/analysis/scripts/scoping\_checks} of the repository.
    The exact tail comes from the Irwin--Hall formula (the exact law of a sum of uniform variables), from the binomial law, or from a lattice convolution with a relative error below $0.5\%$; no sampling is used.
    For the two-point laws the sum takes integer values, and the bounds are compared with $\Pr(D \ge \tau)$.
    The output also covers tails close to $10^{-2}$, $10^{-6}$ and $10^{-8}$: near $10^{-2}$ Bernstein was looser than Cantelli in four of the ten cases, and near $10^{-6}$ and $10^{-8}$ it was tighter in all cases.
    Asymptotically the gap is structural: Cantelli decays only polynomially, as $\operatorname{Var}[D]/(\tau-\mathbb{E}[D])^2$, whereas~\eqref{eq:chernoff_surrogate} decays exponentially in the deviation.}
\end{itemize}
So Cantelli, the simplest bound, can compete with Bernstein only for short accumulation windows and shallow tails; otherwise it is a coarse fallback that needs only the mean and the variance.

Route~II gives an explicit surrogate law, not a bound.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Moment-matched Gamma or inverse Gaussian.} Matching only the first two moments of a family closed under convolution is convenient: Gamma, with shape and rate
    \begin{equation}
      \alpha = \mathbb{E}[\Delta D]^2/\operatorname{Var}[\Delta D]
      \quad\text{and}\quad
      \beta = \mathbb{E}[\Delta D]/\operatorname{Var}[\Delta D],
    \end{equation}
    or inverse Gaussian.
    The sum then stays in the family under the closure conditions of Table~\ref{tab:discrete_models}.
    But this choice is \emph{not} conservative: a law with matched moments in general crosses the empirical survival function and does not dominate it.
  \item \textbf{Dominating shared-rate Gamma.} Using more of the empirical information than the moments restores conservatism.
    Fix a common rate $\beta^\star$, and, for each mission, increase the shape $\alpha_j$ until the survival function of the surrogate dominates the empirical one:
    \begin{equation}
      \overline{\mathrm{Ga}}(x;\alpha_j,\beta^\star) \ge \widehat{\bar F}_j(x) \quad \text{for all } x.
    \end{equation}
    This is possible exactly when some recorded increment is~$0$.
    If every record is positive, $\widehat{\bar F}_j(x) = 1$ below the smallest record, while $\overline{\mathrm{Ga}}(x;\alpha,\beta^\star) < 1$ for every $x > 0$, so no Gamma law dominates.
    If a share $p_0 > 0$ of the records is~$0$, then $\widehat{\bar F}_j(x) \le 1 - p_0$ for $x > 0$, and a large $\alpha_j$ dominates, because $\overline{\mathrm{Ga}}(x_{\max};\alpha,\beta^\star) \to 1$ as $\alpha \to \infty$, with $x_{\max}$ the largest record.
    The accumulated surrogate is then $\widetilde{D} = \mathrm{Ga}(A,\beta^\star)$, by the closure under a shared rate, and $D \le_{\mathrm{st}} \widetilde{D}$ by Proposition~\ref{prop:st_closure}.
    Its law has a cumulative shape
    \begin{equation}
      A = \sum_{t,j} x_{ijt}\,\alpha_j
    \end{equation}
    that is linear in the schedule.
    The reliability constraint reduces to the scalar condition $A \le A^\star(\varepsilon)$.
    In the absence of repairs, the loop constraint reduces to the row $A_T \le A_{H_1}$ on the cumulative shape.
    The cost is the slack that the shared rate and the dominance margin add.
\end{itemize}

In summary, the formulation certifies the reliability constraint of a component with measured increments by one of five Route-I bounds, chosen per component: Markov, Cantelli, Hoeffding, Bernstein or Chernoff.
The loop constraint acts on the statistics that the chosen bound tracks (Section~\ref{sec:loop_routeI}), so it needs no surrogate law.
Route~II is not part of the formulation.

One trade-off cannot be removed.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Route-I bounds.} The bounds assume nothing about the shape of the law, but they give only a tail bound.
  \item \textbf{Dominating Gamma.} The dominating Gamma gives a law that propagates through the dynamics, but it commits to one family and adds some conservatism.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{The five bounds of the formulation.}
The rainflow model of the formulation is \emph{distribution-free}.
The law of a mission increment is measured from recorded load histories by rainflow counting (Palmgren--Miner damage).
It enters the model only through summary statistics: its mean and, as the bound requires, its variance, the width of its range or its cumulant generating function at a fixed point~$s$.
No parametric family is assumed.

Each bound tracks the mean $\mu_k$ of the damage on day~$k$ and at most one more statistic.
The second statistic is the variance~$v_k$, the \emph{Hoeffding budget}~$R_k$ (the sum of the squared range widths of the terms of the damage), or the cumulant generating function $K_k = \ln\mathbb{E}\bigl[e^{sD_k}\bigr]$ at a fixed point $s > 0$.

Table~\ref{tab:five_bounds} lists the rows, with $\kappa = \ln(1/\varepsilon)$ and $c = \kappa\, b_\ell/3$.
Each row is the corresponding tail bound of this section, solved for the target~$\varepsilon$ and written in the tracked statistics.
If the tracked values satisfy a row and are at least the true statistics of the damage, the bound gives $\Pr(D_k > \tau) \le \varepsilon$.
The rows rest on four modelling assumptions.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{A0 (non-negative damage).} Every increment, the initial damage and every replacement draw are non-negative.
  \item \textbf{A1 (independence).} The mission increments are mutually independent, and independent of the initial damage and of every replacement draw.
    Each replacement installs a new draw, independent of everything else.
    Every bound except Markov needs A1.
  \item \textbf{A2 (bounded ranges; Hoeffding and Bernstein).} Each mission increment lies in an interval of known width.
    The initial damage is a known part plus independent terms, each in an interval; its Hoeffding budget is the sum of their squared widths, and its support is the width of the range of their sum.
    The replacement draw is described in the same way.
    A known damage has budget and support~$0$.
  \item \textbf{A3 (repetition).} The row of the bound holds on every day $k = 1, \ldots, T$, and after day~$T$ the operating phase repeats with period~$H_2$, schedule and increment laws included.
    This is the setting of Theorem~\ref{thm:perpetual}.
\end{itemize}

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Reliability rows of the five Route-I bounds, on every day~$k$ of the horizon.
$\mu_k$: tracked mean; $v_k$: tracked variance; $R_k$: tracked Hoeffding budget; $K_k$: tracked cumulant generating function at the fixed point~$s$.}
\label{tab:five_bounds}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Bound} & \textbf{Tracked statistics} & \textbf{Reliability row} \\
\midrule
Markov    & $\mu$       & $\mu_k \le \varepsilon\,\tau$ \\
Cantelli  & $\mu$, $v$  & $(1-\varepsilon)\,v_k \le \varepsilon\,(\tau - \mu_k)^2$, \quad $\mu_k \le \tau$ \\
Hoeffding & $\mu$, $R$  & $(\tau - \mu_k)^2 \ge (\kappa/2)\,R_k$, \quad $\mu_k \le \tau$ \\
Bernstein & $\mu$, $v$  & $(\tau - c - \mu_k)^2 \ge c^2 + 2\kappa\, v_k$, \quad $\mu_k \le \tau - c$ \\
Chernoff  & $\mu$, $K$  & $K_k - s\,\tau \le \ln\varepsilon$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Width of a range.}
The support of an increment~$X$ is the width~$b$ of an interval $[l, l + b]$ that contains $X$ almost surely.
The width has this one meaning in every bound, and a width of~$0$ means that the increment is known.
Hoeffding's inequality uses the widths.
Bernstein's inequality needs an upper bound of the centred term, and the width is one.

A width~$w$ is consistent with a variance~$v$ only if $v \le w^2/4$ (Popoviciu's inequality).
The width is the only information on the range, and the range may start at any point $l \ge 0$.
The inequality $\operatorname{Var} X \le w^2/4$ holds for every such position, so it is a necessary condition for the pair (variance, width).
The derivations of the bound on the centred term and of Popoviciu's inequality are in Appendix~\ref{drv:width}, page~\pageref{drv:width}.

For example, increments in an interval of width $0.2$ have variance at most $0.01$; a two-point law on $\{0, 0.2\}$ with probability $1/2$ each has mean $0.1$ and reaches $0.01$.

A condition of the form $v \le \mu\,(b - \mu)$ assumes that the range starts at~$0$, so it is not a necessary condition when only the width is known.

\paragraph{Day maps of the statistics.}
Between replacements the damage is
\begin{equation}
  D_k = \sum_t w_t X_t:
\end{equation}
the $X_t$ are the known part, the independent terms of the initial damage or of the replacement draw, and the mission increments.
Each weight $w_t \in [0, 1]$ is a product of factors $1 - \rho$, and a term of weight~$0$, possible when $\rho = 1$, is dropped.

Under $\text{ARD}_1$, write $D^- = G + \Delta$, with $G$ the damage just after the last intervention and $\Delta$ the weighted increments added since.
$G$ and $\Delta$ are independent by A1, and a repair gives $D^+ = G + (1-\rho)\,\Delta$.
Before the first intervention, $G$ is the part of the initial damage that the starting anchors describe (Section~\ref{sec:repair_connection}).

For a damage
\begin{equation}
  D = \sum_t w_t X_t,
\end{equation}
write
\begin{equation}
  m(D) = \mathbb{E}D, \qquad V(D) = \operatorname{Var} D, \qquad R(D) = \sum_t w_t^2 r_t, \qquad K(D) = \ln\mathbb{E}\,e^{sD},
\end{equation}
with $r_t$ the squared width of~$X_t$.

\begin{proposition}[Day maps of the statistics]\label{prop:rf_maps}
Let $\theta \in \{m, V, R\}$, with factor $a_\theta = 1-\rho$ for $m$ and $a_\theta = (1-\rho)^2$ for $V$ and~$R$.
Under A1:
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
  \item a mission day adds the statistic of the increment:
    \begin{equation}
      \theta(D + X) = \theta(D) + \theta(X)
      \quad\text{and}\quad
      K(D + X) = K(D) + K(X);
    \end{equation}
  \item under $\text{ARD}_\infty$:
    \begin{equation}
      \theta\bigl((1-\rho)D\bigr) = a_\theta\,\theta(D)
      \quad\text{and}\quad
      K\bigl((1-\rho)D\bigr) \le (1-\rho)\,K(D);
    \end{equation}
  \item under $\text{ARD}_1$:
    \begin{equation}
      \theta(D^+) = a_\theta\,\theta(D^-) + (1 - a_\theta)\,\theta(G)
      \quad\text{and}\quad
      K(D^+) \le (1-\rho)\,K(D^-) + \rho\,K(G).
    \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
The proof is in Appendix~\ref{prf:prop:rf_maps}, page~\pageref{prf:prop:rf_maps}.

So each tracked statistic follows the repair map $a\,\theta + (1-a)\,g$ of Section~\ref{sec:repair_connection}, with $a = 1-\rho$ for $\mu$ and $K$, and $a = (1-\rho)^2$ for $v$ and~$R$.
Its anchor is its value just after the last intervention.

The maps of $\mu$, $v$ and $R$ are exact; the map of $K$ is an upper bound, which is what the domination argument of Section~\ref{sec:encoding} needs.
Hence each true statistic of~$D_k$, written $m$, $V$, $R^{\mathrm{t}}$ and $K^{\mathrm{t}}$, is at most the tracked statistic of the same name (Proposition~\ref{prop:domination}).

\paragraph{Derivation and validity of the rows.}
Each row of Table~\ref{tab:five_bounds} gives $\Pr(D_k > \tau) \le \varepsilon$ when the tracked statistics satisfy it and are at least the true statistics of~$D_k$.
The derivation also gives the following properties of the rows.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Markov.} The row uses the mean only and does not need A1 or A2.
  \item \textbf{Cantelli.} The row is equivalent to
    \begin{equation}
      \mu_k + \kappa_C\sqrt{v_k} \le \tau
      \quad\text{with}\quad
      \kappa_C = \sqrt{(1-\varepsilon)/\varepsilon}.
    \end{equation}
  \item \textbf{Bernstein.} The support constant $b_\ell$ of~\eqref{eq:bernstein_constraint} is a constant, not a decision variable; the replacement support enters it only when replacements are allowed.
  \item \textbf{Chernoff.} The row bounds $K$ by $s\tau - \kappa$, its natural range, and $K \ge 0$.
    So a known zero damage satisfies the row only if $s\tau \ge \kappa$, and a replacement day satisfies it only if the replacement value of~$K$ is at most $s\tau - \kappa$.
\end{itemize}
The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:five_rows}, page~\pageref{drv:five_rows}.

\paragraph{The Bernstein side row.}
The quadratic Bernstein row alone also admits the point $\mu_k = \tau$, $v_k = 0$: a known damage equal to~$\tau$.
That point is safe; for $c > 0$ it is separated from the rest of the feasible set.
The side row $\mu_k \le \tau - c$ removes it and keeps the branch
\begin{equation}
  \tau - \mu_k \ge c + \sqrt{c^2 + 2\kappa v_k}
\end{equation}
of~\eqref{eq:bernstein_constraint}.
At $\mu_k = v_k = 0$ the row needs $\tau \ge 2c$, and a positive variance is feasible only if
\begin{equation}
  \tau > 2c = 2\kappa b_\ell/3.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Initial and replacement values of the Chernoff statistic.}
The statistic $K_k$ is the cumulant generating function of the whole damage, initial damage included.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Known damage.} A known initial damage $\mu_0$ gives the initial value $K_0 = s\,\mu_0$, and a known replacement damage $\mu^{\mathrm{new}}$ gives the reset value $s\,\mu^{\mathrm{new}}$.
  \item \textbf{Random damage.} A random initial or replacement damage needs its own cumulant generating function at~$s$, which is larger.
  \item \textbf{Lower bounds.} The cumulant generating function of every increment at~$s$ is at least $s$ times its mean.
    Also the initial value and the replacement value of~$K$ are at least $s$ times the corresponding mean.
    An input check enforces these inequalities (Section~\ref{sec:check_reasons}).
\end{itemize}
The derivation, from Jensen's inequality, is in Appendix~\ref{drv:chernoff_initial}, page~\pageref{drv:chernoff_initial}.

A start at $K_0 = 0$ ignores the initial damage and can accept an unsafe schedule.
Example: $\tau = 1$, $\varepsilon = 0.1$, $s = 5$, a known initial damage $\mu_0 = 0.6$, and one mission that adds $0$ or $0.5$ with probability $1/2$ each.
The cumulant generating function of the mission at~$s$ is $\ln(0.5 + 0.5\,e^{2.5}) = 1.886$.
The true failure probability is $0.5$.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item With $K_0 = 0$ the row reads
    \begin{equation}
      1.886 - 5 = -3.11 \le \ln 0.1 = -2.30
    \end{equation}
    and accepts the schedule.
  \item With $K_0 = s\,\mu_0 = 3$ the row reads
    \begin{equation}
      4.886 - 5 = -0.11 > -2.30
    \end{equation}
    and rejects it.
\end{itemize}

In the same way, the Hoeffding budget of a known damage is $0$, because its range has width~$0$, and a random initial or replacement damage needs its own budget.
So the defaults of the formulation describe a known initial damage and a known replacement damage: zero variance, zero support, zero budget, and $K = s$ times the mean.

\subsubsection{The loop constraint under the Route-I bounds}
\label{sec:loop_routeI}

The probability comparison~\eqref{eq:loop_prob} asks for $\Pr(D_T > \tau) \le \Pr(D_{H_1} > \tau)$, so that repeating the schedule keeps the failure probability below~$\varepsilon$ without end.
Under Route~I we never observe these probabilities.
We have only an upper bound $U_k \ge \Pr(D_k > \tau)$ from one of the bounds of Table~\ref{tab:five_bounds}, for example the count-weighted Chernoff bound~\eqref{eq:chernoff_permission} or the Bernstein constraint~\eqref{eq:bernstein_constraint}.

We write $U^{(n)}$ for the bound on day $H_1 + nH_2$, the end of the $n$-th repetition of the operating phase, so $U^{(0)} = U_{H_1}$ and $U^{(1)} = U_T$.
A natural translation replaces both sides by their bounds and imposes $U^{(1)} \le U^{(0)}$.
This condition is \emph{not} sufficient for repeatability, and the reason needs a precise look.

\paragraph{Why $U^{(1)} \le U^{(0)}$ does not chain.}
The bound $U$ is a many-to-one function of the parameters of the state, and its level sets are not invariant under the dynamics.
So the relation
\begin{equation}\label{eq:loopI_U_rise}
  U^{(2)} \;>\; U^{(1)} \;=\; U^{(0)}
\end{equation}
is possible: the certified bound can rise slowly over the repetitions, and the inequality over one repetition does not carry over by induction.
The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:loopI_no_chain}, page~\pageref{drv:loopI_no_chain}.

A concrete instance\footnote{The script \texttt{loop\_routeI\_instance.py} in the folder \texttt{merge/analysis/scripts/scoping\_checks} of the repository reproduces these numbers.} has $\tau = 10$, $b_\ell = 3$, $\rho = 0.5$, and an operating phase of one $\text{ARD}_\infty$ repair day followed by mission days with
\begin{equation}
  \sum_j n_{ij}\mu_{\ell j} = 3,
  \qquad
  \sum_j n_{ij} v_{\ell j} = 3.99,
  \qquad
  \bigl(\mathbb{E}[D_{H_1}],\operatorname{Var}[D_{H_1}]\bigr) = (4,9).
\end{equation}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Cantelli.} The bound gives $U^{(0)} = 0.2000$ and $U^{(1)} = 0.1997 \le U^{(0)}$, but $U^{(2)} = 0.2151 > U^{(0)}$, and $U^{(n)} \to 0.2495$.
  \item \textbf{Bernstein.} A similar instance (variance $3.15$ added over the operating phase) also fails at the second repetition: $U^{(0)} = 0.3012$, $U^{(1)} = 0.3006$ and $U^{(2)} = 0.3247$.
  \item \textbf{Loop constraint.} The loop constraint~\eqref{eq:loop} rejects both, since $\mathbb{E}[D_T] = 5 > 4$.
\end{itemize}

A fixed margin $\delta > 0$, $U^{(1)} \le U^{(0)} - \delta$, does not fix this.
Every such margin rejects a schedule whose bound repeats exactly, $U^{(1)} = U^{(0)}$.
A small margin still admits the rise: for Cantelli with $U^{(0)} = 0.2$, instances that satisfy it and still fail exist for every $\delta < 0.088$.
The defect is that the level sets are not invariant, not a constant gap.

\paragraph{The certificate stays valid in every repetition.}
This worsening is not hidden: it can be detected.
Each statistic that Route~I tracks follows a monotone recursion under the dynamics~\eqref{eq:dyn_full}: the mean, the variance, the Hoeffding budget and the cumulant generating function at a fixed point~$s$.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Increment.} Increments add to the statistic.
  \item \textbf{Imperfect repair.} An imperfect repair maps it from~$\theta$ to $a\,\theta + (1-a)\,g$, with its anchor~$g$ and a factor $a \in [0,1]$ (Section~\ref{sec:repair_connection}).
  \item \textbf{Replacement.} A replacement resets it.
\end{itemize}
The recursions are exact for the mean, the variance and the Hoeffding budget, and an upper bound for the cumulant generating function (see below).

Consequently $U_k$ is a valid bound on \emph{every} day of the endless schedule, $\Pr(D_k > \tau) \le U_k$.
The true probability can exceed~$\varepsilon$ only if $U_k$ itself does.
Repeatability therefore needs a loop condition that forces $U_k \le \varepsilon$ on every such day.
So the condition must act on a statistic that both determines $U$ and propagates monotonically, not on the scalar~$U$, which loses information.

\paragraph{The loop on the moments (the natural condition).}
For the Cantelli and Bernstein bounds, the loop constraint~\eqref{eq:loop} acts on the tracked moments,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:loop_moments}
  \mathbb{E}[D_T] \;\le\; \mathbb{E}[D_{H_1}]
  \qquad\text{and}\qquad
  \operatorname{Var}[D_T] \;\le\; \operatorname{Var}[D_{H_1}],
\end{equation}
and, under $\text{ARD}_1$, on their anchors $g^{\mu}$ and $g^{v}$ in the same way.
The moments evolve as monotone recursions, and componentwise domination chains.
So the certified risk does not increase across repetitions,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:loopI_risk}
  U^{(n)} \;\le\; U^{(0)} \;\le\; \varepsilon,
\end{equation}
and the schedule is repeatable.
The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:loopI_moments}, page~\pageref{drv:loopI_moments}.

Both inequalities in~\eqref{eq:loop_moments} are linear in the tracked moments $(\mathbb{E}[D_k],\operatorname{Var}[D_k])$.
The model carries these moments as auxiliary variables, and big-M rows or indicator constraints encode their recursion linearly.
So the loop adds one linear constraint per tracked moment and per anchor.
These constraints are strictly stronger than the single relation $U^{(1)} \le U^{(0)}$, and this stronger form is exactly what makes the induction valid.

\paragraph{The loop on the cumulant generating function under imperfect repair.}
If the Chernoff bound with a fixed~$s$ certifies the reliability instead, the loop acts on the tracked cumulant generating function,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:loop_cumulant}
  K_T \;\le\; K_{H_1}.
\end{equation}
Without imperfect repair, \eqref{eq:loop_cumulant} carries over by induction.
An imperfect (ARD) repair does not keep this scalar exact.
The tracked statistic then follows
\begin{equation}\label{eq:loopI_cgf_maps}
  K \mapsto (1-\rho)\,K \quad \text{under $\text{ARD}_\infty$},
  \qquad
  K \mapsto (1-\rho)\,K + \rho\,g^K \quad \text{under $\text{ARD}_1$},
\end{equation}
with the anchor~$g^K$.
These maps are non-decreasing in $(K, g^K)$, and the tracked $K$ stays an upper bound on the true cumulant generating function, which is all that the Chernoff certificate needs.
The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:loopI_cumulant}, page~\pageref{drv:loopI_cumulant}.

Theorem~\ref{thm:perpetual} then applies: the loop row on $K$, and on its anchor under $\text{ARD}_1$, chains under both repair laws.
The cost is slack: after a repair, the tracked $K$ exceeds the true value.
The moments need no such bound, because a repair acts on each of them through a fixed power of $1-\rho$, which keeps their recursion exact.

\paragraph{Summary.}
Under Route~I the loop constraint must act on statistics that determine the bound and that propagate by themselves through the dynamics, never on the scalar bound~$U$.
The formulation imposes the loop constraint~\eqref{eq:loop} on the statistics of the chosen bound; Table~\ref{tab:loop_routeI_bounds} lists them for each bound.
Under $\text{ARD}_1$ the anchors of these statistics enter the loop too.

The loop is valid for every bound and for both repair laws.
In every case the guarantee is that the \emph{certified} risk does not worsen.
Hence $\Pr(D_k > \tau) \le \varepsilon$ on every day of the endless schedule.
This is the practical meaning of repeatability for a model based on bounds.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Statistics on which the Route-I loop constraint acts, for each bound, and the recursion that tracks them under the dynamics.}
\label{tab:loop_routeI_bounds}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{3.0cm}p{5.8cm}p{6.2cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Bound} & \textbf{Statistics in the loop constraint} & \textbf{Recursion of the statistics} \\
\midrule
Markov              & the mean & exact \\
Cantelli, Bernstein & the mean and the variance~\eqref{eq:loop_moments} & exact \\
Hoeffding           & the mean and the Hoeffding budget & exact \\
Chernoff            & the mean and the cumulant generating function~\eqref{eq:loop_cumulant} & exact for the mean; an upper bound for the cumulant generating function \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{Two refinements of the Route-I constraints: schedule-dependent support and piecewise-linear tightening}
\label{sec:routeI_refinements}

Two identifiable sources of slack remain in the Route-I constraints.
Standard mixed-integer constructions can remove both, at a cost in binaries and rows that can be quantified.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Schedule-dependent support.} The first one, a schedule-dependent support, is not part of the formulation, which uses the constant support of Section~\ref{sec:cons_surrogates}.
  \item \textbf{Piecewise-linear tightening.} The formulation includes the second one, the piecewise-linear tightening, as the form \texttt{pwl} of its reliability rows (Section~\ref{sec:linear_forms}).
\end{itemize}
Neither refinement changes the loop argument of Section~\ref{sec:loop_routeI}.
The moment condition~\eqref{eq:loop_moments} acts on the exactly tracked moments, and it does not depend on how tightly the certificate is enforced on them.\footnote{Every monotonicity and equivalence claim in this subsection, both worked examples and the comparison between the union and the intersection of the tangent regions are checked, symbolically or numerically, by the scripts \texttt{refine\_schedule\_support.py} and \texttt{refine\_pwl\_tightening.py}. The scripts and their outputs are in the folder \texttt{merge/analysis/scripts/scoping\_checks} of the repository.}

\paragraph{Schedule-dependent Bernstein support.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Slack.} The closed form~\eqref{eq:bernstein_constraint} carries the single constant
    \begin{equation}
      b_\ell \;=\; \max\{b^{\mathrm{start}}_\ell,\, b^{\mathrm{new}}_\ell,\, \max_j b_{\ell j}\}.
    \end{equation}
    This constant includes the supports $b^{\mathrm{start}}_\ell$, $b^{\mathrm{new}}_\ell$ of the initial and replacement draws.
    So a component pays the support of the most damaging mission even if the schedule never assigns that mission to it.
    The effect is not small, because the correction $\kappa b_\ell/3$ enters twice at zero variance: even the empty schedule satisfies~\eqref{eq:bernstein_constraint} only if $2\kappa b_\ell/3 \le \tau$.
  \item \textbf{Example.} Take $\varepsilon = 0.01$ ($\kappa = \ln 100 \approx 4.605$), $\tau = 1$, two missions with supports $b_{\ell 1} = 0.02$ and $b_{\ell 2} = 0.30$, initial and replacement supports $0.02$, and moments per day of mission~1 $\mu_{\ell 1} = 0.01$, $v_{\ell 1} = 4\cdot 10^{-6}$.
    Consider a component that never serves mission~2.
    \begin{itemize}[nosep]
      \item The constraint with the constant~$b_\ell$ ($\kappa b_\ell/3 = 0.4605$) leaves a mean budget of $0.079$ and admits at most $n_{i1} = 7$ days of mission~1.
      \item The same constraint evaluated with the supports actually present ($\kappa b_\ell/3 = 0.0307$) leaves $0.939$ and admits $n_{i1} = 90$.
    \end{itemize}
    The gap is a factor of $12.9$, and it comes entirely from the unused $b_{\ell 2}$.
    If $b_{\ell 2}$ rises to $0.35$, then
    \begin{equation}
      2\kappa b_\ell/3 \;=\; 1.075 \;>\; \tau,
    \end{equation}
    and the model with the constant~$b_\ell$ becomes infeasible for a component that never serves mission~2.
  \item \textbf{Tightened constant.} The tightened constant is
    \begin{equation}
      b_\ell(\zeta) \;=\; \max\bigl\{b^{\mathrm{start}}_\ell,\, b^{\mathrm{new}}_\ell,\, \max_{j:\,\zeta_{ij}=1} b_{\ell j}\bigr\}.
    \end{equation}
    Here $\zeta_{ij} \in \{0,1\}$ marks the missions that member~$i$ actually serves ($\zeta_{ij} \ge x_{ijt}$ for all~$t$; only this direction of the forcing is needed).
    The constant $b_\ell(\zeta)$ bounds every summand of the state, and Bernstein's inequality applies without change, on every day and in every repetition of the schedule.
  \item \textbf{Rows.} A monotonicity observation makes $b_\ell(\zeta)$ implementable without a nonlinear tracking of the maximum: the Bernstein row written for a larger constant implies every row written for a smaller one.
    Consequently, impose the row with the per-mission constant $c_j$ \emph{conditionally on} $\zeta_{ij} = 1$, together with one unconditional row at $c_0$, where
    \begin{equation}
      c_j \;=\; \kappa\max\{b_{\ell j}, b^{\mathrm{start}}_\ell, b^{\mathrm{new}}_\ell\}/3,
      \qquad
      c_0 \;=\; \kappa\max\{b^{\mathrm{start}}_\ell, b^{\mathrm{new}}_\ell\}/3.
    \end{equation}
    These rows are exactly equivalent to the single row with the schedule-dependent maximum.
    The derivation of this equivalence and of the validity of $b_\ell(\zeta)$ is in Appendix~\ref{drv:sched_support}, page~\pageref{drv:sched_support}.
  \item \textbf{Cost.} The cost is one binary per pair of member and mission ($F \cdot M$ in total), their linking rows and the conditional certificate rows of each mission.
    All of them are linear in the new variables.
    The rows of the tangent surrogate attach to $\zeta_{ij}$ by indicator constraints or by big-M rows. Written as $2\kappa v \le \dots$, these rows have the uniform valid constant $2\tau(\tau - c_0)$ for every mission with $2c_j < \tau$; a mission with $2c_j \ge \tau$ gets $\zeta_{ij} = 0$ and no row.
    The exact quadratic rows must use big-M, because Gurobi's indicator constraints enforce a linear constraint~\cite{GurobiConstraints}.
  \item \textbf{Side effects.} Two side effects are worth recording.
    \begin{enumerate}[nosep]
      \item Big-M constants derived from the largest admissible variance must be recomputed from $c_0$.
        The feasible region has grown, and a bound taken from the old constant $b_\ell$ would cut it.
      \item The feasibility precondition $2\kappa b_\ell/3 < \tau$ becomes local: only $c_0$ must satisfy it.
        A mission with $2c_j > \tau$ simply forces $\zeta_{ij} = 0$; at $2c_j = \tau$ its row admits only $\mu = v = 0$.
        So that mission is never assigned to that component, and the whole model does not become inconsistent.
    \end{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Effect left out.} By choice, the refinement does \emph{not} capture one further effect.
    The repair weights shrink the support of each summand further, to $w_t\,b_{\ell j}$.
    Exploiting this needs a running maximum over products of repair factors and mission indicators that depend on the schedule, a truly nonlinear refinement.
    The variable~$\zeta$, defined over the whole window, captures the first-order gap: missions that are never served.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Piecewise-linear tightening of the tangent surrogates.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Slack of one tangent.} The linear surrogate with one fixed tangent (the device described after~\eqref{eq:bernstein_constraint}) has a conservatism that grows quadratically with the mean.
    Cantelli's bound~\eqref{eq:cantelli_surrogate} is at most $\varepsilon$ if and only if
    \begin{equation}
      \mathbb{E}[D] + \kappa_C\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}[D]} \;\le\; \tau,
      \qquad\text{with}\qquad
      \kappa_C = \sqrt{(1-\varepsilon)/\varepsilon}.
    \end{equation}
    Write $(\mu, v)$ for the tracked moments.
    The exact feasible set is
    \begin{equation}
      \kappa_C^2\, v \;\le\; f(\mu) := (\tau - \mu)^2,
    \end{equation}
    and the tangent at $\mu_r = 0$ gives the linear row $\kappa_C^2\, v \le \tau^2 - 2\tau\mu$.
    Since $f(\mu) - (\tau^2 - 2\tau\mu) = \mu^2$, the surrogate loses exactly $\mu^2/\kappa_C^2$ of admissible variance.
    It reaches zero at $\mu = \tau/2$, and it excludes \emph{every} point with $\mu > \tau/2$, a deterministic component included.
    The exact set still admits
    \begin{equation}
      v \;=\; (\tau - \mu)^2/\kappa_C^2 \;>\; 0
    \end{equation}
    up to $\mu = \tau$.
  \item \textbf{Several tangents: union, not intersection.} The refinement takes tangents at several points $\mu_r$.
    Each row
    \begin{equation}
      \kappa_C^2\, v \;\le\; T_r(\mu) := (\tau - \mu_r)(\tau + \mu_r - 2\mu)
    \end{equation}
    is conservative by itself, because tangents lie below the convex parabola.
    The way the rows are combined is the critical point.
    Imposing all rows \emph{together} gives their intersection
    \begin{equation}
      \kappa_C^2 v \;\le\; \min_r T_r(\mu),
    \end{equation}
    which is \emph{more} conservative than any single tangent.
    The tightening is the \emph{union} of the single-tangent regions, a disjunction:
    \begin{equation}
      \kappa_C^2 v \;\le\; \max_r T_r(\mu).
    \end{equation}
  \item \textbf{Encodings.} Two encodings give this union.
    \begin{enumerate}[nosep,label=(\roman*)]
      \item One selector binary per tangent, with indicator (or big-M) rows:
        \begin{equation}
          \sum_r \lambda_r = 1,
          \qquad
          \lambda_r = 1 \;\Rightarrow\; \kappa_C^2 v \le T_r(\mu).
        \end{equation}
      \item An auxiliary variable $y$, tied by a piecewise-linear \emph{equality} (the SOS2 $\lambda$-formulation) to the pointwise minimum of the tangents to $\sqrt{v}$ at
        \begin{equation}
          v_r \;=\; (\tau - \mu_r)^2/\kappa_C^2,
        \end{equation}
        followed by the single linear row $\mu + \kappa_C\, y \le \tau$.
    \end{enumerate}
    The two encodings describe the same set: the tangent at $\mu_r$ on the side of~$\mu$ and the tangent at $v_r$ on the side of~$v$ bound the same half-plane
    \begin{equation}
      \kappa_C^2 v \;\le\; (\tau - \mu_r)(\tau + \mu_r - 2\mu).
    \end{equation}
  \item \textbf{Shortcuts that fail.} Two natural shortcuts both fail.
    Requiring $y \ge$ every tangent gives their pointwise \emph{maximum}: a region strictly inside the single-tangent region, worse than no refinement.
    Requiring $y \le$ every tangent lets $y$ fall below $\sqrt{v}$, and this destroys the certificate.
    Likewise, the equality must interpolate the vertices of the tangent envelope, not points on the curve.
    Consecutive tangents meet at
    \begin{equation}
      v \;=\; \sqrt{v_r v_{r+1}}
      \qquad\text{with value}\qquad
      \bigl(\sqrt{v_r} + \sqrt{v_{r+1}}\bigr)/2,
    \end{equation}
    above the curve.
    The chords between points on the curve underestimate the concave square root.
  \item \textbf{Recovery.} The recovery is fast, and it can be quantified exactly because $f$ is quadratic.
    Where the union admits $v \ge 0$, the remaining gap of the union is
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pwl_union_gap}
      \min_r\, (\mu - \mu_r)^2.
    \end{equation}
    This gap is quadratic in the distance to the nearest tangent point, with worst case $(\Delta/2)^2$ for a spacing~$\Delta$.
    So doubling the number of tangents divides it by four.
    The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:pwl_recovery}, page~\pageref{drv:pwl_recovery}.
    With $\tau = 1$, $\varepsilon = 0.01$ ($\kappa_C^2 = 99$) and tangents at $\mu_r \in \{0, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75\}$, the admissible variance recovers as follows ($v \times 10^{3}$):
\begin{center}
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}
\toprule
$\mu$ & $0$ & $0.2$ & $0.4$ & $0.5$ & $0.6$ \\
\midrule
exact $(\tau-\mu)^2/\kappa_C^2$ & $10.10$ & $6.46$ & $3.64$ & $2.53$ & $1.62$ \\
single tangent ($\mu_r = 0$)    & $10.10$ & $6.06$ & $2.02$ & $0$    & infeasible \\
four tangents (union)           & $10.10$ & $6.44$ & $3.54$ & $2.53$ & $1.52$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
    The union recovers $94\%$ of the area of the feasible region that the single tangent loses.
  \item \textbf{Bernstein and the schedule-dependent support.} The construction applies without change to Bernstein.
    The parabola $(\tau - \kappa b_\ell/3 - \mu)^2$ follows the same law for the gap, and the tangents to the concave $\sqrt{(\kappa b_\ell/3)^2 + 2\kappa v}$ lie above it everywhere.
    The construction also combines soundly with the schedule-dependent support above: each conditional row carries its own mission constant and stays conservative for it.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Linear forms of the reliability rows}
\label{sec:linear_forms}

The formulation offers three forms for each quadratic reliability row.
These rows are the Gaussian and Wiener row $\kappa_G^2\, v \le (\tau - \mu)^2$ with $\kappa_G = \Phi^{-1}(1-\varepsilon)$ (Section~\ref{sec:loop_per_model}), and the Cantelli, Hoeffding and Bernstein rows of Table~\ref{tab:five_bounds}.
Each has the form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:quad_row}
  \chi \;\le\; f(\mu), \qquad f(\mu) = c_2\,(\tau - \mu)^2 + c_1\,(\tau - \mu), \qquad \mu \le \bar\mu_{\mathrm{side}},
\end{equation}
where $\chi$ is the variance or the Hoeffding budget, with the constants of Table~\ref{tab:quad_constants}.
The third relation is the side row; it selects the branch of the parabola on which the row is a valid certificate.
Since $c_2 > 0$, $f$ is convex in~$\mu$, with $f'(\mu) = -2c_2(\tau - \mu) - c_1$.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Constants of the quadratic reliability rows~\eqref{eq:quad_row}.}
\label{tab:quad_constants}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lllll@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Row} & $\chi$ & $c_2$ & $c_1$ & $\bar\mu_{\mathrm{side}}$ \\
\midrule
Gaussian, Wiener   & $v$ & $1/\kappa_G^2$ & $0$ & $\tau$ \\
Rainflow, Cantelli & $v$ & $1/\kappa_C^2 = \varepsilon/(1-\varepsilon)$ & $0$ & $\tau$ \\
Rainflow, Hoeffding & $R$ & $2/\kappa$ & $0$ & $\tau$ \\
Rainflow, Bernstein & $v$ & $1/(2\kappa)$ & $-b_\ell/3$ & $\tau - c$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Every form keeps the side row.
\begin{itemize}
  \item \texttt{exact}: the row~\eqref{eq:quad_row} itself.
    The feasible set $\{(\mu, \chi) : \chi \le f(\mu)\}$ lies below a convex function, so it is not convex: the row is a nonconvex quadratic constraint.
    Example (Gaussian row): $(\mu, v) = (\tau, 0)$ and $(0, \tau^2/\kappa_G^2)$ satisfy it, and their midpoint $(\tau/2, \tau^2/(2\kappa_G^2))$ does not, since $\tau^2/2 > \tau^2/4$.
    The solver Gurobi handles such rows through its nonconvex quadratic support.
  \item \texttt{tangent}: one linear row per day, the tangent of~$f$ at a point $\mu_r \in [0, \tau)$ chosen per component; the default is $\mu_r = \tau/2$.
    At $\mu_r = 0$ the Gaussian row becomes $\kappa_G^2\, v + 2\tau\mu \le \tau^2$.
    The point $\mu_r = \tau$ is excluded: for $c_1 = 0$ the tangent at~$\tau$ is $\chi \le 0$.
  \item \texttt{pwl}: the interval $[0, \tau]$ is split into $P$ equal segments, by default $P = 8$.
    One binary per segment and day selects the segment that contains the mean, and the tangent at the midpoint of that segment caps~$\chi$.
    This is encoding~(i) of the piecewise-linear tightening (Section~\ref{sec:routeI_refinements}), with each selector tied to its segment.
    With $P = 1$ the form is the single tangent at $\tau/2$, with no binaries.
\end{itemize}

\begin{proposition}[Safety of the linear forms]\label{prop:tangent_safe}
For every $\mu_r$ and every $\mu$,
\begin{equation}
  f(\mu_r) + f'(\mu_r)\,(\mu - \mu_r) \;=\; f(\mu) - c_2\,(\mu - \mu_r)^2 \;\le\; f(\mu).
\end{equation}
Therefore every point that satisfies the \texttt{tangent} or the \texttt{pwl} form satisfies the \texttt{exact} form, and both linear forms lie inside the certificate set $\mathcal{C}_{i\ell}$ of Section~\ref{sec:encoding}.
\end{proposition}
The proof is in Appendix~\ref{prf:prop:tangent_safe}, page~\pageref{prf:prop:tangent_safe}.

\paragraph{Accuracy.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{The \texttt{tangent} form.} The \texttt{tangent} form loses $c_2(\mu - \mu_r)^2$ in~$\chi$: it is exact at $\mu = \mu_r$ and loosest far from it.
    A tangent point near the operating mean of the component is the best choice.
  \item \textbf{The \texttt{pwl} form.} In the \texttt{pwl} form the loss is
    \begin{equation}
      c_2(\mu - \hat\mu_p)^2 \;\le\; c_2\,\tau^2/(4P^2),
    \end{equation}
    with $\hat\mu_p$ the midpoint of the segment that contains~$\mu$.
    That midpoint is the nearest tangent point to~$\mu$.
    So the \texttt{pwl} form accepts exactly the points with
    \begin{equation}
      \chi \;\le\; \max_p\, [f(\hat\mu_p) + f'(\hat\mu_p)(\mu - \hat\mu_p)],
    \end{equation}
    the union of the $P$ single-tangent regions and the largest set that the $P$ tangents allow.
    The cost is $P$ binaries per component and day.
\end{itemize}

\begin{example}[Cantelli, $\varepsilon = 0.1$, $\kappa_C^2 = 9$, $\tau = 1$, $v = 0.004$]
The largest admissible mean is $1 - \sqrt{9 \cdot 0.004} \approx 0.810$ with \texttt{exact}; $0.482$ with \texttt{tangent} at~$0$ ($9v + 2\mu \le 1$); $0.714$ with \texttt{tangent} at $0.5\,\tau$ ($9v \le 0.75 - \mu$); and $0.810$ with \texttt{pwl} and $P = 8$.
The tangent at~$0$ never admits a mean above $\tau/2$, even with zero variance.
\end{example}

\paragraph{The tangent point of a Bernstein row.}
For Bernstein with $b_\ell > 0$, the tangent point must satisfy $\mu_r < \sqrt{\tau(\tau - 2c)}$, with $c = \kappa b_\ell/3$, and the \texttt{pwl} form needs $\tau/(2P) < \sqrt{\tau(\tau - 2c)}$ for its first segment.
The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:bernstein_tangent_point}, page~\pageref{drv:bernstein_tangent_point}.

\subsubsection{Random fatigue life in the Palmgren--Miner rule}
\label{sec:miner_randomN}

The rainflow pipeline of Section~\ref{sec:cons_surrogates} measures the law of the per-mission increment $\Delta D^{(j)}$ directly and nonparametrically.
A complementary pipeline keeps the rainflow \emph{counts} deterministic and makes the fatigue life $N_f$ in Miner's rule~\eqref{eq:miner} the random object.
It has two steps.
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
  \item Fit a scatter family of Table~\ref{tab:sn_scatter} to constant-amplitude test data or to records of failures in service.
    Basquin's law~\eqref{eq:basquin} ties the location to the stress amplitude.
    For the log-normal,
    \begin{equation}
      \ln N_f \;\sim\; \mathcal{N}(\mu(\sigma_a),\sigma^2),
      \qquad\text{with}\qquad
      \mu(\sigma_a) \;=\; \ln C - m \ln \sigma_a
    \end{equation}
    and a $\sigma$ that does not depend on the stress.
    For the Weibull, the scale is $\lambda(\sigma_a) = C\sigma_a^{-m}$, with a common shape $k$.
    Run-outs (tests stopped before failure) enter the likelihood as censored observations.
  \item \emph{Derive} the increment from the rainflow histogram $\{n^{(j)}_c\}$ of the mission as
    \begin{equation}
      \Delta D^{(j)} \;=\; \sum_c n^{(j)}_c / N_f(\sigma_c).
    \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
Both pipelines follow the same idea: collect data, then approximate a distribution.
They differ in the random object and in the assumptions they commit to.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Empirical route.} It is distribution-free, and it captures the variability of the load and of the material together.
  \item \textbf{Random-$N_f$ route.} It is parametric.
    It pools information across stress levels, extrapolates to unobserved amplitudes through~\eqref{eq:basquin}, and separates the load spectrum (the counts) from the material scatter (the law of $N_f$).
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Inverse moments of the scatter families.}
Every quantity that the framework uses later is a moment of $1/N_f$.
For the three families of Table~\ref{tab:sn_scatter} and $r > 0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:miner_invmom}
  \mathbb{E}\big[N_f^{-r}\big] \;=\;
  \begin{cases}
    \exp\big({-r\mu + r^2\sigma^2/2}\big), & \ln N_f \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^2) \quad \text{(finite for all $r$)},\\[3pt]
    \lambda^{-r}\,\Gamma(1 - r/k), & N_f \sim \mathrm{Wei}(k,\lambda) \quad \text{(finite if and only if $r < k$)},\\[3pt]
    e^{-r\mu}\,\Gamma(1 - r\beta), & \ln N_f \sim \mathrm{Gumbel}_{\min}(\mu,\beta) \quad \text{(finite if and only if $r\beta < 1$)}.
  \end{cases}
\end{equation}
The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:miner_invmom}, page~\pageref{drv:miner_invmom}.
In particular, for the three families,
\begin{equation}
  \operatorname{Var}[1/N_f] \;=\;
  \begin{cases}
    e^{-2\mu+\sigma^2}(e^{\sigma^2}-1), & \text{log-normal},\\[3pt]
    \lambda^{-2}\big[\Gamma(1-2/k)-\Gamma(1-1/k)^2\big], & \text{Weibull},\\[3pt]
    e^{-2\mu}\big[\Gamma(1-2\beta)-\Gamma(1-\beta)^2\big], & \text{Gumbel-minimum}.
  \end{cases}
\end{equation}
These values match the increment moments already given for the log-normal below Table~\ref{tab:sn_scatter}.

\begin{remark}[Two tail behaviours, one real restriction]\label{rem:miner_tails}
The Gumbel-minimum log-life is \emph{exactly} a Weibull life:
\begin{equation}
  \Pr(N_f > x) \;=\; \exp\big(-e^{(\ln x - \mu)/\beta}\big) \;=\; \exp\big(-(x/e^{\mu})^{1/\beta}\big),
\end{equation}
that is, $\mathrm{Wei}(k = 1/\beta,\ \lambda = e^{\mu})$.
The existence conditions in~\eqref{eq:miner_invmom} correspond to each other.
So the three families of Table~\ref{tab:sn_scatter} give two tail behaviours for $1/N_f$: log-normal (all polynomial moments finite) and Fr\'echet tails with index $k$ (moments finite only below order $k$).

The second behaviour is a real restriction, not a formality.
The mean of the increment requires $k > 1$, and the variance, the input of the Cantelli certificate~\eqref{eq:cantelli_surrogate}, requires $k > 2$.
So the fitted shape must be checked before the method is used.
For $k \in (1,2]$ only the Markov bound remains, and for $k \le 1$ the untruncated model has an infinite mean increment and cannot enter the framework at all.

Moreover, for \emph{all three} untruncated families, $1/N_f$ has unbounded support and an infinite moment generating function.
So the exponential certificates (Chernoff~\eqref{eq:chernoff_permission}, Bernstein~\eqref{eq:bernstein_constraint}, Hoeffding) are not admissible.
Only a lower bound $N_{\min} > 0$ on the life, defended on physical grounds, restores them (a truncated family).
This bound gives the per-mission support $b_{\ell j} = n_j / N_{\min}$, and the moments must then be recomputed under the truncated law (closed forms with Gaussian tails and incomplete gamma functions).
\end{remark}

\paragraph{What ``random $N_f$'' means: two interpretations.}
The marginal law of $N_f$ does not determine the model.
The joint law of the scatter \emph{across days} must also be chosen, and the two extreme choices lead to different mathematics.
Table~\ref{tab:miner_interpretations} compares the two interpretations.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{The two interpretations of a random fatigue life $N_f$ in the Palmgren--Miner rule.}
\label{tab:miner_interpretations}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{3.0cm}p{6.0cm}p{6.0cm}@{}}
\toprule
 & \textbf{(I) Block-independent scatter} & \textbf{(II) Unit-specific scatter} \\
\midrule
Scatter across days & each execution of mission~$j$ draws a new, independent life & the component carries \emph{one} realisation of the scatter for its entire life \\
Stochastic state & the moments of the Route-I pipeline, with the per-mission inputs~\eqref{eq:miner_routeI} & a deterministic scalar $M_{i\ell k}$ with linear dynamics \\
Chance constraint & Cantelli certificate~\eqref{eq:cantelli_surrogate} & the exact linear inequality~\eqref{eq:miner_quantile} \\
Loop constraint & moment loop~\eqref{eq:loop_moments} & the linear condition~\eqref{eq:miner_loop_det} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\emph{(I) Block-independent scatter.}
Interpretation~(I) suits the case in which the variability from cycle to cycle and from day to day dominates, and the component does not carry one fixed $N_f$.
Assume a single dominant stress level per mission.
(For a spectrum in bins, the means add over the bins; the variance depends on the assumed dependence within the mission, and it is computed from~\eqref{eq:miner_invmom} in the same way.)
Then the per-mission inputs are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:miner_routeI}
  \mu_{\ell j} \;=\; n_j\,\mathbb{E}\big[N_f^{-1}\big],
  \qquad
  v_{\ell j} \;=\; n_j^2\,\operatorname{Var}\big[N_f^{-1}\big].
\end{equation}
Independence across days is exactly the hypothesis of Equation~\eqref{eq:additive_functionals}.
So the Cantelli certificate~\eqref{eq:cantelli_surrogate} and the moment loop~\eqref{eq:loop_moments} apply \emph{without change}, with parametric instead of empirical moments per mission.
The rainflow route of Section~\ref{sec:cons_surrogates} already uses this interpretation.
The random-$N_f$ model is then only another input pipeline for the same formulation, subject to the admissibility conditions of Remark~\ref{rem:miner_tails}.

\emph{(II) Unit-specific scatter.}
The fitted families have a scale or a shape that does not depend on the stress, so the realisation of the scatter is a single multiplicative factor,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:miner_unit}
  N_{f,j} \;=\; A\,\bar N_j, \qquad A > 0 \ \text{drawn once at installation},
\end{equation}
with $\bar N_j$ the median life (log-normal: $e^{\mu_j}$) or the characteristic life (Weibull: $\lambda_j$; Gumbel: $e^{\mu_j}$), and $A \sim F_A$ the unit factor ($\ln A \sim \mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^2)$, $A \sim \mathrm{Wei}(k,1)$ or $A = e^{\beta G}$, respectively).
This is exactly the ``random critical damage sum'' of source~(ii) above.
Dividing the Miner sum by $A$ is the same as comparing a deterministic sum with a random capacity.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Damage.} The damage becomes $D_k = M_k / A$, where $M_k$ is the \emph{deterministic} Miner state.
    Its dynamics mirror~\eqref{eq:dyn_full}: a mission day adds
    \begin{equation}
      \sum_j x_{ijk}\, n_j/\bar N_j,
    \end{equation}
    an ARD repair maps $M \mapsto (1-\rho)M$, and a replacement resets $M$ to zero \emph{and draws a new}~$A$ (a new physical component carries a new factor with the same law).
  \item \textbf{Reliability constraint.} For a continuous $F_A$ the reliability constraint is then \emph{exact}, deterministic and linear in the assignment counts:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:miner_quantile}
  \Pr(D_k \ge 1) \;=\; \Pr(A \le M_k) \;=\; F_A(M_k) \;\le\; \varepsilon
  \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  M_k \;\le\; q_\varepsilon \;:=\; F_A^{-1}(\varepsilon),
\end{equation}
    with the closed-form quantiles
    \begin{equation}
      q_\varepsilon \;=\;
      \begin{cases}
        e^{\sigma\Phi^{-1}(\varepsilon)} & \text{(log-normal)},\\[3pt]
        (-\ln(1-\varepsilon))^{1/k} & \text{(Weibull)},\\[3pt]
        (-\ln(1-\varepsilon))^{\beta} & \text{(Gumbel-minimum)}.
      \end{cases}
    \end{equation}
    In fact no family is needed beyond one scalar: an empirical $\varepsilon$-quantile of $A$ with a confidence margin serves equally well.
    Without repair since the last replacement, and with $n_{ij}$ counted from it, rewriting $M_k \le q_\varepsilon$ as
    \begin{equation}
      \sum_j n_{ij}\,n_j/(q_\varepsilon \bar N_j) \;\le\; 1
    \end{equation}
    gives back a classical design practice: deterministic Miner on the $\varepsilon$-percentile lives
    \begin{equation}
      N^{(\varepsilon)}_j \;=\; q_\varepsilon \bar N_j,
    \end{equation}
    which are exactly the $\varepsilon$-quantiles of $N_{f,j}$.
    Here this practice is an \emph{exact} chance constraint rather than a heuristic, in the per-day sense of~\eqref{eq:abs_rel}.
  \item \textbf{Loop constraint.} The loop constraint simplifies in the same way.
    $F_A$ is strictly increasing on $(0,\infty)$, so the probability comparison~\eqref{eq:loop_prob} is equivalent to the single linear condition
\begin{equation}\label{eq:miner_loop_det}
  M_T \;\le\; M_{H_1}.
\end{equation}
    Unlike the scalar bound of Section~\ref{sec:loop_routeI}, which loses information, this condition \emph{chains}.
    So imposing~\eqref{eq:miner_quantile} on all $k = 1,\dots,T$ extends the per-day reliability to every repetition, replacements (which draw a new~$A$) included.
    The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:miner_loop}, page~\pageref{drv:miner_loop}.
\end{itemize}

\begin{remark}[The two interpretations are not two levels of conservatism]\label{rem:miner_dichotomy}
Interpretations~(I) and~(II) can share the \emph{same} marginal law per day and differ only in the dependence across days: zero or perfect correlation of the scatter.
Yet they give different answers.
The means coincide, while the variances satisfy
\begin{equation}
  \operatorname{Var}_{\mathrm{II}}/\operatorname{Var}_{\mathrm{I}} \;=\; \Big(\sum_t m_t\Big)^2 \Big/ \sum_t m_t^2.
\end{equation}
By Cauchy--Schwarz this ratio is at most the number of days, with equality for equal daily increments $m_t$.
So under~(II) the dispersion never averages out.

In the small-$\varepsilon$ regime, (II) has by far the heavier tail.
For example, take a log-normal factor with $\sigma = 0.5$ and fifty days with equal increments, with Miner sum $M = 0.6$, the median of the damage under~(II).
Under~(I), each day draws its own factor with the same law.
The failure probability $\Pr(D \ge 1)$ is then $0.153$ under~(II) and $2.5 \cdot 10^{-7}$ under~(I).
The order reverses only at high damage.
In the same example, the two probabilities are equal ($0.39$) at $M = 0.87$.
For a larger $M$, (I) gives the larger probability.
So neither interpretation is conservative in all cases.\footnote{The scripts and their outputs are in the folder \texttt{merge/analysis/scripts/scoping\_checks/miner} of the repository. They check the closed forms~\eqref{eq:miner_invmom}, the divergent sample variance at Weibull $k \le 2$, the exactness of~\eqref{eq:miner_quantile} along a schedule with repair and replacement, the chaining of~\eqref{eq:miner_loop_det}, the variance ratio and the example. The value under~(I) comes from a discretised convolution, which bounds it between $2.45 \cdot 10^{-7}$ and $2.46 \cdot 10^{-7}$.}

The choice is a question of identification.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Unit-specific~(II).} Scatter that comes from the variability between specimens (material batch, manufacturing) is unit-specific~(II).
  \item \textbf{Block-independent~(I).} Scatter that comes from the randomness of the load history within a mission is block-independent~(I).
\end{itemize}
Rainflow-counted data recorded in service on a fleet mix both.
A decomposition of the variance of the recorded increments into a part between units and a part within units estimates the split.
When both sources matter, one writes $N_f = A\,\widetilde N_f$, with a unit factor and block-independent residuals.

Conservative combinations exist.
One example is $\varepsilon$-splitting:
\begin{equation}
  \Pr(\text{fail}) \;\le\; \delta + \Pr(\text{fail} \mid A = a_\delta),
\end{equation}
with $a_\delta$ the $\delta$-quantile of $A$.
After this step the increments are again independent across days, with moments scaled by $a_\delta^{-1}$ and $a_\delta^{-2}$, and the Route-I method runs on the remaining budget $\varepsilon - \delta$.
The calibration of such combinations is left as future work.
\end{remark}

\paragraph{Implications for the abstract formulation.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Interpretation~(I).} Interpretation~(I) requires no change to Problem~\eqref{eq:abstract}.
    It supplies the per-mission moments~\eqref{eq:miner_routeI} to the same Route-I pipeline that uses the moments measured by rainflow counting, and it keeps the certificates and the loop condition of that pipeline.
  \item \textbf{Interpretation~(II).} Interpretation~(II) defines a strictly simpler model family (Table~\ref{tab:miner_interpretations}).
    There is no moment tracking, no slack from a tail bound and no big-M linearisation of a variance recursion.
    One quantile $q_\varepsilon$ per component holds the entire stochastic description.
    So interpretation~(II) is a natural candidate for a dedicated model type (a deterministic equivalent) beside the models based on processes.
\end{itemize}
The caveat of Remark~\ref{rem:miner_dichotomy} applies: which interpretation matches a given component is an empirical question, not a preference of the formulation.

\subsection{Stochastic orderings}
\label{sec:stoch_orders}

A comparison of the damage of a component at two days of the schedule needs a notion of ``one random variable is larger than another''.
Several stochastic orders formalise this idea, with increasing strength~\cite{ShakedShanthikumar2007,WikiStochDom}.
Let $X$ and $Y$ be two non-negative random variables with CDFs $F_X$, $F_Y$, survival functions $\bar F_X = 1-F_X$, $\bar F_Y = 1-F_Y$, and densities $f_X$, $f_Y$ (when they exist).

\begin{definition}[Usual stochastic order]\label{def:st}
  $X \le_{\mathrm{st}} Y$ if $F_X(t) \ge F_Y(t)$ for all $t \in \mathbb{R}$,
  or equivalently $\Pr(X > t) \le \Pr(Y > t)$ for all~$t$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Hazard rate order]\label{def:hr}
  $X \le_{\mathrm{hr}} Y$ if $\bar F_Y(t)/\bar F_X(t)$ is non-decreasing in $t$ over $\{t : \bar F_X(t) > 0\}$.
  Equivalently, the hazard rates satisfy $r_X(t) \ge r_Y(t)$ for all~$t$, where $r(t) = f(t)/\bar F(t)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Likelihood ratio order]\label{def:lr}
  $X \le_{\mathrm{lr}} Y$ if $f_Y(t)/f_X(t)$ is non-decreasing in $t$ over the union of their supports.
\end{definition}

These three orders are progressively stronger~\cite[Ch.~1]{ShakedShanthikumar2007}.
The implications are strict, and none of the converses holds in general:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:order_implications}
  X \le_{\mathrm{lr}} Y
  \;\Longrightarrow\;
  X \le_{\mathrm{hr}} Y
  \;\Longrightarrow\;
  X \le_{\mathrm{st}} Y
  \;\Longrightarrow\;
  \mathbb{E}[X] \le \mathbb{E}[Y].
\end{equation}

\begin{remark}[Connection to the loop constraint]
The probability comparison~\eqref{eq:loop_prob} requires
\begin{equation}
  \Pr(D_{i\ell T} > \tau) \le \Pr(D_{i\ell H_1} > \tau),
\end{equation}
which is a \emph{pointwise} comparison of the survival functions at the single threshold~$\tau$.
This is strictly weaker than
\begin{equation}
  D_{i\ell T} \le_{\mathrm{st}} D_{i\ell H_1},
\end{equation}
which demands the inequality at every point.
So the usual stochastic order is a convenient sufficient condition for~\eqref{eq:loop_prob}, but not a necessary one.
The loop constraint~\eqref{eq:loop} of the formulation compares tracked statistics instead (Section~\ref{sec:loop_constraint}).
\end{remark}

\subsubsection{Stochastic ordering within each degradation family}

For the fleet management problem, the relevant comparison is between two damages of the same component that belong to the same parametric family.
An example is the damage at the end of the transitory phase and the damage at the end of the horizon.

Tables~\ref{tab:ordering_lr}--\ref{tab:ordering_st} give, for each family of Table~\ref{tab:deg_models}, the necessary and sufficient conditions for each of the three orders.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item An entry marked ``---'' means that the author knows no simple closed-form characterisation.
  \item For the inverse Gaussian rows, the $\le_{\mathrm{lr}}$ conditions remain a valid sufficient criterion by~\eqref{eq:order_implications}.
  \item For the compound Poisson rows, see the discussion below.
\end{itemize}

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Conditions for $X \le_{\mathrm{lr}} Y$ (likelihood ratio order) within each family.  Conditions are necessary and sufficient except where noted.}
\label{tab:ordering_lr}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{2.1cm}p{3.8cm}p{4.0cm}p{4.0cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Family} & $X$ & $Y$ & \textbf{Condition} \\
\midrule
Gamma
  & $\mathrm{Ga}(\alpha_1,\,\beta_1)$
  & $\mathrm{Ga}(\alpha_2,\,\beta_2)$
  & $\alpha_1 \le \alpha_2$ and $\beta_1 \ge \beta_2$ \\[4pt]
Gaussian
  & $\mathcal{N}(\mu_1,\,\sigma_1^2)$
  & $\mathcal{N}(\mu_2,\,\sigma_2^2)$

Inv.\ Gaussian
  & $\mathrm{IG}(\mu_1,\,\lambda_1)$
  & $\mathrm{IG}(\mu_2,\,\lambda_2)$

Comp.\ Poisson
  & $\mathrm{CP}(\nu_1,\,F_{Y_1})$
  & $\mathrm{CP}(\nu_2,\,F_{Y_2})$
  & --- (can fail even for $F_{Y_1}{=}F_{Y_2}$, $\nu_1 \le \nu_2$; holds, for example, for deterministic jumps; see text) \\

\toprule

  & $\mathrm{Ga}(\alpha_1,\,\beta_1)$
