\paragraph{Gamma.}
All three orders coincide for the Gamma family~\cite[Sec.~1.C]{ShakedShanthikumar2007}.
Under the closure condition (same~$\beta$), they all reduce to $\alpha_1 \le \alpha_2$.
The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:order_gamma}, page~\pageref{drv:order_gamma}.

\paragraph{Gaussian.}
All three orders coincide: $\sigma_1 = \sigma_2$ and $\mu_1 \le \mu_2$.
The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:order_gauss}, page~\pageref{drv:order_gauss}.

\paragraph{Inverse Gaussian.}
For $X \sim \mathrm{IG}(\mu_1,\lambda_1)$ and $Y \sim \mathrm{IG}(\mu_2,\lambda_2)$, $X \le_{\mathrm{lr}} Y$ holds if and only if
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_2 \ge \lambda_1
  \quad\text{and}\quad
  \lambda_1/\mu_1^2 \ge \lambda_2/\mu_2^2.
\end{equation}
The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:order_ig}, page~\pageref{drv:order_ig}.
Under the closure condition (same $\mu^2/\lambda$), these simplify to $\mu_1 \le \mu_2$.
For the general two-parameter case, the standard references give no closed-form necessary and sufficient conditions for $\le_{\mathrm{hr}}$ and $\le_{\mathrm{st}}$.
The $\le_{\mathrm{lr}}$ conditions serve as a sufficient criterion.

\paragraph{Compound Poisson.}
Take the same jump-size distribution~$F_Y$ for both laws, with $Y \ge 0$ and $\Pr(Y>0) > 0$.
Then
\begin{equation}
  \mathrm{CP}(\nu_1, F_Y) \le_{\mathrm{st}} \mathrm{CP}(\nu_2, F_Y)
\end{equation}
if and only if $\nu_1 \le \nu_2$.
The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:order_cp}, page~\pageref{drv:order_cp}.

The stronger orders do \emph{not} follow in general.
Take $Y$ uniform on $\{1, 10\}$, $\nu_1 = 0.1$ and $\nu_2 = 0.2$.
The likelihood ratio drops from ${\approx}463$ at $x = 9$ (reachable only by nine unit jumps) to ${\approx}1.8$ at $x = 10$ (reachable by a single large jump), so $\le_{\mathrm{lr}}$ fails.
The ratio of the survival functions is not monotone either, so $\le_{\mathrm{hr}}$ fails as well.
Both orders hold in special cases, such as deterministic jump sizes, where the process is a scaled Poisson process.

When the jump-size distributions differ, the ordering conditions depend on the specific form of~$F_Y$, and no universal closed-form characterisation is available.

\paragraph{Implications for the fleet problem.}
Under the closure conditions of Table~\ref{tab:discrete_models}, the cumulative damage $D_{i\ell k}$ depends on the schedule only through cumulative parameters.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{One-parameter families.} The comparison of two damages is scalar:
    for Gamma (shared rate~$\beta$), the total shape $\sum_t \alpha_t$;
    for IG (shared dispersion $\mu^2/\lambda$), the total mean $\sum_t \mu_t$.
  \item \textbf{Gaussian family.} The state carries \emph{two} cumulative parameters,
    \begin{equation}
      \bigl(\sum_t \mu_t,\ \sum_t \sigma_t^2\bigr).
    \end{equation}
    The variance depends on the schedule: it accumulates with the mission days served, and only an intervention reduces it.
    The usual stochastic order between Gaussian laws requires equal variances (Table~\ref{tab:ordering_st}).
    So the Gaussian comparison uses the single-threshold condition~\eqref{eq:loop_gauss} or the componentwise domination of the mean and the variance, not the mean alone.
\end{itemize}

The formulation does not rely on closure for the Gamma and inverse Gaussian models, for two reasons.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item The terms of the damage need not share the closure parameter.
    The missions of one component may have different Gamma rates, and an inverse Gaussian term of mean~$a$ in the formulation has the dispersion $\mu^2/\lambda = \eta\, a$, which grows with~$a$ (the model is stated below).
  \item A repair acts on the damage path, and the repaired damage leaves the family with the shared parameter, in both cases.
    Indeed,
    \begin{equation}
      (1-\rho)\,\mathrm{Ga}(A,\beta) = \mathrm{Ga}\bigl(A,\,\beta/(1-\rho)\bigr)
    \end{equation}
    has another rate, and
    \begin{equation}
      c\,\mathrm{IG}(\mu,\lambda) = \mathrm{IG}(c\mu,\,c\lambda)
    \end{equation}
    multiplies the dispersion $\mu^2/\lambda$ by~$c$.
\end{itemize}
For these two models the formulation therefore tracks the mean only.
It bounds the failure probability over a whole class of laws that contains every possible law of the damage, and it imposes a cap on the tracked mean.
The inverse Gaussian cap uses a further order.

\begin{definition}[Increasing convex order]\label{def:icx}
For integrable non-negative $X$ and $Y$, $X \le_{\mathrm{icx}} Y$ if $\mathbb{E}(X-d)_+ \le \mathbb{E}(Y-d)_+$ for every $d \ge 0$.
\end{definition}

The quantity $\mathbb{E}(X-d)_+$ is the stop-loss transform of~$X$ at~$d$.
This order does not compare tails directly: $X \le_{\mathrm{icx}} Y$ allows $\Pr(X > \tau) > \Pr(Y > \tau)$.
For example, the constant $X = 1$ and the variable $Y$ equal to $0$ or $2$ with probability $1/2$ each satisfy $X \le_{\mathrm{icx}} Y$, while
\begin{equation}
  \Pr(X > 0.9) = 1 > \Pr(Y > 0.9) = 1/2.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Inverse Gaussian: a cap on the mean.}
The inverse Gaussian law $\mathrm{IG}(\mu, \lambda)$ with mean $\mu > 0$ and shape $\lambda > 0$ has the density
\begin{equation}
  f(x; \mu, \lambda) = \sqrt{\frac{\lambda}{2\pi x^3}}\,\exp\!\Bigl(-\frac{\lambda\,(x - \mu)^2}{2\mu^2 x}\Bigr), \qquad x > 0,
\end{equation}
and the variance $\mu^3/\lambda$; for $c > 0$,
\begin{equation}
  c\,\mathrm{IG}(\mu, \lambda) = \mathrm{IG}(c\mu, c\lambda).
\end{equation}

The formulation gives each inverse Gaussian component one parameter $\eta > 0$.
A term of mean $a$ has the law $\mathrm{IG}(a, a/\eta) = a\,Z$, with $Z \sim \mathrm{IG}(1, 1/\eta)$.
Its density is
\begin{equation}
  \sqrt{a/(2\pi\eta x^3)}\,\exp\bigl(-(x - a)^2/(2\eta a x)\bigr),
\end{equation}
and $\eta$ is its squared coefficient of variation.
Every term has this law with its own mean: each mission increment, the initial damage and each replacement draw.
A known damage~$a$ is the constant term $a \cdot 1$.
The terms may depend on each other in any way.

Between replacements, under either repair law, the damage is
\begin{equation}
  D_k = \sum_t w_t\, a_t\, Z_t,
\end{equation}
where each $Z_t$ has the law of~$Z$ or equals~$1$, and each weight $w_t \in [0,1]$ is a product of factors $1-\rho$.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Weight zero.} A term of weight~$0$, possible when $\rho = 1$, is dropped.
  \item \textbf{Repair laws.} $\text{ARD}_\infty$ multiplies every weight by $1-\rho$, and $\text{ARD}_1$ multiplies only the weights of the terms added since the last intervention.
  \item \textbf{Anchor below the initial mean.} When the anchor before the first intervention is below the initial mean~$\mu_0$, the initial damage $\mu_0 Z_0$ is read as the two terms $g\,Z_0$ and $(\mu_0 - g)\,Z_0$, with $g$ the anchor.
  \item \textbf{Mean.} The mean $\sum_t w_t a_t$ is at most the tracked mean $\mu_k$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:domination}).
\end{itemize}

\begin{proposition}[Stop-loss order]\label{prop:ig_order}
Let
\begin{equation}
  D = \sum_t w_t\, a_t\, Z_t
\end{equation}
with weights $w_t \in [0,1]$, means $a_t \ge 0$ and $\sum_t w_t a_t \le \mu$, where each $Z_t$ has the law of~$Z$ or equals~$1$, under any joint law.
Then $D \le_{\mathrm{icx}} \mu Z$.
\end{proposition}
The proof is in Appendix~\ref{prf:prop:ig_order}, page~\pageref{prf:prop:ig_order}.

Let $q$ be the $(1-\varepsilon)$-quantile of~$Z$, so $\Pr(Z > q) = \varepsilon$, and let $\bar q = \mathbb{E}[Z \mid Z > q]$, the conditional value at risk (CVaR) of~$Z$ at the level $1-\varepsilon$.

\begin{theorem}[CVaR cap]\label{thm:ig_cap}
Let $D \ge 0$ satisfy $D \le_{\mathrm{icx}} \mu Z$ with $0 \le \mu \le \tau/\bar q$.
Then $\Pr(D > \tau) \le \varepsilon$.
\end{theorem}
The proof is in Appendix~\ref{prf:thm:ig_cap}, page~\pageref{prf:thm:ig_cap}.

So the formulation tracks the mean of an inverse Gaussian component and imposes the linear row
\begin{equation}
  \mu_k \le \bar\mu = \tau/\bar q
\end{equation}
on every day, under both repair laws.
By Proposition~\ref{prop:ig_order} and Theorem~\ref{thm:ig_cap}, the row gives $\Pr(D_k > \tau) \le \varepsilon$, whatever the dependence between the terms.

The quantile~$q$ follows from the distribution function~\eqref{eq:ig_cdf} of $Z$, with $\mu = 1$ and $\lambda = 1/\eta$.
The tail mean has the closed form $\mathbb{E}[Z;\, Z > q] = F_Z(1/q)$, so
\begin{equation}
  \bar\mu = \tau\,\varepsilon/F_Z(1/q).
\end{equation}
The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:ig_tailmean}, page~\pageref{drv:ig_tailmean}.
Table~\ref{tab:ig_cap} gives values of the cap.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{The CVaR cap $\bar\mu/\tau$ of the inverse Gaussian model, for three reliability levels and five values of~$\eta$.}
\label{tab:ig_cap}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}
\toprule
 & $\eta = 0.05$ & $0.25$ & $1$ & $4$ & $10$ \\
\midrule
$\varepsilon = 0.001$ & 0.491 & 0.242 & 0.101 & 0.0370 & 0.0190 \\
$\varepsilon = 0.01$ & 0.570 & 0.322 & 0.155 & 0.0679 & 0.0401 \\
$\varepsilon = 0.1$ & 0.693 & 0.477 & 0.299 & 0.183 & 0.141 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

No larger cap is safe for every law below $\mu Z$ in the order of Definition~\ref{def:icx}.
The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:ig_sharp}, page~\pageref{drv:ig_sharp}.
The schedules produce only some of the laws below $\mu Z$, so the row can reject some safe schedules.

The quantile cap $\tau/q$, at which the single law $\mu Z$ meets the target exactly, is not safe for sums.
Example: $\eta = 10$ and $\varepsilon = 0.1$, so $\tau/q = 0.473\,\tau$.
Three independent missions of equal mean, with total mean $\tau/q$, give $\Pr(D > \tau) = 0.118$.
The CVaR cap is $0.141\,\tau$ there.
At $\varepsilon = 0.01$ and $\eta$ from $0.05$ to $10$, the CVaR cap $\bar\mu$ is $0.93$ to $0.59$ times $\tau/q$.

\paragraph{Computation of the inverse Gaussian cap.}
The cap~$\bar\mu$ is computed once per component, before the model is built.
Its numerical choices have the following reasons.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Distribution function.} $F_Z$ is a sum of two positive terms, and $\ln F_Z$ is computed from their logarithms.
    So the sum loses no digits, and the factor $e^{2/\eta}$ of~\eqref{eq:ig_cdf} does not overflow when $\eta$ is small.
  \item \textbf{Survival function.} $1 - F_Z$ is a difference of two positive terms, and $\ln(1 - F_Z)$ is computed from their logarithms.
    The subtraction $1 - F_Z$ would lose the relative accuracy when $\varepsilon$ is small, because $F_Z$ is then close to~$1$ at the quantile.
  \item \textbf{Quantile.} The quantile is the root of $\ln\Pr(Z > e^u) = \ln\varepsilon$ in $u = \ln q$.
    The root search \texttt{brentq} of SciPy (a scientific library of the Python language) finds it on an interval $[-50, \ln b]$.
    By Cantelli's inequality~\eqref{eq:cantelli_surrogate} with $\operatorname{Var} Z = \eta$, the right end satisfies $\Pr(Z > b) \le \varepsilon/2$.
    So the function changes sign on the interval.
    The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:ig_bracket}, page~\pageref{drv:ig_bracket}.
  \item \textbf{Tail mean.} The closed form $\mathbb{E}[Z;\, Z > q] = F_Z(1/q)$ needs no numerical integration, and it is evaluated in logarithms too.
  \item \textbf{Accuracy of the quantile.} The root search is more accurate than the quantile function \texttt{invgauss.isf} of SciPy.
    The table gives the relative error of the survival function at each computed quantile, against a survival function computed with $60$ significant digits.
    \begin{center}
    \small
    \begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
    \toprule
     & \texttt{invgauss.isf} & root search \\
    \midrule
    $\eta = 10^{-6}$, $\varepsilon = 0.5$ & $1.6 \cdot 10^{-6}$ & $2 \cdot 10^{-13}$ \\
    $\eta = 10^{-3}$, $\varepsilon = 10^{-12}$ & $1.4 \cdot 10^{-5}$ & $5 \cdot 10^{-14}$ \\
    \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \end{center}
  \item \textbf{Checks.} On a grid of $11$ values of~$\eta$ from $10^{-3}$ to $10^{3}$ and $9$ values of~$\varepsilon$ from $10^{-6}$ to $0.9$, the quantile agrees with \texttt{invgauss.isf} to a relative $2.2 \cdot 10^{-12}$.
    On the same grid the closed-form tail mean agrees with a numerical integral to $4 \cdot 10^{-14}$.
    For $\eta$ from $10^{-6}$ to $10^{6}$ and $\varepsilon$ from $10^{-12}$ to $0.999$, the computation never fails, and every cap lies in $(0, \tau/q]$.
    A Monte Carlo sample of $2 \cdot 10^{6}$ draws of~$Z$ gives the conditional tail mean $2.61699$ against the computed $\bar q = 2.61568$ at $\eta = 0.5$ and $\varepsilon = 0.1$.
    At $\eta = 2$ and $\varepsilon = 0.05$ it gives $5.76704$ against $5.76507$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Gamma: a sharp cap on the mean.}
The formulation lets each mission of a Gamma component have its own rate: an increment of mean $m > 0$ and rate~$\beta$ has the law $\mathrm{Ga}(\beta m, \beta)$.
The initial damage and each replacement draw are Gamma terms with their own rates, or known constants.
All terms are independent.

A repair acts on the damage path by the law of the component: $\text{ARD}_\infty$ gives $D^+ = (1-\rho)\,D^-$, and $\text{ARD}_1$ gives
\begin{equation}
  D^+ = D^{\mathrm{last}} + (1-\rho)\,(D^- - D^{\mathrm{last}}).
\end{equation}
After a repair the damage is, in general, not a Gamma variable: it is a weighted sum of independent Gamma terms and known constants.
The cap below holds for every such sum.
The day map of the tracked mean is exact for the true mean $\mathbb{E}[D_k]$, because expectation is linear.

Let $\beta^*$ be the smallest rate among the terms of the component with a positive mean, over the missions and the days of both phases, and $t = \beta^*\tau$.
The formulation computes $\beta^*$ from the data; no input gives it.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item A mission with mean~$0$ adds no term, and a known damage is a constant, which does not enter~$\beta^*$.
  \item The rate of the replacement draw enters only when replacements are allowed.
  \item If no term has a rate, the damage is a known constant, and the cap below reads $\mu_k \le \tau$.
\end{itemize}
Measured in units of $1/\beta^*$, every law below has a rate of at least~$1$.
Write $Q(a, x) = \Pr(\mathrm{Ga}(a,1) > x)$ for the regularised upper incomplete gamma function.

\begin{definition}[Class of weighted Gamma sums]\label{def:gamma_class}
For $m \ge 0$, the class $\mathcal{G}(m)$ holds the laws of the random variables
\begin{equation}
  S = \delta + \sum_{i=1}^{n} c_i\, Y_i
\end{equation}
with $Y_i \sim \mathrm{Ga}(a_i, 1)$ independent, $c_i \in (0,1]$, $a_i > 0$, $\delta \ge 0$, $n \ge 0$, and mean $\delta + \sum_i c_i a_i = m$.
The term $c\,Y$ with $Y \sim \mathrm{Ga}(m'/c, 1)$ has mean~$m'$; as $c \to 0$ it tends to the constant~$m'$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[True states belong to the class]\label{lem:gamma_class}
On every day~$k$ of a schedule, under either repair law, the law of $\beta^* D_k$ belongs to $\mathcal{G}(\beta^*\,\mathbb{E}[D_k])$.
\end{lemma}
The proof is in Appendix~\ref{prf:lem:gamma_class}, page~\pageref{prf:lem:gamma_class}.

For $0 < m < t$, let $W(m, t)$ be the supremum of $\Pr(S > t)$ over the random variables $S$ whose law is in $\mathcal{G}(m)$.
The one-term element $c\,\mathrm{Ga}(m/c, 1)$ has the tail $Q(m/c,\, t/c)$ at~$t$.

An element of $\mathcal{G}(m)$ is described by its \emph{atoms}: the pairs $(m_i, c_i)$ of a term mean $m_i = c_i a_i$ and a scale~$c_i$, and the constant~$\delta$, read as an atom at scale $c = 0$.
Terms with equal scales merge into one term, because a sum of independent Gamma variables of one rate is a Gamma variable.

\begin{lemma}[First-order rates]\label{lem:gamma_rates}
Let the law of~$S$ be an element of $\mathcal{G}(m)$ with at least one term, with density~$f_S$.
Since $\delta \le m < t$, $f_S$ is continuous at~$t$.
For $c \in (0, 1]$ let
\begin{equation}
  \omega_S(c) = \frac{1}{c}\int_0^\infty \Pr(t - y < S \le t)\,\frac{e^{-y/c}}{y}\, \mathrm{d}y,
  \qquad \omega_S(0) = f_S(t).
\end{equation}
(a) Adding mean $\Delta m$ at scale~$c$ (a term $c\,\mathrm{Ga}(\Delta m/c, 1)$, or the constant $\Delta m$ when $c = 0$) raises $\Pr(S > t)$ at the rate $\omega_S(c)$ as $\Delta m \to 0$.
Removing mean from an atom at scale~$c$ lowers it at the same rate.
(b) Moving the scale of an atom $(m_i, c_i)$, $c_i \in (0,1)$, at fixed mean~$m_i$ changes $\Pr(S > t)$ at the rate $m_i\, \partial\omega_S/\partial c\,(c_i)$.
\end{lemma}
The proof is in Appendix~\ref{prf:lem:gamma_rates}, page~\pageref{prf:lem:gamma_rates}.

\begin{theorem}[One term is the worst case]\label{thm:gamma_oneterm}
For $0 < m < t$,
\begin{equation}
  W(m, t) \;=\; \max_{c \in (0,1]} Q\!\left(\frac{m}{c},\, \frac{t}{c}\right).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
The proof is in Appendix~\ref{prf:thm:gamma_oneterm}, page~\pageref{prf:thm:gamma_oneterm}.

\begin{lemma}[Monotone in the mean]\label{lem:gamma_monotone}
$W(m, t)$ is non-decreasing in $m \in (0, t)$.
\end{lemma}
The proof is in Appendix~\ref{prf:lem:gamma_monotone}, page~\pageref{prf:lem:gamma_monotone}.

For $A \in [0, t)$ let
\begin{equation}
  w(A) = \max_{c \in (0,1]} Q(A/c, t/c).
\end{equation}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item $w$ is continuous and non-decreasing in~$A$.
  \item $w(A) < 1/2$ for every $A < t$.
  \item $w(A) \to 1/2$ as $A \to t$.
\end{itemize}
The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:gamma_w}, page~\pageref{drv:gamma_w}.
So, for $\varepsilon < 1/2$, the \emph{sharp cap} $A_1(t,\varepsilon)$ is the largest $A \in [0, t)$ with $w(A) \le \varepsilon$, and it lies below~$t$.
For $\varepsilon \ge 1/2$ no largest value exists, because every $A < t$ qualifies.
The cap is then a value below~$t$ that the computation fixes.

The formulation tracks the mean of a Gamma component and imposes, on every day and under both repair laws, the linear row
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gamma_cap}
  \mu_k \;\le\; \frac{A_1(\beta^*\tau,\, \varepsilon)}{\beta^*}.
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[The sharp cap is safe]\label{prop:gamma_safe}
If the tracked mean $\mu_k$ satisfies~\eqref{eq:gamma_cap} and $\mu_k \ge \mathbb{E}[D_k]$, then $\Pr(D_k > \tau) \le \varepsilon$, for any mix of rates.
\end{proposition}
The proof is in Appendix~\ref{prf:prop:gamma_safe}, page~\pageref{prf:prop:gamma_safe}.

\paragraph{Computation of the cap.}
The cap $A_1$ is computed once per component, before the model is built, by bisection on~$A$ with an inner maximum over~$c$.
Three facts justify the computation, and $w(A_1) \le \varepsilon$ holds at the returned value.
The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:gamma_computation}, page~\pageref{drv:gamma_computation}.

The numerical choices of the computation have the following reasons.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{One-rate cap.} A bisection on the first argument of~$Q$ finds the exact one-rate cap~$\hat A$, the root of $Q(\hat A, t) = \varepsilon$.
    Its interval keeps $Q \le \varepsilon$ at the lower end and $Q > \varepsilon$ at the upper end, and the bisection returns the lower end.
    So $Q(\hat A, t) \le \varepsilon$, and the numerical error lies on the safe side.
  \item \textbf{Grid and its extensions.} The maximum over~$c$ starts from a logarithmic grid of $401$ values of~$c$.
    When the best grid point is the smallest value of the grid, the maximum may lie below the grid.
    A new grid of $401$ values then covers the next factor~$100$ below, at most $10$ times.
    After that the computation stops with an error: a maximum below the last grid would be underestimated, and the cap would be too large.
  \item \textbf{Golden-section search.} A golden-section search shrinks an interval around a single peak by the factor $0.618$ per step.
    The function of~$c$ has a single peak, by the second of the three facts.
    So $60$ steps between the neighbours of the best grid point reduce the interval by the factor $0.618^{60} \approx 3 \cdot 10^{-13}$.
    The search returns the largest value found, so it never returns less than the best grid value.
  \item \textbf{Tolerances and cost.} The bisection for~$\hat A$ stops at a relative width $10^{-12}$, and the bisection on~$A$ at $10^{-9}$.
    Each step of the bisection on~$A$ computes $w$ once.
    One cap takes about $30$\,ms and about $16$ evaluations of~$w$.
  \item \textbf{Levels of at least one half.} For $\varepsilon \ge 1/2$ the test $w(A) \le \varepsilon$ holds without computing~$w$, because $w(A) < 1/2$ for every $A < t$.
    Near $A = t$ the maximum over~$c$ moves towards $c \to 0$, where $Q$ approaches~$1/2$, so the grid would need many extensions.
    The bisection returns a value close to~$t$: $A_1/t = 0.999999999069$ at $\varepsilon = 0.5$.
  \item \textbf{Cache.} The cap is computed once for each pair $(t, \varepsilon)$ in a run and then reused.
    The input check of the replacement state and the row of the model use the same cap, and components with the same pair share it.
    So every use after the first costs nothing.
  \item \textbf{Accuracy.} The table compares the computation with independent ones.
    The brute force evaluates $20{,}001$ values of $s = t/c$, refines the best one and bisects on~$A$ to $10^{-13}$.
    \begin{center}
    \small
    \begin{tabular}{@{}llllc@{}}
    \toprule
    Value & Reference & $\varepsilon$ & $t$ & Largest difference \\
    \midrule
    $\hat A$ & \texttt{brentq}, tolerance $10^{-15}$ & $10^{-6}$ to $0.99$ & $10^{-6}$ to $10^{4}$ & $8.8 \cdot 10^{-13}$ (relative) \\
    $A_1$ & brute force & $0.001$ to $0.3$ & $10^{-4}$ to $1000$ & $6.7 \cdot 10^{-10}$ \\
    $A_1$ & brute force & $10^{-6}$ and $10^{-8}$ & & $2.2 \cdot 10^{-10}$ \\
    $A_1$ & brute force & $0.3$ to $0.499$ & $10^{-6}$ to $10^{4}$ & $7.4 \cdot 10^{-10}$ \\
    \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \end{center}
    In the last row no grid reaches its last extension.
    At nine pairs with $A_1 < \hat A$, $w(A_1)/\varepsilon = 1.000000000$ and $w\bigl(A_1 (1 + 10^{-6})\bigr)/\varepsilon = 1.000001$.
    So the computed cap is safe, and a cap larger by a relative $10^{-6}$ is not.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{The sharp cap and the exact one-rate cap.}
$\hat A$ is the exact cap of a single Gamma law of rate~$\beta^*$, and $A_1 \le \hat A$, because $c = 1$ is in the maximum.
Let $t_1(\varepsilon)$ be the value of~$t$ at which $\partial_c Q(\hat A/c, t/c)$ at $c = 1$ changes sign: $t_1 = 0.436$, $0.448$ and $0.614$ for $\varepsilon = 0.001$, $0.01$ and $0.1$.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Above $t_1$.} On a grid of $200$ values of~$t$ in $[t_1, 10^5]$, no $c < 1$ gives a larger tail than $c = 1$, so $A_1 = \hat A$ there. This is a numerical check, not a proof; the computation of the cap does not rely on it, because it tests $w(\hat A) \le \varepsilon$ before it uses~$\hat A$.
  \item \textbf{Below $t_1$.} $A_1 < \hat A$.
\end{itemize}
For $\varepsilon = 0.01$:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}
\toprule
$t = \beta^*\tau$ & 0.01 & 0.1 & 0.3 & 1 & 10 \\
\midrule
$A_1/\hat A$ & 0.142 & 0.641 & 0.961 & 1 & 1 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
When every term has the same rate and no repair occurs, the true state is a single Gamma law and $\hat A$ is exact for it.
With several rates, or with repairs, the true state is a weighted sum, and below~$t_1$ the exact one-rate cap is not safe.

Example: $\varepsilon = 0.01$ and $\beta^*\tau = 0.1$, so $\hat A = 0.0055012$.
A new part serves one mission of mean $\hat A/\beta^*$ and rate $4.56\,\beta^*$.
Its mean meets the exact one-rate cap, but its tail is $1.565\,\varepsilon$.
The sharp cap there is $A_1 = 0.641\,\hat A$.

\paragraph{Below the threshold.}
For $t \le t_1(\varepsilon)$,
\begin{equation}
  A_1(t, \varepsilon) = r^*(\varepsilon)\, t.
\end{equation}
The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:gamma_below}, page~\pageref{drv:gamma_below}.
The mean cap is then $A_1/\beta^* = r^*(\varepsilon)\,\tau$, whatever the rates: $r^* = 0.00355$, $0.0353$ and $0.331$ for $\varepsilon = 0.001$, $0.01$ and $0.1$.

\paragraph{The choice of $\beta^*$.}
The mean cap
\begin{equation}
  A_1(\beta^*\tau, \varepsilon)/\beta^* = \tau\, A_1(t, \varepsilon)/t
\end{equation}
is non-decreasing in~$\beta^*$.
The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:gamma_beta}, page~\pageref{drv:gamma_beta}.
Lemma~\ref{lem:gamma_class} holds for every $\beta^*$ at most the smallest rate.
So the smallest rate gives the largest safe cap of this form.

The class $\mathcal{G}(m)$ also contains laws that no schedule produces, so below~$t_1$ the row can reject some safe schedules.
The row keeps the mean of each mission.
$\beta^*$ only bounds how dispersed each term may be.

\subsubsection{Loop constraint for each degradation model}
\label{sec:loop_per_model}

The loop constraint~\eqref{eq:loop} is one rule for every model: each tracked statistic, and its anchor under $\text{ARD}_1$, must be no larger at the end of the horizon than at the end of the transitory phase.
Table~\ref{tab:loop_per_model} lists the tracked statistics and the reliability row of each model of the formulation.
Every model accepts both repair laws, and Theorem~\ref{thm:perpetual} applies to each of them.
We drop the $(i,\ell)$ subscripts for clarity.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Tracked statistics and reliability rows of the five degradation models of the formulation, on every day~$k$ of the horizon.
Under $\text{ARD}_1$ each tracked statistic also has an anchor, except the Wiener variance.
The loop constraint~\eqref{eq:loop} covers every tracked statistic and every anchor.
$\kappa_G = \Phi^{-1}(1-\varepsilon)$, with $\varepsilon < 0.5$.}
\label{tab:loop_per_model}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Tracked statistics} & \textbf{Reliability row} \\
\midrule
Gaussian         & mean $\mu$, variance $v$ & $\kappa_G^2\, v_k \le (\tau - \mu_k)^2$, \quad $\mu_k \le \tau$ \\
Wiener           & mean $\mu$, variance $v$ & as for the Gaussian model \\
Inverse Gaussian & mean $\mu$ & $\mu_k \le \tau/\bar q$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:ig_cap}) \\
Gamma            & mean $\mu$ & $\mu_k \le A_1(\beta^*\tau, \varepsilon)/\beta^*$, Eq.~\eqref{eq:gamma_cap} \\
Rainflow         & mean $\mu$ and the statistic of the bound & Table~\ref{tab:five_bounds} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Gamma.}
With one shared rate~$\beta$ and no repair, the damage is $\mathrm{Ga}(A_k,\,\beta)$, where $A_k$ is the cumulative shape on day~$k$.
Its survival function is $\Pr(D > \tau) = Q(A,\,\beta\tau)$, with $Q$ the regularised upper incomplete gamma function of Section~\ref{sec:stoch_orders}.
Since $Q(a,x)$ is strictly increasing in~$a$ for fixed $x > 0$, a loop on the shape would then be a scalar inequality.

One caveat concerns repairs.
The \emph{pathwise} ARD repair $D \mapsto (1-\rho)D$ leaves the shared-rate sub-family, since
\begin{equation}
  (1-\rho)\,\mathrm{Ga}(A,\beta) = \mathrm{Ga}\bigl(A,\,\beta/(1-\rho)\bigr).
\end{equation}
A repair modelled in parameter space, as the shape reduction $A \mapsto (1-\rho)A$ at fixed rate~$\beta$, matches ARD in expectation,
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb{E}[D^+] = (1-\rho)\,\mathbb{E}[D^-].
\end{equation}
But it is a different operator on the paths, and it can accept an unsafe schedule.

Example: $\tau = 1$, $\varepsilon = 0.01$, $\rho = 0.5$ and one rate $\beta = 0.01$, so the exact one-rate cap is $\hat A = 0.0024877$.
A new part serves a mission of shape~$\hat A$ on day~1, is repaired on day~2, and serves a mission of shape $0.5\,\hat A$ on day~3.
The shapes in parameter space are $\hat A$, $0.5\,\hat A$ and $\hat A$, so every cap row holds.
But the true tail on day~3 is $0.0133$, that is, $1.33\,\varepsilon$.

The formulation therefore repairs the damage path, tracks the mean, and certifies reliability by the sharp cap~\eqref{eq:gamma_cap}, which is safe for every weighted sum of Gamma terms (Proposition~\ref{prop:gamma_safe}).
Its loop constraint acts on the mean and, under $\text{ARD}_1$, on the anchor of the mean.
Facts (M1)--(M3) of Section~\ref{sec:encoding} hold.
The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:mfacts_gamma}, page~\pageref{drv:mfacts_gamma}.

The Gaussian family is closed under both readings of the repair,
\begin{equation}
  (1-\rho)\,\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^2) = \mathcal{N}\bigl((1-\rho)\mu,\,(1-\rho)^2\sigma^2\bigr).
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Gaussian.}
Let
\begin{equation}
  D_{H_1} \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_{H_1},\,\sigma_{H_1}^2)
  \quad\text{and}\quad
  D_T \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_T,\,\sigma_T^2).
\end{equation}
The survival function is
\begin{equation}
  \Pr(D > \tau) = \Phi\bigl((\mu - \tau)/\sigma\bigr),
\end{equation}
where $\Phi$ is the standard normal CDF.
Since $\Phi$ is strictly increasing, the probability comparison~\eqref{eq:loop_prob} is equivalent to
\begin{equation}\label{eq:loop_gauss}
  \frac{\mu_T - \tau}{\sigma_T} \;\le\; \frac{\mu_{H_1} - \tau}{\sigma_{H_1}},
\end{equation}
or equivalently
\begin{equation}
  (\tau - \mu_T)/\sigma_T \ge (\tau - \mu_{H_1})/\sigma_{H_1}.
\end{equation}

Note that $\sigma_T = \sigma_{H_1}$ does \emph{not} follow from serving the same missions in both phases.
Without interventions the variance accumulates,
\begin{equation}
  \sigma^2_T = \sigma^2_{H_1} + \sum_{k = H_1+1}^{T} v^{\mathrm{inc}}_k > \sigma^2_{H_1},
\end{equation}
where $v^{\mathrm{inc}}_k$ is the variance of the increment of day~$k$ ($0$ on a day without a mission).
Equality requires that the repairs or replacements of the operating phase (which scale the variance by $(1-\rho)^2$ or reset it) remove exactly the variance that the mission days add.
In that case~\eqref{eq:loop_gauss} reduces to $\mu_T \le \mu_{H_1}$.

In general, condition~\eqref{eq:loop_gauss} couples the mean and the standard deviation: a sufficiently lower mean can compensate a higher variance at~$T$.
Condition~\eqref{eq:loop_gauss} constrains only the single scalar $(\mu - \tau)/\sigma$ of a two-parameter state.
So, by itself, it does not make the schedule repeatable (see Section~\ref{sec:loop_routeI}).

The componentwise condition $\mu_T \le \mu_{H_1}$ and $\sigma^2_T \le \sigma^2_{H_1}$ restores repeatability; it is the analogue of~\eqref{eq:loop_moments} for the exact law.
It implies~\eqref{eq:loop_gauss} whenever $\mu_{H_1} < \tau$, and it chains because both moments propagate monotonically under the dynamics.
This componentwise condition is the loop constraint~\eqref{eq:loop} of the Gaussian model, together with the rows on the two anchors under $\text{ARD}_1$.

In the formulation, a mission increment of a Gaussian component is $\mathcal{N}(\mu^{\mathrm{inc}}, v^{\mathrm{inc}})$, the initial damage is $\mathcal{N}(\mu_0, v_0)$, and each replacement draw is $\mathcal{N}(\mu^{\mathrm{new}}, v^{\mathrm{new}})$.
A known damage has variance~$0$.
These terms are mutually independent (assumption N1).
A repair acts on the damage path by the law of the component.

The Gaussian law gives negative damage a small probability.
The input checks $\mu \ge 3\sqrt{v}$ for the initial damage and for each increment keep this probability at most $\Phi(-3) = 0.135\,\%$ for each of these terms.

\begin{proposition}[The state stays Gaussian]\label{prop:gauss_exact}
Under N1, on every day of a schedule $D_k \sim \mathcal{N}(m_k, V_k)$, where $(m_k, V_k)$ follow the day maps of the mean and the variance, with factors $1-\rho$ and $(1-\rho)^2$, and with anchors equal to the mean and the variance just after the last intervention.
\end{proposition}
The proof is in Appendix~\ref{prf:prop:gauss_exact}, page~\pageref{prf:prop:gauss_exact}.

So $\text{ARD}_1$ is exact for this model.
Its variance map uses the factor $(1-\rho)^2$ and the variance anchor.
For $D \sim \mathcal{N}(m, V)$,
\begin{equation}
  \Pr(D > \tau) = 1 - \Phi\bigl((\tau - m)/\sqrt{V}\bigr) \le \varepsilon
\end{equation}
exactly when $m + \kappa_G\sqrt{V} \le \tau$, with $\kappa_G = \Phi^{-1}(1-\varepsilon)$.
For $\varepsilon < 0.5$, so that $\kappa_G > 0$, and with $\tau - \mu \ge 0$, this is equivalent to the squared row of Table~\ref{tab:loop_per_model}.

\emph{Validity.} Let $(m_k, V_k) \le (\mu_k, v_k)$ be the true moments (Proposition~\ref{prop:gauss_exact} and Proposition~\ref{prop:domination}), and let $(\mu_k, v_k)$ satisfy the row.
Then $\Pr(D_k > \tau) \le \varepsilon$.
The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:gauss_validity}, page~\pageref{drv:gauss_validity}.

\begin{remark}[The level $\varepsilon$ and the range $V_{\max}$]\label{rem:vmax}
$V_{\max} = \tau^2/\kappa_G^2$ is the largest variance that the Gaussian row admits, at $\mu = 0$.
So it is the natural range of the variance, and the big-M constant of its rows (Section~\ref{sec:impl_encoding}).

$V_{\max}/\tau^2$ is $0.18$ at $\varepsilon = 0.01$, $0.61$ at $0.1$, $3.6$ at $0.3$ and about $1600$ at $0.49$.
It grows without bound as $\varepsilon \to 0.5$, and at $\varepsilon \ge 0.5$ the squared row does not describe the reliability requirement, because $\kappa_G \le 0$.
Hence the condition $\varepsilon < 0.5$ for Gaussian and Wiener components.
The range is large only close to~$0.5$.

The other models and bounds have no limit below $\varepsilon = 1$: for example, the variance cap $\tau^2\varepsilon/(1-\varepsilon)$ of the Cantelli row is finite for every $\varepsilon < 1$.
The variance ranges of the rainflow rows behave like $V_{\max}$ at the other end: the Cantelli and Bernstein variance ranges and the Hoeffding range $2\tau^2/\kappa$ grow without bound as $\varepsilon \to 1$.
\end{remark}

Facts (M1)--(M3) of Section~\ref{sec:encoding} hold.
The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:mfacts_gauss}, page~\pageref{drv:mfacts_gauss}.

\paragraph{Wiener.}
The damage is a Wiener process with drift, observed once a day.
A mission adds a Gaussian increment with a mean that depends on the mission and with the variance~$\sigma^2$, the same for every mission.
The reason is that $\sigma$ describes the material of the component, not the route.
The initial damage is $\mathcal{N}(\mu_0, v_0)$ with $v_0 \ge 0$, and each replacement draw is $\mathcal{N}(\mu^{\mathrm{new}}, v^{\mathrm{new}})$.
These terms are mutually independent (assumption W1).

In the formulation, a repair acts on the mean only; this is a modelling choice.
The repair shifts the damage by a known amount: $D^+ = D^- - \rho\, m^-$ under $\text{ARD}_\infty$ and $D^+ = D^- - \rho\,(m^- - g^{\mu})$ under $\text{ARD}_1$, with $m^- = \mathbb{E}D^-$.
The law stays Gaussian, the variance is unchanged, and only a replacement lowers it.

Under W1 the damage is $\mathcal{N}(m_k, V_k)$ on every day, with $(m_k, V_k)$ given by these maps: a mission adds an independent Gaussian term, and a repair or a replacement acts as stated.

The reliability row is the Gaussian one, with the same validity argument, forms and remark on~$V_{\max}$.
The rows of the Wiener model are safe also when a repair acts on the damage path, as in the Gaussian model.
The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:wiener_path}, page~\pageref{drv:wiener_path}.

Facts (M1)--(M3) hold as for the Gaussian model, with the factor $1$ for the variance, which has no anchor.
The loop row on the variance forces a replacement in the operating phase whenever the member serves a mission there.
Without a replacement,
\begin{equation}
  v_T \ge v_{H_1} + n\,\sigma^2 > v_{H_1}
\end{equation}
after $n \ge 1$ mission days.
No input check enforces this.
When replacements are not allowed, the member of a Wiener component cannot serve a mission in the operating phase.

\paragraph{Inverse Gaussian.}
The CDF of $\mathrm{IG}(\mu,\lambda)$ admits the closed form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ig_cdf}
  F_{\mathrm{IG}}(x;\,\mu,\lambda) \;=\;
    \Phi\!\Biggl(\sqrt{\frac{\lambda}{x}}\Bigl(\frac{x}{\mu}-1\Bigr)\Biggr)
    \;+\; e^{2\lambda/\mu}\;
    \Phi\!\Biggl(-\sqrt{\frac{\lambda}{x}}\Bigl(\frac{x}{\mu}+1\Bigr)\Biggr).
\end{equation}
For
\begin{equation}
  D_{H_1} \sim \mathrm{IG}(\mu_{H_1},\,\lambda_{H_1})
  \quad\text{and}\quad
  D_T \sim \mathrm{IG}(\mu_T,\,\lambda_T),
\end{equation}
the probability comparison~\eqref{eq:loop_prob} reads
\begin{equation}
  F_{\mathrm{IG}}(\tau;\,\mu_T,\lambda_T) \;\ge\; F_{\mathrm{IG}}(\tau;\,\mu_{H_1},\lambda_{H_1}).
\end{equation}
This is an analytical but nonlinear inequality in the four parameters $(\mu_{H_1},\lambda_{H_1},\mu_T,\lambda_T)$.
Under the closure condition (same $\mu^2/\lambda$), the $\le_{\mathrm{lr}}$ results of Table~\ref{tab:ordering_lr} guarantee that $\mu_T \le \mu_{H_1}$ is a sufficient condition.

The formulation does not use this comparison, because its damage is not an inverse Gaussian variable in general (Section~\ref{sec:stoch_orders}).
It tracks the mean, imposes the CVaR cap of Theorem~\ref{thm:ig_cap}, and loops on the mean and, under $\text{ARD}_1$, on the anchor of the mean.
Facts (M1)--(M3) of Section~\ref{sec:encoding} hold.
The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:mfacts_ig}, page~\pageref{drv:mfacts_ig}.

\paragraph{Rainflow.}
The tracked statistics and the rows are those of Section~\ref{sec:cons_surrogates}.
Facts (M1)--(M3) of Section~\ref{sec:encoding} hold.
The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:mfacts_rf}, page~\pageref{drv:mfacts_rf}.
The loop constraint covers the mean, the second statistic of the bound and, under $\text{ARD}_1$, their anchors.

\paragraph{Compound Poisson.}
When $D \sim \mathrm{CP}(\nu,F_Y)$, the survival function does not admit a universal closed form.
Conditioning on the number of shocks gives the series
\begin{equation}\label{eq:surv_cp}
  \Pr(D > \tau) \;=\; \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \frac{e^{-\nu}\,\nu^n}{n!}\;\Pr\!\Bigl(\sum_{k=1}^{n} Y_k > \tau\Bigr),
\end{equation}
which can be evaluated numerically (it converges rapidly) or bounded.

\paragraph{Upper bounds when no closed form is available.}
Sometimes the survival function $\Pr(D > \tau)$ has no closed form.
This happens for the compound Poisson process, and when the closure condition fails and the marginal of the sum is not in a standard family.
The \emph{reliability} constraint can then still be enforced through conservative upper bounds on the failure probability:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item Markov ($\Pr(D > \tau) \le \mathbb{E}[D]/\tau$);
  \item Cantelli~\eqref{eq:cantelli_surrogate};
  \item Chernoff~\eqref{eq:chernoff_surrogate}.
\end{itemize}
Section~\ref{sec:cons_surrogates} develops these bounds in per-mission form, together with the Bernstein and Hoeffding refinements, their relative tightness in the small-$\varepsilon$ regime and their MILP encodings.
For the compound Poisson process the Chernoff bound is particularly convenient, since $M_D(s) = \exp\bigl(\nu\,(M_Y(s)-1)\bigr)$ has a closed form whenever $M_Y$ has one.

The \emph{loop} constraint, by contrast, must not be translated into the same bounds.
Imposing $U^{(1)} \le U^{(0)}$ on a scalar bound~$U$ does not make the schedule repeatable.
The loop acts instead on the tracked statistics of the bound, for example through the componentwise moment condition~\eqref{eq:loop_moments}, whose rows are linear in the tracked statistics (Section~\ref{sec:loop_routeI}).

\subsection{Maintenance strategies}
\label{sec:maint_strategies}

Maintenance actions on a repairable component lie between two extremes.
\emph{Minimal repair} restores the component to its state just before failure (``as bad as old'', ABAO).
\emph{Perfect repair}, or \emph{replacement}, resets it to the condition of a new part (``as good as new'', AGAN).
Real interventions almost always lie between these two extremes, and together they are called \emph{imperfect maintenance}~\cite{PhamWang1996}.
This section surveys the principal modelling families.

\subsubsection{Taxonomy of repair actions}

Table~\ref{tab:repair_actions} classifies five canonical maintenance actions by their effect on the component state.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Taxonomy of maintenance actions, from least to most restorative.}
\label{tab:repair_actions}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{3.0cm}p{4.5cm}p{5.8cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Action} & \textbf{Effect on damage $D$} & \textbf{Model} \\
\midrule
No intervention
  & $D^+ = D^-$
  & Damage unchanged \\[3pt]
Minimal repair (ABAO)
  & $D^+ = D^-$
  & Failure intensity restored to pre-failure level; damage history preserved~\cite{BrownProschan1983} \\[3pt]
Imperfect repair
  & $D^- > D^+ > D^{\mathrm{new}}$
  & Partial reduction of damage or virtual age (see below) \\[3pt]
Perfect repair (AGAN)
  & $D^+ = D^{\mathrm{new}}$
  & Component returned to like-new state \\[3pt]
Replacement
  & $D^+ = D^{\mathrm{new}}$
  & Physical component substituted; equivalent to perfect repair \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\noindent Here $D^-$ and $D^+$ denote the damage immediately before and after maintenance, and $D^{\mathrm{new}}$ is the damage of a new component.

\subsubsection{Virtual age models}

Kijima~\cite{Kijima1989} formalised imperfect repair through the concept of \emph{virtual age}.
After the $n$-th repair, the component behaves as if it had the age $V_n$ instead of its calendar age.
The failure intensity at calendar time~$t$ is $\lambda(t - T_n + V_n)$, where $T_n$ is the time of the $n$-th repair and $\lambda(\cdot)$ is the baseline intensity.
Two variants are standard:
\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Kijima~I.} The repair acts only on the virtual age accumulated since the previous repair:
    \begin{equation}
      V_n \;=\; V_{n-1} + (1-\rho)\,(T_n - T_{n-1}),
    \end{equation}
    where $\rho \in [0,1]$ is the repair efficiency.
    Only the time since the previous repair becomes partly younger (``memory-one'').
  \item \textbf{Kijima~II.} The repair acts on the entire accumulated virtual age:
    \begin{equation}
      V_n \;=\; (1-\rho)\,(V_{n-1} + T_n - T_{n-1}).
    \end{equation}
    The whole history becomes younger (``infinite memory'').
\end{itemize}
In both cases $\rho = 0$ gives minimal repair (ABAO) and $\rho = 1$ gives perfect repair (AGAN).

\subsubsection{Reduction-based models}

Doyen and Gaudoin~\cite{DoyenGaudoin2004} classify imperfect repair models by the \emph{quantity} that the repair reduces.
The classes ARA (arithmetic reduction of age) reduce the virtual age, and the classes ARI (arithmetic reduction of intensity) reduce the failure intensity.
Each class has a memory: the largest number of previous failure times that enter the failure intensity~\cite[p.~8]{DoyenGaudoin2004}.
The memory-one variant carries the subscript~1, and the infinite-memory variant the subscript~$\infty$.

No class of Doyen and Gaudoin acts on a degradation level.
Mercier and Castro~\cite{MercierCastro2019} define a reduction that acts on the degradation level itself.
Table~\ref{tab:reduction_classes} compares the reduction classes.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Reduction classes of imperfect repair (arithmetic reduction of age, ARA; of deterioration, ARD; of intensity, ARI): the reduced quantity, the laws and their sources.}
\label{tab:reduction_classes}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{1.2cm}p{3.3cm}p{2.6cm}p{6.9cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Class} & \textbf{Reduced quantity} & \textbf{Laws} & \textbf{Source} \\
\midrule
ARA
  & virtual age~$V$
  & $\text{ARA}_1$~\eqref{eq:ara1}\newline $\text{ARA}_\infty$~\eqref{eq:arainf}
  & \cite[eq.~(22), p.~12]{DoyenGaudoin2004} for $\text{ARA}_1$ and \cite[eq.~(20), p.~11]{DoyenGaudoin2004} for $\text{ARA}_\infty$ \\[3pt]
ARD
  & degradation level~$D$
  & $\text{ARD}_1$~\eqref{eq:ard1}\newline $\text{ARD}_\infty$~\eqref{eq:ardinf}
  & $\text{ARD}_1$: the ``arithmetic reduction of deterioration of order~1'' of Mercier and Castro~\cite[\S3.1, eq.~(4)]{MercierCastro2019}; $\text{ARD}_\infty$: neither source names it, and its name is an analogy \\[3pt]
ARI
  & failure intensity~$\lambda$
  & $\text{ARI}_1$~\eqref{eq:ari1}\newline $\text{ARI}_\infty$~\eqref{eq:ariinf}
  & Doyen and Gaudoin~\cite{DoyenGaudoin2004} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Arithmetic Reduction of Age (ARA).}
The repair removes a fraction~$\rho$ of the virtual age~$V$:
\begin{align}
  \text{ARA}_1: &\quad V^+ = V^- - \rho\,(V^- - V^{\mathrm{last}}), \label{eq:ara1} \\
  \text{ARA}_\infty: &\quad V^+ = (1-\rho)\,V^-, \label{eq:arainf}
\end{align}
where $V^{\mathrm{last}}$ is the virtual age just after the previous repair.
$\text{ARA}_1$ reduces only the age accumulated since the previous repair, and $\text{ARA}_\infty$ reduces the whole virtual age.
With
\begin{equation}
  V^- = V^{\mathrm{last}} + (T_n - T_{n-1}),
\end{equation}
$\text{ARA}_1$ coincides with Kijima~I and $\text{ARA}_\infty$ with Kijima~II.

\paragraph{Arithmetic Reduction of Deterioration (ARD).}
The repair removes a fraction~$\rho$ of the degradation level~$D$:
\begin{align}
  \text{ARD}_1: &\quad D^+ = D^- - \rho\,(D^- - D^{\mathrm{last}}), \label{eq:ard1} \\
  \text{ARD}_\infty: &\quad D^+ = (1-\rho)\,D^-. \label{eq:ardinf}
\end{align}
$\text{ARD}_1$ removes the fraction~$\rho$ of the degradation accumulated since the last maintenance.
It acts on the degradation path, and the age is unchanged.
The reference level $D^{\mathrm{last}}$ is the level just after the last intervention, repair or replacement.
Before the first intervention, it is the initial level.
$\text{ARD}_\infty$ applies the rule of $\text{ARA}_\infty$ to the whole degradation level, as $\text{ARD}_1$ applies the rule of $\text{ARA}_1$ to the degradation accumulated since the last intervention.

\paragraph{Arithmetic Reduction of Intensity (ARI).}
Instead of reducing the virtual age, the repair directly reduces the failure intensity:
\begin{align}
  \text{ARI}_1: &\quad \lambda^+ = \lambda^- - \rho\,(\lambda^- - \lambda^{\mathrm{last}}), \label{eq:ari1} \\
  \text{ARI}_\infty: &\quad \lambda^+ = (1-\rho)\,\lambda^-, \label{eq:ariinf}
\end{align}
where $\lambda^{\mathrm{last}}$ is the failure intensity just after the previous repair.
ARI models are natural when maintenance targets the failure rate (for example, condition-based interventions) rather than the underlying degradation level.
They are in general not equivalent to ARA or ARD models.
A reduction in intensity corresponds to a unique reduction in virtual age only if the baseline intensity is strictly monotone~\cite{DoyenGaudoin2004}.

\subsubsection{Connection to the fleet management formulation}
\label{sec:repair_connection}

The abstract repair operator $\mathcal{R}(D^-,\,\rho)$ of Section~\ref{sec:overview} covers the ARD models and replacement.
In particular:
\begin{itemize}
  \item $\text{ARD}_\infty$: $\mathcal{R}(D^-,\rho) = (1-\rho)\,D^-$, the default repair law of the formulation;
  \item $\text{ARD}_1$:
    \begin{equation}
      \mathcal{R}(D^-,\rho) = D^- - \rho\,(D^- - D^{\mathrm{last}}),
    \end{equation}
    requiring the additional state variable $D^{\mathrm{last}}$;
  \item Replacement / AGAN: $\mathcal{R}(D^-,\rho) = D^{\mathrm{new}}$ (captured by the separate replacement variable $x^r_{i\ell k}$ in the formulation).
\end{itemize}
The formulation offers both repair laws for every degradation model, chosen per component.
The choice of the repair law affects the damage dynamics~\eqref{eq:dyn_full}, the definition of the repair-cost variable~$z_{i\ell k}$ and the tractability of the resulting optimisation problem.

ARI models do not fit directly into the formulation of Section~\ref{sec:overview}, which tracks the damage.
They would need a reformulation in terms of the failure intensity instead of the damage.
No law of the formulation acts on a virtual age.

\paragraph{Anchors.}
Under $\text{ARD}_1$ the formulation tracks, for each statistic of the damage, an \emph{anchor}: the value of the statistic just after the last intervention, repair or replacement.
On an intervention day the anchor takes the new value of its statistic.
On any other day it keeps its value.

Example: mean $0.6$ and anchor $0.2$ before a repair with $\rho = 0.5$.
The repair gives the mean $0.6 - 0.5\,(0.6 - 0.2) = 0.4$ and the anchor $0.4$.
On the next day the gap between the mean and the anchor is $0$, and it grows again with each mission.

Before the first intervention of the horizon, the anchor is the value just after the last intervention before the horizon.
When that value is unknown, the formulation takes the initial value of the statistic.
Each statistic is non-decreasing between interventions (fact~(M1) of Section~\ref{sec:encoding}), so the true value lies between the value of a new part and the initial value.
A larger anchor gives a weaker repair, so the initial value never overstates what a repair achieves.

Example: initial mean $0.5$, $\rho = 0.5$, and a repair on day~1 before any mission.
With the anchor $0$ the mean drops to $0.25$.
With the anchor $0.5$ it stays at $0.5$, and the repair removes only damage accumulated within the horizon.

\paragraph{Repair maps of the tracked statistics.}
A model tracks moments or other summaries of the damage~$D$, not $D$ itself.
A repair maps a tracked statistic~$\theta$ with anchor~$g$ to $a\,\theta + (1-a)\,g$, with a factor $a \in [0,1]$ and $g = 0$ under $\text{ARD}_\infty$.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Mean.} $a = 1-\rho$.
  \item \textbf{Variance and Hoeffding budget.} $a = (1-\rho)^2$.
  \item \textbf{Cumulant generating function of the Chernoff bound.} $a = 1-\rho$ gives an upper bound (Proposition~\ref{prop:rf_maps}).
  \item \textbf{Wiener variance.} It is the exception: a repair leaves it unchanged ($a = 1$), and it has no anchor (Section~\ref{sec:loop_per_model}).
\end{itemize}
The derivation of the factors of the mean and the variance is in Appendix~\ref{drv:repair_maps}, page~\pageref{drv:repair_maps}.
These maps are non-decreasing in the statistic and in its anchor, as fact (M1) and Lemma~\ref{lem:monotone} require.

\paragraph{Laws per model.}
Every model of the formulation accepts both laws.
Table~\ref{tab:laws_per_model} gives the reason for each model.
Under $\text{ARD}_1$ with $\rho < 1$, the loop constraint requires a replacement in the operating phase of a component that gains damage there (Proposition~\ref{prop:ard1_replacement}).

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Repair laws accepted by each degradation model, with the reason.}
\label{tab:laws_per_model}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lllp{7.4cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Model} & $\text{ARD}_\infty$ & $\text{ARD}_1$ & \textbf{Reason or note} \\
\midrule
Gaussian & yes & yes & both laws keep the state Gaussian, with exact moments (Proposition~\ref{prop:gauss_exact}) \\
Inverse Gaussian & yes & yes & the cap holds for any repair weights and any dependence between the terms (Proposition~\ref{prop:ig_order} and Theorem~\ref{thm:ig_cap}) \\
Wiener & yes & yes & on the mean only; the variance changes only by replacement \\
Gamma & yes & yes & both laws keep the state a weighted sum of Gamma terms (Lemma~\ref{lem:gamma_class}), and the sharp cap holds for every such sum (Proposition~\ref{prop:gamma_safe}) \\
Rainflow, every bound & yes & yes & under both laws, Chernoff uses a conservative recursion for the cumulant generating function (Proposition~\ref{prop:rf_maps}) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Maintenance under the Palmgren--Miner damage model.}
The remaining-life framework of Section~\ref{sec:remaining_life} represents damage as the normalised fraction
\begin{equation}
  D_{\mathrm{PM}} = \sum_i n_i/N_{f,i} \ge 0,
\end{equation}
with failure at $D_{\mathrm{PM}} \ge 1$.
$D_{\mathrm{PM}}$ adds up over loading blocks.
So it fits the same damage-tracking structure as the stochastic processes of Table~\ref{tab:deg_models}: each mission day adds an increment $\Delta D_{\mathrm{PM}} = n_j / N_{f,j}$.
This increment is deterministic if the S--N parameters are fixed, and random if the scatter is modelled~\cite{MunizCalvente2022}.
The repair operator~$\mathcal{R}$ and the replacement therefore apply directly:
\begin{itemize}
  \item \emph{Replacement} resets the fatigue fraction to zero: $D_{\mathrm{PM}}^+ = 0$ (equivalently $D^{\mathrm{new}} = 0$).
    A new component, with a cycle count of zero, takes the place of the physical component.
  \item $\text{ARD}_\infty$:
    \begin{equation}
      D_{\mathrm{PM}}^+ = (1-\rho)\,D_{\mathrm{PM}}^-.
    \end{equation}
    Physically, this models an intervention that partly restores the fatigue life without resetting the whole loading history, for example a surface treatment, shot peening or a local crack repair.
  \item $\text{ARD}_1$:
    \begin{equation}
      D_{\mathrm{PM}}^+ = D_{\mathrm{PM}}^- - \rho\,(D_{\mathrm{PM}}^- - D_{\mathrm{PM}}^{\mathrm{last}}).
    \end{equation}
    The repair reverses part of the damage accumulated since the last intervention (repair or replacement), and only that damage.
\end{itemize}
Unlike the stochastic process models, $D_{\mathrm{PM}}$ is normalised so that failure occurs at~$1$.
So the threshold is fixed at $\tau = 1$, and the reliability constraint becomes
\begin{equation}
  \Pr(D_{\mathrm{PM}} \ge 1) \le \varepsilon.
\end{equation}
In the deterministic variant of Miner's rule this is a hard feasibility check.
In the stochastic extension it becomes a chance constraint analogous to~\eqref{eq:abs_rel}.

Closure under summation holds trivially: $D_{\mathrm{PM}}$ is a sum of real-valued increments.
When the increments are independent, as under the block-independent scatter~(I) of Section~\ref{sec:miner_randomN}, the marginal on any day is the convolution of the individual increments, whatever their distributional family.
Under the unit-specific scatter~(II) the increments share the factor~$A$, and the marginal is the law of $M_k/A$.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item In the deterministic variant of Miner's rule, the comparison of two schedules reduces to the comparison of two real numbers, and no stochastic order is needed.
  \item In the stochastic extension (scatter in $N_{f,i}$), the parametric ordering conditions of Section~\ref{sec:stoch_orders} apply only if the assumption on the scatter places the increments $n_i/N_{f,i}$ in one of the families of Table~\ref{tab:deg_models}.
    Otherwise the general definitions of the orders still hold, but they must be verified case by case.
\end{itemize}

\section{Focus on electric vehicles}

A battery-electric road vehicle, such as a transit bus or a passenger car, is a complex repairable system.
Its parts degrade at very different rates and through very different physical mechanisms.
Not every part needs an explicit place in the fleet-management model of Section~\ref{sec:overview}.
We keep a component if it satisfies two criteria:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(C\arabic*)]
  \item \textbf{Usage-driven degradation.}
    Its condition worsens measurably with \emph{how} the vehicle is used.
    So the mission assigned on a given day produces a damage increment $\Delta D^{(j)}_{i\ell k}$ that can be quantified (Section~\ref{sec:overview}).
    A part that ages only with calendar time, whatever the mission served, carries no information for the schedule.
    Its damage need not enter the model, which tracks the damage that the missions cause.
  \item \textbf{Operational significance.}
    Its failure or its preventive renewal strongly affects at least one of the three quantities that the schedule balances: \emph{safety}, \emph{availability} or \emph{life-cycle maintenance cost}.
\end{enumerate}
We choose \emph{not} to require that the component has a degradation model within the families of Section~\ref{sec:stoch_orders}.
A component can be worth considering even when the abstract model of Section~\ref{sec:overview} does not yet capture it.
Such cases are listed here with the modelling extension that they would need, rather than dropped without a note.

The components that meet these criteria form two groups.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item The \emph{electrochemical and electrical powertrain} (traction battery, electric motor, power electronics, charging interface) degrades through electrochemical ageing and through thermal and electrical cycling.
    Energy throughput, charge and discharge rates and temperature drive this degradation.
  \item The \emph{mechanical running gear} (tires, friction brakes, wheel bearings, suspension, reduction gear) degrades through wear and fatigue.
    Distance travelled, vehicle mass, road quality and the profile of acceleration and braking drive this degradation.
\end{itemize}
Table~\ref{tab:ev_components} lists the main candidates in each group, the dominant degradation mechanism, the operational quantity that drives it, and the reason why the component is operationally significant.

Two facts of the domain shape the relative importance of these components.
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{The traction battery dominates.}
    A recent predictive-maintenance study of electric buses~\cite[Secs.~2,~4.2]{Ibrahim2025} centres entirely on the battery pack as the main maintenance-critical component.
    The study models the end of life of the pack through the accumulated charge throughput.
    It shows that optimising the driving profile alone can cut the modelled capacity loss by 25\%.
    This is direct evidence that battery damage can be steered by exactly the kind of mission-assignment decisions that our framework optimises.
  \item \textbf{Regenerative braking shifts the wear burden to the tires.}
    Regenerative braking absorbs most of the service braking, and it reduces the friction-brake wear emissions by an estimated 60--90\%~\cite[Sec.~6.1]{Fussell2022}.
    The relative wear burden therefore shifts to the tires, and the higher mass of electric vehicles increases tire wear.
    On motorways, the savings from regenerative braking do not compensate the added tire-wear particle emissions~\cite[Sec.~6.1]{Fussell2022}.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Power-electronic and electronic components.}
Beyond the battery, the traction chain contains three electrical sub-families, and they fit the framework to different degrees.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Power semiconductors.}
    Junction-temperature-swing fatigue governs the \emph{power semiconductors} of the inverter and of the DC--DC and on-board-charger \emph{converters}.
    They fit well into the remaining-life family (treated in detail below).
  \item \textbf{Converter capacitors.}
    The \emph{DC-link capacitors} of the converters stand out among the components that fail in the field~\cite[Sec.~I]{WangBlaabjerg2014}.
    \begin{itemize}[nosep]
      \item \textbf{Mechanisms.} Small electrolytic capacitors wear out mainly by vaporisation of the electrolyte, and large ones mainly by an electrochemical reaction of the oxide layer~\cite[Sec.~III-A]{WangBlaabjerg2014}.
        Film capacitors lose capacitance through self-healing breakdowns of the film and through corrosion by moisture~\cite[Sec.~III-A, Table~II]{WangBlaabjerg2014}.
        When the ripple current is high, as in electric vehicles, film capacitors can give a cheaper and smaller DC link~\cite[Sec.~II]{WangBlaabjerg2014}.
      \item \textbf{End of life.} In both types the capacitance falls or the equivalent series resistance (ESR) rises until an end-of-life limit, for example a 20\% loss of capacitance for an electrolytic capacitor~\cite[Table~III]{WangBlaabjerg2014}.
      \item \textbf{Lifetime model.} The standard model is an Arrhenius law in the temperature and a power law in the voltage~\cite[Eqs.~(1)--(2)]{WangBlaabjerg2014}.
        The ripple current is not a variable of this model: it dissipates power in the ESR and so raises the hot-spot temperature~\cite[Table~I, Secs.~IV-B and~V, Fig.~10]{WangBlaabjerg2014}.
        Humidity is a further critical stressor of film capacitors~\cite[Table~II]{WangBlaabjerg2014}.
      \item \textbf{Mission.} The mission acts through the load of the converter, which sets the ripple current and raises the local ambient temperature of the capacitor~\cite[Sec.~IV, Fig.~7]{WangBlaabjerg2021}.
      \item \textbf{Fit to the framework.} Wear-out predictions under changing stress usually add the damage linearly, by the Palmgren--Miner rule~\cite[Sec.~III-D]{WangBlaabjerg2021}.
        The accuracy of this linear rule is still an open question~\cite[Sec.~IV-C]{WangBlaabjerg2021}.
        So capacitor wear-out is a monotone accumulation driven by usage, and it fits the framework.
    \end{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Printed circuit boards.}
    The \emph{control and power printed circuit boards} (gate-driver, battery-management and converter boards) are the borderline case.
    \begin{itemize}[nosep]
      \item \textbf{Wear-out.} Temperature cycles, such as power on/off cycles or day--night cycles, fatigue the solder joints and the plated through-holes of a board~\cite[Table~1]{Wileman2021}.
        Vibration also fatigues the solder joints~\cite[Table~1]{Wileman2021}.
        With each cycle the damage accumulates, until a crack grows and the joint fails~\cite[Table~1]{Wileman2021}.
        The power on/off cycles and the vibration depend on usage, so the boards satisfy~(C1).
        Field data from photovoltaic and wind-turbine converters rank control boards high among the failure sources~\cite[Sec.~II-A]{WangBlaabjerg2021}, so the boards also satisfy~(C2).
      \item \textbf{Sudden failures.} Not every failure of a board comes from accumulated damage.
        Sudden failures come from design defects, manufacturing issues, single-event effects, overstress or misuse~\cite[Sec.~II]{WangBlaabjerg2021}.
        A mechanical shock, for example, loads the solder joints suddenly~\cite[Table~1]{Wileman2021}.
        For the board failures seen in the field, the causes and the dominant stressors are still open~\cite[Sec.~II-A]{WangBlaabjerg2021}.
        The damage-accumulation dynamics of Section~\ref{sec:overview} do not fully capture this mix of wear-out and sudden failures.
      \item \textbf{Faithful model.} It would need either a compound-Poisson shock term (Section~\ref{sec:stoch_orders}) or a formulation in the failure intensity instead of the damage formulation of Equation~\eqref{eq:dyn_full}.
    \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}
We list PCBs here, with this caveat, so that the limitation is recorded and not hidden by the choice of model.

\paragraph{Modelling shortlist.}
For a first concrete instance of the abstract framework, the most informative components meet two conditions.
They are safety- or cost-critical, \emph{and} mature, monotone degradation models describe them and fit the closure and ordering analysis of Section~\ref{sec:stoch_orders}.
These components are the \emph{traction battery pack}, the \emph{tires}, the \emph{brake friction elements}, the \emph{wheel bearings}, the \emph{traction-motor insulation} and the \emph{inverter and converter power modules}.

Each of them maps onto a single damage variable $D_{i\ell k}$ per component.
The operational driver in the third column of Table~\ref{tab:ev_components} governs its mission-dependent increment $\Delta D^{(j)}_{i\ell k}$.
For example, a long highway mission produces large increments of tire wear and of deep-discharge battery damage.
A stop-intensive urban mission instead multiplies the braking events and the charge--discharge micro-cycles.
This dependence on the mission is exactly the coupling that the scheduling problem of Section~\ref{sec:overview} exploits.

\paragraph{Worked example: traction-battery capacity fade.}
The battery pack is the most expensive component of an electric vehicle and the focus of its predictive maintenance~\cite[Secs.~2,~4.2]{Ibrahim2025}.
So it serves as the reference instance of the abstract increment $\Delta D^{(j)}_{i\ell k}$.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Damage and threshold.}
    Its damage is the relative capacity fade (one minus the state of health), and the failure threshold~$\tau$ is the largest admissible fade (the end-of-life criterion).
  \item \textbf{Mechanisms.}
    Capacity is lost through three families of side reactions: growth of the SEI (solid electrolyte interphase) film, lithium plating and gas generation.
    Their contributions split into loss of lithium inventory and loss of active material~\cite[Secs.~2.1,~4.1, Eqs.~28--29]{Lv2025}.
  \item \textbf{Semi-empirical models.}
    At the fleet level, semi-empirical models abstract this process and split the fade into a \emph{calendar} term and a \emph{cycling} term.
    \begin{itemize}[nosep]
      \item The calendar term grows with an Arrhenius factor $a\exp(-E_a/RT)$ in temperature and with a power law in time and state of charge.
      \item The cycling term grows with the number of cycles, modulated by stress factors for temperature, C-rate and depth of discharge~\cite[Eqs.~1--3]{Hosen2021}.
    \end{itemize}
    An even simpler engineering abstraction links the end of life directly to the accumulated charge throughput~\cite[Sec.~4.2]{Ibrahim2025}.
  \item \textbf{Cycling term.}
    For our framework the decisive structure is the following.
    \begin{itemize}[nosep]
      \item The cycling fade is monotone and adds up over days.
      \item The daily increment is a function of the energy served on the mission, of its depth of discharge and of its temperature profile.
        The energy includes the recharge that restores it, so fast charging (a high C-rate) makes the same mission more damaging.
      \item So the cycling fade is a monotone, mission-dependent accumulation process; the Gamma and inverse Gaussian processes of Section~\ref{sec:stoch_orders} are two models of this kind.
      \item The time-varying increment parameters that the formulation already allows ($\Delta D^{(j)}_{i\ell k}$ may depend on~$k$) absorb the non-stationary (power-law) dependence on time within the horizon.
        Beyond the horizon the endless schedule repeats the increment laws of the operating phase with period~$H_2$ (Section~\ref{sec:damage_dynamics}), so a dependence on time that does not repeat is not represented there.
    \end{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Calendar term.}
    The calendar term, by contrast, accrues also on idle days and depot days.
    So the dynamics of Section~\ref{sec:overview}, which leave the state unchanged on such days, do \emph{not} capture it.
    Over short horizons the calendar term can be neglected.
    Over long horizons the dynamics~\eqref{eq:dyn_full} must be extended with a small drift that does not depend on the activity and is added to every case, idle days included.
  \item \textbf{Maintenance.}
    Maintenance also takes a specific form.
    Replacing a subset of battery modules removes a corresponding fraction of the damage of the pack.
    This matches the mean map of the $\text{ARD}_\infty$ operator~$\mathcal{R}$, with $\rho$ the fraction of modules renewed, but not its action on the damage path.
    With independent modules the variance falls by the factor $1-\rho$, not $(1-\rho)^2$, so a model that tracks the variance cannot use the variance map of Section~\ref{sec:repair_connection} for this action.
    A full replacement of the pack is the AGAN action~$x^r$.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Degradation models for the shortlisted components}
\label{sec:ev_deg_models}

The other shortlisted components degrade through the same two structural mechanisms as the battery.
The first is a monotone \emph{wear or accumulation} driven by usage, and the second is the \emph{consumption of fatigue life} under cyclic loads.
So these components map onto the same two model families of Section~\ref{sec:deg_families}.
Table~\ref{tab:ev_deg_models} summarises, for each component, the governing model, the operational quantity that drives the per-mission increment, and the abstract family that the model instantiates.

``Accumulation'' denotes a monotone damage-accumulation process (Gamma / inverse-Gaussian / compound-Poisson); ``remaining-life'' denotes a cycles-to-failure model accumulated by the Palmgren--Miner rule.
SOC: state of charge; LAM: loss of active material; DOD: depth of discharge.}
\label{tab:ev_deg_models}

  & Power throughput, thermal cycles
  & Remaining-life (Miner) \\

\paragraph{Tires.}
The frictional work spent at the tire--road contact patch governs the loss of tread.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Model.}
    The classical theory of Schallamach and Turner assumes that abrasion is proportional to the energy dissipated by friction~\cite[Eq.~7]{SchallamachTurner1960}.
    It derives that, at small slip, wear grows approximately with the \emph{square} of the slip~\cite[Eq.~19]{SchallamachTurner1960}.
    So the wear rate rises steeply with the tangential forces of traction, braking and cornering.
  \item \textbf{Factors.}
    The main factors are the tire characteristics (compound, construction), the road-surface characteristics and the vehicle operation~\cite[Sec.~2.1.2]{Fussell2022}.
    The higher kerb mass (the mass of the empty vehicle) of electric vehicles raises these contact forces.
    The effect is large: on motorways, the brake-wear savings of regenerative braking do not compensate the added tire-wear particle emissions~\cite[Sec.~6.1]{Fussell2022}.
  \item \textbf{Fit to the framework.}
    Tread depth decreases monotonically from its new value to the legal limit.
    The damage is the tread depth lost, and the threshold~$\tau$ is the new depth minus the legal limit.
    The per-mission increment depends on distance, load, road surface and the aggressiveness of the driving.
  \item \textbf{Renewal.}
    For bus fleets, retreading (renewing the tread of a used tire) acts as a near-AGAN replacement of the wear state.
    Tire rotation only redistributes wear across the positions on the vehicle, and it stays outside the per-component model.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Brake friction elements.}
Friction braking wears the brake pad and the brake disc.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Model.}
    A physics-based route applies Archard's law on a fractal, elastoplastic contact~\cite[Eqs.~11,~14]{Sha2022}.
    Sha et al.\ apply it to the brake disc, not to the pad.
    The model gives the average wear depth $h_{\mathrm{wear}}$ of the disc in one braking~\cite[Eq.~21]{Sha2022}.
    The life of the disc, as a number of brakings, is then
    \begin{equation}
      N = H_0/(S_0\,h_{\mathrm{wear}}),
    \end{equation}
    with $H_0$ the maximum admissible average wear depth and $S_0$ a dimensionless wear safety factor for frictional heat and other external effects~\cite[Eq.~22]{Sha2022}.
  \item \textbf{Regenerative braking.}
    In an electric vehicle the regenerative brake absorbs most service braking, and it cuts friction-brake wear by an estimated 60--90\%~\cite[Sec.~6.1]{Fussell2022}.
    The remaining increments concentrate in the events where regeneration is unavailable or insufficient: a battery near full charge, a very low temperature, an emergency stop.
  \item \textbf{Fit to the framework.}
    The cumulative wear of a friction element remains a sum of non-negative, mission-dependent increments, one per friction-braking event.
    So it is a monotone accumulation process, whose rate now also depends on the energy-management strategy.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Wheel bearings.}
Bearing degradation has two stages: a long \emph{initiation} phase of rolling-contact fatigue, and then a rapid \emph{spall propagation} once a surface defect forms (a spall is a flake of material that breaks off the surface).
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Model.}
    Ohana et al.~\cite[Sec.~2.1.3]{Ohana2023} model the propagation phase with a formulation from continuum damage mechanics.
    In it, a scalar damage variable accumulates per over-rolling cycle,
    \begin{equation}
      \mathrm{d}D/\mathrm{d}N = \bigl(\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}/[\sigma_r(1-D)]\bigr)^m.
    \end{equation}
    The growth is driven by the tensile residual stresses that repeated impacts of the rolling elements generate at the edge of the spall.
    The model is meant as the basis of a physics-based prognostic tool that estimates the remaining useful life once a critical spall size is reached.
  \item \textbf{Fit to the framework.}
    The underlying driver is the number of load cycles, and hence the running distance and the speed.
    So the bearing damage is a monotone accumulation process.
    A Gamma or inverse Gaussian process (initiation) that feeds a remaining-life threshold (propagation) represents it well.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Traction-motor insulation.}
The winding insulation is the main element that limits the life of the traction motor, and its dominant ageing mechanism is thermal.
A high winding temperature accelerates the chemical degradation of the dielectric.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Model.}
    Dakin recognised thermal ageing as a chemical reaction whose rate constant follows the Arrhenius exponential law $K = A\,e^{-B/T}$ in absolute temperature~\cite[Eq.~5]{Dakin1948}.
    This gives the thermal-endurance life $L(T) = a\,\exp(b/T)$~\cite[Eq.~1]{Zhang2025insul}.
  \item \textbf{Montsinger's rule.}
    The earlier empirical rule of Montsinger states that the ageing rate roughly doubles for each 8\,$^\circ$C rise of temperature~\cite[Synopsis, p.~776]{Montsinger1930}.
    This rule halves the life for each fixed rise, and Dakin showed that it is the constant-coefficient approximation of the Arrhenius law, accurate at one temperature only~\cite[Fig.~1 and discussion of Eq.~5]{Dakin1948}.
  \item \textbf{Fit to the framework.}
    Under a temperature profile that varies with the mission, the fraction of life consumed in an interval $\mathrm{d}t$ at temperature $T_i(t)$ is $\mathrm{d}t/L[T_i(t)]$.
    The total consumed fraction is the integral of this loss rate over the profile.
    This is a cumulative-damage (Palmgren--Miner) extension of the Arrhenius model~\cite[Eq.~2 and Fig.~10]{Zhang2025insul}.
    So the abstract description coincides with the remaining-life model of Section~\ref{sec:remaining_life}, with the mission driving the thermal load (current, cooling, ambient conditions).
  \item \textbf{Stochastic processes.}
    The same review also surveys Wiener- and Gamma-process formulations of insulation degradation~\cite[Sec.~5]{Zhang2025insul}, which match directly the stochastic families of Section~\ref{sec:stoch_orders}.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Inverter power modules.}
The IGBT modules of the traction inverter fail mainly by thermo-mechanical fatigue: lift-off of the aluminium bond wires and cracking of the solder layer~\cite[Sec.~1]{Alavi2024}.
The mismatch of the coefficients of thermal expansion under repeated junction-temperature swings $\Delta T_j$ drives this fatigue~\cite[Secs.~1 and~4]{Alavi2024}.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Model.}
    The classical model of the number of cycles to failure is a Coffin--Manson power law~\cite[Eq.~3]{Alavi2024}:
    \begin{equation}
      N_f = N_0\,(\Delta T_j)^{-n}.
    \end{equation}
    An Arrhenius factor in the mean junction temperature $T_{jm}$ refines it~\cite[Eq.~4]{Alavi2024}:
    \begin{equation}
      N_f = A\,(\Delta T_j)^{-n}\exp\!\bigl(E_a/(k_b T_{jm})\bigr).
    \end{equation}
    Here $A$, $n > 0$ and the activation energy $E_a > 0$ are fitted parameters, and $k_b$ is the Boltzmann constant.
    Alavi et al.\ write the exponent as $\alpha = -n$ and report $\alpha = -5.039$~\cite[Sec.~4]{Alavi2024}.
    The CIPS08 model adds further factors.
    Bayerer et al.\ published it at the 5th International Conference on Integrated Power Electronics Systems (CIPS 2008)~\cite[Eq.~(1)]{Bayerer2008}:
    \begin{equation}
      N_f = K\,\Delta T_j^{\beta_1} e^{\beta_2/(T_j+273)}\, t_{\mathrm{on}}^{\beta_3}\, I^{\beta_4}\, V^{\beta_5}\, d_{\mathrm{w}}^{\beta_6}.
    \end{equation}
    The factors are the temperature swing~$\Delta T_j$, the junction temperature~$T_j$ in $^\circ$C, the power-on time~$t_{\mathrm{on}}$, the current~$I$ per bond foot on the chip, the blocking voltage~$V$ of the chip and the bond-wire diameter~$d_{\mathrm{w}}$~\cite[Secs.~2.2 and~3.1, Fig.~2]{Bayerer2008}.
    Alavi et al.\ enter $V$ in volts divided by~100~\cite[Sec.~4]{Alavi2024}.
    Here $K$ is a scale factor, and $\beta_1,\dots,\beta_6$ are exponents fitted by multiple linear regression on the logarithm of the model~\cite[Sec.~3.2]{Bayerer2008}.
    Bayerer et al.\ give two sets of exponents $\beta = (\beta_1, \ldots, \beta_6)$~\cite[Eqs.~(5)--(6)]{Bayerer2008}:
    \begin{itemize}[nosep]
      \item with $T_j$ the maximum junction temperature of the cycle,
        \begin{equation}
          \beta = (-3.483,\ 1917,\ -0.438,\ -0.717,\ -0.751,\ -0.564);
        \end{equation}
      \item with $T_j$ the minimum (starting) junction temperature, which removes a correlation between the variables~\cite[Sec.~3.4]{Bayerer2008},
        \begin{equation}
          \beta = (-4.416,\ 1285,\ -0.463,\ -0.716,\ -0.761,\ -0.5).
        \end{equation}
    \end{itemize}
    In both sets every exponent is negative except $\beta_2 > 0$.
    The authors note that the relation of the voltage factor to the chip thickness does not apply to modules for traction applications, whose setup and bonding differ~\cite[Sec.~3.1]{Bayerer2008}.
  \item \textbf{Calibration.}
    The model is empirical: it is limited to the range of the test data and cannot be used for extrapolation~\cite[Secs.~3.1 and~3.4]{Bayerer2008}.
    Its predictions, moved from accelerated tests to real use, can be inaccurate~\cite[Sec.~4]{Alavi2024}.
    Its coefficients depend on the device type, mainly $\beta_1$ and $\beta_2$, so a new device type needs a new calibration~\cite[Secs.~1 and~4]{Alavi2024}.
    A recent fit on 27 power-cycling tests of one discrete IGBT moved the estimates of $(\beta_1,\beta_2,\beta_3)$ to $(-2.910,\ 1083.714,\ -4.521)$~\cite[Sec.~5.2, Table~2]{Alavi2024}.
    Its 95\% confidence bounds contain the first three exponents of both sets of Bayerer et al.~\cite[Table~2]{Alavi2024}.
    All these tests used one power-on time, $t_{\mathrm{on}} = 2$\,s, so they cannot determine~$\beta_3$.
    For a photovoltaic inverter, the same study also modified a second empirical model, SKiM63, by a factor that depends on~$\Delta T_j$.
    This change cut the predicted B10 lifetime (the time at which 10\% of the units have failed) from 43.79 to 13.56~years~\cite[Sec.~5.3 and Fig.~15]{Alavi2024}.
  \item \textbf{Fit to the framework.}
    Under a realistic mix of missions, the thermal cycles have different amplitudes.
    The Palmgren--Miner rule sums the accumulated damage,
    \begin{equation}
      D_{\mathrm{PM}} = \sum_i n_i/N_{f,i},
    \end{equation}
    with failure at $D_{\mathrm{PM}} = 1$.
    This places the power module clearly in the remaining-life family of Section~\ref{sec:remaining_life}, with the mission determining the transmitted power and hence the spectrum of temperature swings.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Common structure.}
Every shortlisted component reduces to one of the two abstract descriptions of Section~\ref{sec:deg_families}:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item a \emph{monotone accumulation process} (battery cycling fade, tires, brakes, bearings; for bearings, the propagation stage ends at a remaining-life threshold);
  \item a \emph{remaining-life model accumulated by the Palmgren--Miner rule} (insulation, power modules).
\end{itemize}
In both, an operational driver that the schedule controls sets the per-mission damage increment $\Delta D^{(j)}_{i\ell k}$.
The drivers are energy throughput and charging rate, distance and load, braking profile, or thermal cycling.
This is exactly the structure that the abstract formulation of Section~\ref{sec:overview} assumes.
So the electric-vehicle fleet is a faithful concrete instance of the general problem.
This analysis also fixes which degradation family describes each component of an electric-vehicle fleet.

\paragraph{Excluded parts.}
Some components deteriorate almost independently of the mission served: body corrosion, interior fittings, static low-voltage electronics and the 12-volt auxiliary battery.
They satisfy criterion~(C2) but not~(C1).
Time-based overhaul outside the optimisation suits them best, so the model does not track their damage.
Their fixed maintenance windows can still enter the model as exogenous availability constraints.
