The calendar ageing of the traction battery is the one borderline case.
The drift extension discussed in the worked example above handles it.

\section{Optimization problem for a fleet of electric vehicles}
\label{sec:evs}

Table~\ref{tab:notation_evs} lists the additional symbols that appear when the abstract formulation of Section~\ref{sec:overview} is specialised to a concrete fleet of electric vehicles.
All symbols of Table~\ref{tab:notation} remain valid.
The entries below either instantiate abstract quantities or add new ones.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Additional notation for the electric-vehicle fleet specialisation (extends Table~\ref{tab:notation}).}
\label{tab:notation_evs}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lp{10.5cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Symbol} & \textbf{Description} \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\textit{Cost parameters}} \\
$C_M$                        & Cost of a depot day, paid once per member and depot day (scalar) \\
$C_D$                        & Damage-penalty coefficient (scalar) \\
$C_{R,i\ell}$                & Cost of imperfect repair per unit of expected damage removed \\
$C_{\mathrm{rep},i\ell}$     & Cost of a replacement of component $\ell$ of member $i$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection{Cost function}

With the cost parameters of Table~\ref{tab:notation_evs}, the abstract objective of Section~\ref{sec:overview} takes the form:
\begin{align}\label{eq:objective_evs}
\min \quad
  & C_M \sum_{k=1}^{T} \sum_{i=1}^{F} x_{i0k}
  \;+\; \sum_{k=1}^{T} \sum_{i=1}^{F} \sum_{\ell=1}^{L} C_{R,i\ell}\, z_{i\ell k}
  \;+\; \sum_{k=1}^{T} \sum_{i=1}^{F} \sum_{\ell=1}^{L} C_{\mathrm{rep},i\ell}\, x^r_{i\ell k} \notag \\
  & +\; C_D\, u.
\end{align}
The first three terms are weighted sums over the planning horizon.
The first term counts depot days, and the second grows with the damage that each repair removes.
The third term counts replacements, and the last one penalises the largest aggregate expected damage through~$u$ (see~\eqref{eq:u_def}).

\section{Implementation details}

The formulation of this document is implemented in the Python package \texttt{fleet\_management}.
The package builds the mixed-integer program of Section~\ref{sec:overview} for a given fleet and solves it with the commercial solver Gurobi.

The package covers the five degradation models of Table~\ref{tab:loop_per_model}, both repair laws, the five Route-I bounds and the three forms of the reliability rows.
It also offers tools around the solve: sweeps over the horizon lengths, warm starts, plots of a schedule, and a check of a result against its input.

The software specification \texttt{spec/spec.tex} is the reference for the implementation.
It states the formulation in full, with every row, the input and output files and the tools.
The code follows it.
Where this document and the specification differ in a detail, the specification holds.

The subsections below give the reasons behind the implementation choices of the specification.

\subsection{Big-M and indicator forms of the one-sided rows}
\label{sec:impl_encoding}

The specification writes each one-sided row of Section~\ref{sec:encoding} in one of two forms, chosen per component.
In the \emph{big-M form}, each rule of a tracked statistic~$s$ holds unless a binary switches it off through a constant~$M_s$.
The rows are the following:
\begin{align}
  s_k &\ge s_{k-1} + \delta s_k - M_s\,(x^m_k + x^r_k) && \text{(the carry row)}, \\
  s_k &\ge a\,s_{k-1} + (1-a)\,g_{k-1} - M_s\,(1 - x^m_k) && \text{(the repair row)}, \\
  |s_k - s^{\mathrm{new}}| &\le M_s\,(1 - x^r_k) && \text{(the replacement rows)}, \\
  |g_k - s_k| &\le M_s\,(1 - x^m_k - x^r_k) && \text{(the anchor rows)}, \\
  |g_k - g_{k-1}| &\le M_s\,(x^m_k + x^r_k) && \text{(the anchor rows)}.
\end{align}
In the \emph{indicator form}, the same inequalities are Gurobi indicator constraints, switched on by the binary of the day's case.
At binary points the two forms have the same feasible set.

The constant $M_s$ is the \emph{natural range} of~$s$: an upper bound of~$s$ at every point that satisfies the reliability row of the component (for the mean at a Chernoff cell, at every point of the reference trajectory).
For a variance, the Hoeffding budget and the Chernoff statistic, the value below is the largest value that the exact row admits at $\mu \ge 0$.
For a mean, $\tau$ is an upper bound: the row itself, its side row or a smaller cap bounds the mean by~$\tau$.
The Chernoff row does not contain the mean; there $\mu \le K/s < \tau$ holds on the reference trajectory, because the input checks keep $K \ge s\mu$ (Jensen's inequality).
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item $\tau$ for a mean and for its anchor.
  \item $V_{\max} = \tau^2/\kappa_G^2$ for a Gaussian or Wiener variance (Remark~\ref{rem:vmax}).
  \item $\varepsilon\tau^2/(1-\varepsilon)$ for a Cantelli variance.
  \item $\tau(\tau - 2c)/(2\kappa)$ for a Bernstein variance.
  \item $2\tau^2/\kappa$ for the Hoeffding budget.
  \item $s\tau - \kappa$ for the Chernoff statistic.
\end{itemize}
An anchor has the range of its statistic.

\paragraph{Validity of the big-M constants.}
Every tracked statistic and every anchor lies in $[0, M_s]$.
The initial values, the starting anchors and the replacement values lie in $[0, M_s]$ too, by the input checks (Section~\ref{sec:check_reasons}).
A relaxed row then cuts no point.
The derivation is in Appendix~\ref{drv:bigM_validity}, page~\pageref{drv:bigM_validity}.

\paragraph{Choice of the default form.}
The big-M form is the default.
Gurobi accepts a binary as integral within its integrality tolerance \texttt{IntFeasTol} (default $10^{-5}$).
A big-M row can therefore leak by at most $2 \cdot 10^{-5} M_s$, because the carry row and the anchor rows contain the sum $x^m_k + x^r_k$ of two binaries.
Because $M_s$ is the range of the statistic itself, this is a relative error of at most $2 \cdot 10^{-5}$ per row. The leaks of the carry rows of successive days add up, to at most $2 \cdot 10^{-5}\,T$ over the horizon.
Indicator constraints have no such leak, and Gurobi usually needs more time to solve them.
Which form solves this model faster has not been measured.

\subsection{Reasons for the input checks}
\label{sec:check_reasons}

The specification validates the input before it builds the model, and a violation raises an error that names the key and the component.
A check of one model runs only at the components of that model.
Each item below gives a rule and, after the dash, its reason.

\paragraph{Keys, shapes and entries.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item Every key is known, and every required key is present. --- A misspelled or missing key must not change the problem without a message.
  \item Each value has one of the shapes of its key. --- Silent broadcasting by the array library (the automatic extension of an array to a larger shape) could read a value for another component.
  \item At a component where a key applies, every entry is a finite number, or a string for a selector. --- A missing value at a component must not take a default silently.
  \item At the three keys without a default (the optional variance cap and the rates of the initial and the replacement damage), \texttt{null} at a component means that the key is not given there. --- These keys can be given at some components of a model and not at others, and an array needs an entry at every component; for example, the optional variance cap at one Gaussian component only.
  \item An unknown value of a selector is an error at every component, also where the reliability row is linear and the form is ignored. --- Reading an unknown value as \texttt{exact} would turn a linear model into a nonconvex one without a message.
  \item Selector values are case sensitive, like key names. --- Older inputs name the law $\text{ARD}_\infty$ with the value \texttt{ARD1} (Section~\ref{sec:spec_changelog}); a comparison that ignores the case would read it as \texttt{ard1}, the other law, without a message.
  \item An integer key accepts a number with zero fractional part, such as $3.0$, and refuses any other number, such as $2.5$, and a flag. --- Numerical tools often write integers as floating-point numbers, above all in HDF5 files (a binary file format for numerical data); a flag is not a number, although Python reads it as the integer $0$ or~$1$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Sizes and selectors.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item $F > M$. --- Every mission needs a member on every day; with $F = M$ no member could be in the depot, and no repair could take place.
  \item $H_1, H_2 \ge 1$. --- Each phase has at least one day.
  \item With a positive $C_D$ and the quadratic penalty, every quadratic reliability row is in its exact form. --- The quadratic penalty is a quadratic row, and the linear forms exist to give a linear model (Section~\ref{sec:cost_function}).
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Reliability, repair and initial state.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item $\tau > 0$. --- The threshold is a positive degradation level.
  \item $\varepsilon \in (0, 1)$, and $\varepsilon < 0.5$ at Gaussian and Wiener components. --- A probability lies in $(0,1)$; the normal quantile $\kappa_G = \Phi^{-1}(1-\varepsilon)$ of the Gaussian and Wiener rows is positive only below~$0.5$; at $\varepsilon \ge 0.5$ the squared row does not describe the requirement (Remark~\ref{rem:vmax}).
  \item $\rho \in (0, 1]$. --- A repair removes a positive fraction of the level, at most all of it.
  \item The initial mean satisfies
    \begin{equation}
      0 \le \mu_0 < \tau.
    \end{equation}
    --- A component at its threshold has failed already.
  \item Initial and replacement variances, supports and budgets are non-negative. --- They describe a variance, a width or a squared width.
  \item The relative tangent point lies in $[0, 1)$, and the number of \texttt{pwl} segments is at least~$1$. --- At the point~$\tau$ the tangent row reduces to $\chi \le 0$ (Gaussian, Wiener, Cantelli, Hoeffding) or admits no point (Bernstein with $b > 0$), and a value outside $[0,1]$ has no tangent point in $[0, \tau]$.
  \item Each starting anchor lies between $0$ and the initial value of its statistic. --- Each statistic is non-decreasing between interventions (fact~(M1) of Section~\ref{sec:encoding}), so its level after the last intervention cannot exceed its initial level.
  \item With the shortcut for a new part, every replacement value is~$0$. --- The shortcut means a new part, and a non-zero value would be discarded without a message.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Natural ranges and replacement states.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item Every initial value, starting anchor and replacement value lies in the natural range of its statistic (Section~\ref{sec:impl_encoding}). --- The big-M rows take the natural range as their constant, and this constant is valid only for values in the range (Appendix~\ref{drv:bigM_validity}, page~\pageref{drv:bigM_validity}): with a value outside it, a switched-off row can cut feasible points.
  \item When replacements are allowed, the replacement state satisfies every row that holds on a replacement day, in the form built at the component (for \texttt{pwl}, the cap of the segment that contains the replacement mean), its side row, and the optional variance cap. --- The rows hold on the day of a replacement, so a replacement state that violates one of them makes every replacement infeasible (Section~\ref{sec:damage_dynamics}).
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Increments.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item The mean of an increment satisfies
    \begin{equation}
      0 \le \mu^{\mathrm{inc}} < \tau
    \end{equation}
    for every mission and day. --- A mission with mean $\tau$ or more fails the component in one day; the mean~$0$ is allowed, so that a mission that does not wear a component can be written as~$0$.
  \item Where the mean of an increment is~$0$, its variance, support and cumulant generating function are~$0$. --- A non-negative increment with mean~$0$ is $0$ almost surely, so it has no variance, no range and a zero cumulant generating function.
  \item Variances, supports and cumulant generating functions of increments are non-negative, and a transitory profile comes with its operating profile. --- A variance and a width are not negative, and the lookup of the increment laws needs the operating laws after the transitory phase (Section~\ref{sec:damage_dynamics}).
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Model-specific checks.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item Gaussian: $\mu \ge 3\sqrt{v}$ for the initial damage, for every increment and, when replacements are allowed, for the replacement draw. --- A Gaussian term is then negative with probability at most about $0.13\,\%$, so the damage stays non-negative in practice.
  \item Inverse Gaussian: $\eta > 0$. --- The squared coefficient of variation of the inverse Gaussian terms must be positive.
  \item Wiener: $\sigma > 0$ and $v_0 \ge 0$. --- The diffusion constant is positive; a known initial damage has variance~$0$ and is a valid input.
  \item Gamma: every given rate is finite and positive. --- The rate of a Gamma law is positive, and the smallest rate sets the cap (Section~\ref{sec:stoch_orders}); an omitted rate of the initial or replacement damage means a known damage, which has no Gamma law and no rate.
  \item Optional variance cap: positive, and at least the initial variance and, when replacements are allowed, the replacement variance. --- A cap below the replacement variance makes every replacement infeasible, and a cap below the initial variance forces an intervention on day~1.
  \item Bernstein: every variance is at most a quarter of the squared width of its range, for increments, the initial damage and the replacement draw. --- Popoviciu's inequality (Section~\ref{sec:cons_surrogates}): a law on a range of width~$w$ has variance at most $w^2/4$, so a larger variance contradicts the width.
  \item Bernstein: $\tau > 2\kappa\, b_\ell/3$. --- Otherwise the certificate admits no positive variance: it is infeasible at zero damage, and at equality only the point
    \begin{equation}
      \mu = v = 0
    \end{equation}
    survives, so that the big-M rows break.
  \item Bernstein with a linear form: the tangent point, or the midpoint $\tau/(2P)$ of the first \texttt{pwl} segment, lies below $\sqrt{\tau(\tau - 2c)}$. --- Otherwise no point with $v > 0$ satisfies the row (Section~\ref{sec:linear_forms}).
  \item Chernoff: $s > \ln(1/\varepsilon)/\tau$. --- The statistic $K$ is not negative, so the row $K - s\tau \le \ln\varepsilon$ needs $s\tau \ge \ln(1/\varepsilon)$; with a smaller~$s$ every schedule is infeasible, and at equality only a component without damage is feasible.
  \item Chernoff: the cumulant generating function of every increment, the initial value, the starting anchor under $\text{ARD}_1$ and, when replacements are allowed, the replacement value are at least $s$ times the corresponding mean. --- Jensen's inequality (Section~\ref{sec:cons_surrogates}); the two starting anchors describe the same damage after the last intervention, and the defaults satisfy the inequality.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Run options and costs.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item The evaluation horizon, when given, exceeds~$H_1$. --- The projected cost needs at least one operating day beyond the transitory phase; for a smaller horizon the operating cost would have a negative weight (Section~\ref{sec:cost_function}).
  \item Numerical run options lie in their ranges, and flags are flags. --- A value outside its range must raise an error and must not be changed silently.
  \item All costs are finite and non-negative. --- A negative cost would reward a depot day, a repair, a replacement or damage.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Tolerance of the checks.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item A non-strict inequality $a \le b$ passes when it holds up to $10^{-12}\max(1, |a|, |b|)$. --- The rounding of floating-point numbers can break an exact comparison on valid data.
    For example, with $s = 5$ and $\mu_0 = 0.14$, the Chernoff initial value $K_0 = 0.7$ fails $K_0 \ge s\mu_0$ without a tolerance, because $5 \cdot 0.14 = 0.7000000000000001$ in floating point.
    The rounding error grows with the size of the numbers, so the tolerance is relative; the term~$1$ keeps the tolerance $10^{-12}$ near~$0$.
  \item A strict inequality has no tolerance. --- It excludes its boundary on purpose: for example, a component with $\mu_0 = \tau$ has failed already.
  \item A negative value of the run option \texttt{mip\_gap} (the relative gap at which Gurobi stops) that passes through the tolerance is stored as~$0$. --- Gurobi refuses a negative gap.
    Other values that pass through the tolerance reach the model unchanged, for example an initial variance of $-10^{-13}$; the feasibility tolerance of Gurobi, $10^{-6}$, absorbs them.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Outputs}
\label{sec:impl_outputs}

\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Solution and status.}
    The solution arrays are present whenever Gurobi has found at least one solution, and then the status tells how the solve ended.
    For example, a solution found before the time limit is reported with the status \texttt{time\_limit}.
    With the default MIP gap of $0.1$ (MIP means mixed-integer program), the status \texttt{optimal} means that the best solution exceeds the best bound by at most $10\,\%$ of its own value, that is, by at most about $11\,\%$ of the bound.
  \item \textbf{Tracked statistics.}
    The output carries every tracked statistic and anchor, so that a validator can check every loop row.
    The dynamics rows are one-sided, so these values, and the costs computed from them, are upper bounds, not exact values (Section~\ref{sec:encoding}).
  \item \textbf{Objective value.}
    When the variable~$u$ has no slack, the objective value of the solve equals the cost of the objective mode computed from the output.
    Otherwise it is larger by $C_D$ times that slack.
  \item \textbf{Result file.}
    The extension and the directory of the result file are checked before the model is solved, so that a long solve is not lost.
  \item \textbf{Numbers that are not finite.}
    YAML and JSON files (two plain-text data formats) write NaN (not a number), $+\infty$ and $-\infty$ as \texttt{null}.
    Standard JSON has no notation for these values, and the two text formats follow one rule.
    HDF5 files store floating-point numbers in binary form and keep these values.
  \item \textbf{Objective without a value.}
    HDF5 has no \texttt{null}, so an HDF5 file does not store the objective of a solve without a solution.
    Every reader treats a missing key as \texttt{null}, so the file carries the same information as a YAML or JSON file.
  \item \textbf{One reader.}
    One function, \texttt{read\_result}, reads a result file of every format for every tool: the schedule plot, the validators and the dashboards.
    These tools need the same reading rules, for example NaN at a \texttt{null} entry of an array, so the rules have one implementation.
    The function sits in the module of the writers, so the rules for writing and for reading a result file are in one place.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Horizon sweeps}
\label{sec:impl_sweeps}

A single solve uses one horizon pair $H = [H_1, H_2]$.
A \emph{horizon sweep} solves one scenario for several pairs and compares the solved cases.
It ranks the cases by the operating average
\begin{equation}
  \bar J_{\mathrm{op}} = J_{\mathrm{op}}/H_2,
\end{equation}
the cost per day of the repeating regime, which is comparable across values of~$H_2$ (Section~\ref{sec:cost_function}).
A sweep over both horizons with an evaluation horizon~$E$ ranks by the projected cost
\begin{equation}
  P_E = J_{\mathrm{init}} + \frac{E - H_1}{H_2}\,J_{\mathrm{op}},
\end{equation}
which is the cost of the transitory phase, then of the operating phase repeated until day~$E$.
This criterion also prices the transitory phase, so it compares different values of~$H_1$.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{What a proof certifies.} A case counts as proven when Gurobi reports \texttt{optimal} at the MIP gap requested for that case.
    Such a case is optimal within that gap, not exactly optimal, and the sweep report records the requested gap of each case.
    The status certifies the objective that the solve minimised: $\bar J_{\mathrm{op}} + C_D\,u$ or $P_E + C_D\,u$, while the ranking value is $\bar J_{\mathrm{op}}$ or $P_E$ alone.
    So the sweep ranks by cost and uses the damage penalty only inside each solve.
  \item \textbf{Early stop.} With the default threshold, a case qualifies for the early stop when Gurobi has met the gap requested for it.
    A case that does not qualify, for example one that stopped at the time limit above its requested gap, is recorded, but it cannot trigger a stop.
  \item \textbf{Parallel cases.} Each running solve uses one Gurobi licence token, so a licence with one token allows one case at a time.
    The parallel cases do not use each other's results.
    So the parallel option is refused together with warm starts between cases, which need the preceding results.
    It is also refused together with the early stop, which needs the preceding cases before it starts the following ones.
  \item \textbf{Report.} The report is a mapping of plain types, written by a safe writer of YAML (a plain-text data format), so it contains no arrays and no program objects.
    If a job ends before the run is complete, the checkpoint file is a valid report of the cases evaluated so far.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Warm starts}
\label{sec:impl_warm}

A warm start changes the time of a solve, not the model: it does not change the feasible set or the objective.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Day mapping.} A start taken from a previous result maps its days to the days of the new problem.
    \begin{itemize}[nosep]
      \item The transitory days keep their index.
      \item The operating days repeat the operating phase of the source cyclically, so a shorter operating phase can seed a longer one.
      \item When the new transitory phase is longer than the source's, its extra days all take the first operating day of the source.
    \end{itemize}
    When the source has the same sizes $F$, $M$, $L$, the mapped schedule copies whole days of the source, so it keeps the one-activity rows, the mission coverage and the depot-day rows of every day.
    It need not satisfy the dynamics or the loop rows of the new horizons.
  \item \textbf{Invalid source.} A source whose horizons are not integers of at least~$1$, or whose arrays do not have the days of these horizons on their last axis, raises an error.
    The day mapping needs the horizons of the source, and values taken from the new problem in their place would map the days wrongly without a message.
  \item \textbf{Start.} A start sets values of the binaries only.
    Gurobi tries to complete it by itself, with limited effort, and discards a start that it cannot make feasible.
    The greedy heuristic returns its start also when its loop pass counts failures, and it requests the completion of the specification.
    That completion can then end with the status \texttt{infeasible}, with an irreducible infeasible subset of rows.
  \item \textbf{Completion report.} In the mode \texttt{hint} the completion never runs, although the greedy heuristic requests it.
    The report then gives the status \texttt{not\_requested}, the status of every completion that does not run, so no further status is needed.
    Its flag \texttt{requested} still shows the request of the source.
  \item \textbf{Hints.} Gurobi uses hints to guide its heuristics and its branching during the whole solve.
    A hint needs no feasible point, so it also helps when the mapped schedule does not fit the horizons of the problem and Gurobi discards it as a start.
  \item \textbf{Relaxation start.} The relaxed solution need not be a feasible point of the original model.
    It gives integer values only, and the original model must find its own first solution.
  \item \textbf{Greedy heuristic.} The heuristic builds a schedule that tries to satisfy the rows of the model.
    It does not try to give a low cost.
    After its construction pass the missions are fixed, so the components are independent except through the depot days of a member.
    \begin{itemize}[nosep]
      \item \textbf{Rows in the excess.} The excess of a component counts the violations of the reliability row, of its side row and of the optional variance cap.
        The tracked values are at least the reference values (Proposition~\ref{prop:domination}), and each of these rows sets upper limits on them.
        So a schedule whose reference trajectory breaks one of these rows has no feasible completion.
      \item \textbf{Reference trajectory.} The excess is computed on the reference trajectory.
        This test is stricter than the one-sided rows, because the reference trajectory of a feasible schedule can violate the loop row (Section~\ref{sec:acceptance}).
        In a Gaussian test case the heuristic counts one failure, and the same schedule is feasible for the rows of the model.
      \item \textbf{Wiener components.} A free day is an operating day on which the member flies no mission and the component has no intervention.
        At a Wiener component the heuristic tries a repair and a replacement on every free day, and it keeps the intervention with the smaller excess.
        At the components of the other models it tries repairs first, and a replacement only when no repair lowers the excess.
        The Wiener variance falls only at a replacement (Section~\ref{sec:loop_replacements}), so a repair placed first can take the last free day of a component that needs a replacement.
        Example: a Wiener test case with $H = [2, 2]$.
        A repair on day~$4$ removes the excess of the mean and takes the only free day; the excess of the variance remains, and each of the two members counts one failure.
        A replacement on that day removes both excesses.
      \item \textbf{Report.} The report gives the largest ratio of a reference mean to its threshold on the final trajectory of each component.
        So the ratio describes the schedule that the heuristic returns.
    \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Plots}
\label{sec:impl_plots}

\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Readers.} The schedule plot reads its file with the reader of the result files (Section~\ref{sec:impl_outputs}), and the sweep plot reads its reports with the input reader.
    A file whose data are not a mapping raises the type error of the reader, so every way of reading a file has one error for this case.
  \item \textbf{Result without models.} A result file without the key of the models is plotted, and each row shows the model ``unknown''.
    Older result files lack this key, and every solve writes it.
  \item \textbf{Counts from the Gurobi log.} The summary panel of the schedule plot takes the presolved numbers of rows and variables from the last line ``Presolved'' of the Gurobi log.
    The completion of a warm start and the relaxation start solve their own copies of the model before the main solve, and they write to the same log.
    So the last such line belongs to the main solve.
  \item \textbf{Sweep without cases.} A sweep report whose list of cases is empty raises an error.
    There is nothing to plot, and a sweep writes its first report only after its first case.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Validation tools}
\label{sec:impl_validation}

\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Objective check.} The saved damage penalty~$u$ is the maximum of its rows.
    The solver objective contains the variable~$u$, which can lie above this maximum in a solution that is not optimal.
    So the check is one-sided: it tests that the reported objective is not below the cost of the reported solution, and it does not test optimality.
  \item \textbf{Loop checks.} They use the saved tracked states, which satisfy the loop rows, and not the reference trajectory: the reference trajectory of a feasible schedule can violate the loop row (Section~\ref{sec:acceptance}).
  \item \textbf{Scope of the structural validator.} It checks the binaries, the assignment and depot-day rows, the loop rows of every tracked statistic and anchor, and the objective.
    It does not check the dynamics rows or the reliability rows; the dashboards show the reliability rows as margins.
  \item \textbf{Fixed list of checks.} The report always lists the eight loop checks, with the violation~$0$ where the result holds no number for the statistic.
    So every report has the same $14$ checks, or $13$ for a result without the array of idle days.
  \item \textbf{Incomplete results.} A result with the schedule but without another solution array, the damage penalty or the objective raises an error.
    No solve writes such a result, and without the error the report would hold NaN, which the JSON writer refuses.
  \item \textbf{Margins in the dashboards.} A margin of at least the negative tolerance means that the row holds.
    The exact form is used also when the solve used a linear form, so the margin shows the true slack.
    At a row with a side row, the margin is the smaller of the two slacks, because the row holds if and only if both relations hold.
    The slack of the quadratic relation alone can be positive when the side row fails: a Gaussian component with $\mu = 1.2$, $\tau = 1$ and $v = 0$ has $(\tau - \mu)^2 = 0.04 > 0$.
    The two slacks have different units.
    The margins of components with different models and bounds are not comparable in size.
    Only their signs are comparable.
  \item \textbf{Model of the result.} The dashboards and the Gamma validators raise an error when the model of a component in the result differs from the input.
    The structural validator does not compare the models.
    The margins and the Gamma validators need the parameters that the input gives for the model of each component, and the structural validator needs no model.
  \item \textbf{Tolerance of the dashboards.} The tolerance is at least $10^{-12}$, because the Gamma validators that the dashboards run need a positive tolerance.
  \item \textbf{Start of the dashboards.} The entry script draws the page only when Python runs it as its main program.
    It imports Streamlit (a Python library for web dashboards) only inside the functions that draw.
    The command \texttt{streamlit run} executes the script as the main program, so the page is drawn.
    An import of the script, by the package or by a test, draws nothing and needs no Streamlit, which is an optional part of the installation.
  \item \textbf{Repair cost in the replay Gamma validator.} The check of the repair cost uses the saved means and anchors, not the reference values.
    This is the row of the model, and at binary points the saved difference between the mean and the anchor is at least the reference difference, under both repair laws.
    So the check on the saved values also implies the check on the reference values.
  \item \textbf{Exact Gamma validator.} It computes the tail $\Pr(D_k > \tau)$ of the sum of the terms with the Moschopoulos series.
    \begin{itemize}[nosep]
      \item \textbf{Mixture.} The series writes the law of a sum of independent Gamma variables with different rates as a mixture of Gamma laws with non-negative weights.
        When all rates are equal, the tail is the Gamma tail.
      \item \textbf{Certified bound.} The partial sum of the series is a lower bound of the tail, so adding the mixture weight that remains gives a certified upper bound.
      \item \textbf{Saved means.} The saved means may be larger than the exact ones, because they are upper bounds.
      \item \textbf{Initial damage under $\text{ARD}_1$.} The initial damage is split at the starting anchor into a part that no repair reduces and a part that the repairs reduce.
        Otherwise a repair could bring the damage below its level after the last intervention, which the law $\text{ARD}_1$ excludes.
      \item \textbf{Cost.} The number of terms of the series grows with the ratio of the largest to the smallest rate.
        Frequent repairs under $\text{ARD}_\infty$ make this ratio large, because each repair multiplies the rates of the repaired terms by $1/(1-\rho)$.
        Example: one term repaired $2$, $4$, $6$, $8$ and $10$ times at $\rho = 0.5$, beside a new term, needs $151$, $725$, about $3{,}000$, about $12{,}400$ and $49{,}884$ terms.
        One tail then takes about $2$\,s after $8$ repairs and about $31$\,s after~$10$.
        From $11$ repairs the series needs more than $100{,}000$ terms, the default limit, and the validator stops with an error.
      \item \textbf{Precision.} The series stops when the mixture weight that remains falls below the convolution tolerance, $10^{-12}$ by default.
        Its sums use the extended precision of NumPy, the numerical library of Python: $80$ bits on Linux, and the usual $64$ bits on Windows.
        With $80$ bits the series can reach a convolution tolerance near $10^{-19}$.
        With $64$ bits the remaining weight is understated by $2 \cdot 10^{-15}$ to $9 \cdot 10^{-15}$ after $2$, $4$ and $6$ repairs in the example above.
        This is about $1\,\%$ of the default convolution tolerance, and far below the default tolerance $10^{-8}$ with which a day passes.
        The convolution tolerance has no lower limit: a value that the series cannot reach ends in an error.
    \end{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Monte Carlo validator.} Its result is an empirical stress test and not a proof: zero failures in $n$ runs does not show that the true failure rate is below $1/n$.
    It raises no error for a violation of the schedule rows.
    The mode \texttt{both} runs the replay and the Monte Carlo validators together, and it reports the schedule through the replay validator.
  \item \textbf{Settings of the Gamma validators.} An unknown key in the input block of these settings raises an error.
    The input checks test only that the block is a mapping, so a misspelled key, such as \texttt{repetition} for \texttt{repetitions}, would otherwise be ignored without a message.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Package and inputs}
\label{sec:impl_package}

\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Modules.}
    The package has one module per task.
    The shared Gurobi model and the registry of component builders are in one module, and each degradation model has its own module.
  \item \textbf{Modules without Gurobi.}
    The inverse Gaussian and Gamma modules compute their caps with NumPy and SciPy only, and they import the module of the shared Gurobi model only when Gurobi is installed.
    So each cap lives in the module of its model.
    The input check of the replacement state calls the same cap function as the row, also on a computer without Gurobi.
    The rainflow module loads in the same way, so the constants of its reliability rows can be tested without Gurobi.
    Without Gurobi these modules register no builder.
  \item \textbf{One route for every fleet.}
    Every fleet, whatever its models, goes through the same route: no model has a separate solver.
    Every builder defines the tracked mean on every day for each of its components, because the damage penalty and the output read it.
    After the solve, a shared extraction reads the variables, and then the extraction of each model runs.
  \item \textbf{Cluster scripts.}
    The cluster scripts call only the public functions of the package, so the behaviour that the specification defines does not depend on them.
  \item \textbf{Plots and dashboards.}
    The package has one schedule plot and one sweep plot.
    The schedule plot colours each component by its tracked mean divided by the failure threshold.
    The tracked means are upper bounds of the expected damage (Section~\ref{sec:encoding}).
    The dashboards are Streamlit pages (Streamlit is a Python library for web dashboards).
  \item \textbf{Run options.}
    Run options are keys of the input.
    They do not change the mathematical problem, except the objective mode and the evaluation horizon, which choose the objective.
    A horizon sweep is not an input option: the sweep functions read the same input and solve it for several horizons.
  \item \textbf{Input parser.}
    The input parser rejects old and unknown keys, because each of them names a quantity that has another name, or another meaning, in the specification.
    It cannot detect an array whose mission axis and component axis are swapped when $M = L$.
\end{itemize}

The input example of the specification has $F = 3$ members, $M = 2$ missions and $L = 2$ components.
Its six components use the Gaussian, inverse Gaussian, Wiener and Gamma models, and the rainflow model with the Bernstein bound and with the Chernoff bound.
It passes every input check.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Inverse Gaussian component.}
    It uses $\text{ARD}_1$ with the starting anchor
    \begin{equation}
      0.06 \le \mu_0 = 0.10.
    \end{equation}
  \item \textbf{Gamma component.}
    It has the starting anchor
    \begin{equation}
      0.05 \le \mu_0 = 0.08
    \end{equation}
    and no rate for the initial damage, so its initial damage is known.
  \item \textbf{Chernoff component.}
    It has
    \begin{equation}
      K_0 = 5 \cdot 0.14 = 0.70
    \end{equation}
    by default, so its starting anchor $0.50$ is allowed.
  \item \textbf{Bernstein component.}
    It has the support constant
    \begin{equation}
      b = \max(0.05, 0.03, 0.05) = 0.05,
    \end{equation}
    so
    \begin{equation}
      2\kappa b/3 = 0.077 < \tau = 1,
    \end{equation}
    with $\kappa = \ln 10$.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Open questions}
\label{sec:open_questions}

These are the design questions that the formulation leaves open.
The planned work of the project is listed in its to-do file, \texttt{Things to change.txt}.
\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Hierarchical failure-probability bounds.}
    Every reliability row bounds the failure probability of one component:
    \begin{equation}
      \Pr(D_{i\ell k} > \tau_{i\ell}) \le \varepsilon_{i\ell} \quad \text{for all } i, \ell, k.
    \end{equation}
    A user may want to state the requirement at a coarser level.
    \begin{itemize}[nosep]
      \item \textbf{Member level:}
        \begin{equation}
          \Pr(\exists\,\ell : D_{i\ell k} > \tau_{i\ell}) \le \varepsilon_i \quad \text{for all } i, k,
        \end{equation}
        the probability that \emph{any} component of member~$i$ fails.
        Under independence of the components this reads
        \begin{equation}
          1 - \prod_\ell (1 - \Pr(D_{i\ell k} > \tau_{i\ell})) \le \varepsilon_i,
        \end{equation}
        a nonlinear row that couples the components of a member.
      \item \textbf{Fleet level:}
        \begin{equation}
          \Pr(\exists\,i,\ell : D_{i\ell k} > \tau_{i\ell}) \le \varepsilon \quad \text{for all } k,
        \end{equation}
        the probability that \emph{any} component of the fleet fails.
        Under independence this row couples all members.
    \end{itemize}
    Both levels need either approximations by the union bound, which are conservative and linear, or rows in product form, which are exact and nonlinear.
    The union bound is $\Pr(A \cup B) \le \Pr(A) + \Pr(B)$.
    The per-component levels give both through the union bound: $\varepsilon_{i\ell} \le \varepsilon_i/L$ for every~$\ell$ gives the member level, and $\varepsilon_{i\ell} \le \varepsilon/(FL)$ for every component gives the fleet level.
  \item \textbf{Sampling error of the empirical inputs.}
    Rainflow counting yields \emph{estimates} of the per-mission mean, variance, support width and cumulant generating function, not exact values.
    A guaranteed reliability bound needs one-sided confidence margins on these inputs.
  \item \textbf{Gamma cap over the reachable states.}
    The sharp cap of Section~\ref{sec:stoch_orders} is the largest cap that is safe for every member of the class $\mathcal{G}(m)$.
    The states that a schedule can reach form a smaller set, because the tracked mean also satisfies the cap on every earlier day.
    For a component with one rate and $\beta^*\tau$ below the threshold $t_1(\varepsilon)$, a larger safe cap may exist.
    Its value is not known.
\end{itemize}

\section{Changelog of the software specification}
\label{sec:spec_changelog}

This section records the versions of the software specification \texttt{spec/spec.tex}.
Versions 0.3 to 0.5 use older names and an older horizon.
Their law $\text{ARD}_1$ is the law $\text{ARD}_\infty$ of this document, and their law $\text{ARA}_1$ is the law $\text{ARD}_1$ of this document.
Their horizon has $2H$ days, and the second $H$ days are the schedule to repeat.

\paragraph{Version 0.3 (panel review).}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item The $\text{ARA}_1$ maintenance was removed from the Gaussian model, because no source in the literature supports its extension to the variance.
  \item The output \texttt{v} has the shape $F \times L \times 2H$, with NaN (not a number) at the components without a variance.
  \item The bound on $\varepsilon$ became tighter: from $(0, 0.5)$ to $(0, 0.01]$.
  \item A verified proposition, with a proof by the monotone likelihood ratio (MLR), replaced the open item on first-order stochastic dominance (FSD) of the inverse Gaussian (IG) model.
  \item The remark on tightness became an argument with three mechanisms.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Version 0.4 (rainflow extension).}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item The rainflow degradation model was added.
    It is distribution-free, and its inputs are the empirical mean, variance and support of each mission, from rainflow counting.
    A Cantelli or a Bernstein tail bound certifies the reliability; the new option \texttt{tail\_bound} selects the bound, with the default \texttt{"cantelli"}.
    The specification gives both an exact nonconvex quadratic formulation and a conservative linear tangent formulation.
    The loop constraint acts on both tracked moments.
    The argument is that the componentwise domination of the moments chains across repetitions, while the scalar comparison of the bounds, $U_{2H} \le U_H$, does not.
    The model accepts $\text{ARD}_1$ only, as the Gaussian model did.
  \item The value \texttt{"socp"} of the key \texttt{formulation} was renamed \texttt{"exact"}.
    \texttt{"socp"} remained as a deprecated alias, with a \texttt{UserWarning}.
  \item The exact reliability constraint of the Gaussian and Wiener models had been called a rotated second-order cone (SOC); this was corrected.
    The variance is affine in the decisions, so the squared form is a nonconvex quadratic constraint, handled by the \texttt{NonConvex=2} support of Gurobi.
    The \texttt{"quadratic"} damage penalty remains a truly convex SOC constraint.
  \item The wording of the Wiener loop constraint was corrected after the accepted review item A-02: the constraint bounds the moments; it is not first-order stochastic dominance.
  \item The change log of version 0.3 above describes the revision that the title page had called ``v0.2 (revised)''.
  \item A panel review after the draft (markers RF-042--RF-045 and corrections made in place) made further changes.
    \begin{itemize}[nosep]
      \item It fixed the base case of the induction over the loop ($m \ge 2$, with the loop row as the case $m = 1$).
      \item It fixed the wording at the boundary of the precondition $\tau > 2\kappa b/3$.
      \item It fixed the centring step in the application of Bernstein's inequality, which holds a fortiori (it follows from a stronger fact).
      \item It fixed the statement of the problem class for fleets whose components are all inverse Gaussian or Gamma.
      \item It extended the consistency checks based on the Bhatia--Davis inequality (a bound on the variance from the mean and the range) to the initial and replacement states.
      \item It extended the list of checked array shapes to $b^{\mathrm{inc}}$, $b^{0}$ and $b^{\mathrm{new}}$.
      \item It moved \texttt{tail\_bound} to the input tables of the models.
      \item It added the fifth border style of the plot.
      \item It corrected the value of $\varepsilon$ in the YAML example, which did not respect the bound $(0, 0.01]$ of the specification.
    \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Version 0.5 (removal of the loop penalty).}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item The soft penalty of the loop constraint, $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{loop}}$, and its coefficient $C_P$ were removed from every place: the objective, the penalty formulas of the models, the total penalty sum, the input tables, the YAML example and the statement of the full problem.
    The hard loop constraints of all five models did not change.
  \item The remark on the tightness of the big-M rows had named three mechanisms.
    It was revised to name the two cost mechanisms that remain, $C_D\,u$ and $C_R\,z$.
  \item An argument on robustness to slack was added.
    The tracked states are componentwise upper bounds on the true moments, and each one-sided dynamics row dominates the exact monotone map of one day.
    So the hard loop constraint gives $\Pi(s_H) \le s_H$ for the exact map $\Pi$ of one repetition of the second half (Section~\ref{sec:perpetual}; that version wrote $\Phi$ for this map).
    This inequality is the premise of the induction in the repeatability argument.
    The monotonicity of the reliability certificates then keeps every future repetition certified, even when the tracked states carry slack.
    The only role of the penalty had been to break ties in favour of larger sustainability margins.
  \item A later review (markers REV-001--REV-022) closed a gap of the loop constraint under $\text{ARA}_1$: the loop did not cover the anchors.
    It added the hard rows
    \begin{align}
      \mu^{\mathrm{last}}_{2H} &\le \mu^{\mathrm{last}}_{H} && \text{(Wiener with $\text{ARA}_1$)}, \\
      \alpha^{\mathrm{last}}_{2H} &\le \alpha^{\mathrm{last}}_{H} && \text{(Gamma with $\text{ARA}_1$)}.
    \end{align}
    A derivation of monotonicity on the full state shows that these rows restore the chaining premise $\Pi(s_H) \le s_H$.
    The review extended the remark on robustness to slack in the same way.
  \item The same review made further changes.
    \begin{itemize}[nosep]
      \item It stated that an idle day is a row of zeros in the assignment, so the term with $C_M$ is a real decision cost, not a constant.
      \item It corrected the day index of the anchor in the $\text{ARA}_1$ repair-cost row ($k \to k{-}1$).
      \item It removed the old duplicate parameter \texttt{alpha\_target}, a former name of $\tau$.
      \item It made consistent which keys are optional: \texttt{tail\_bound} and the input fields that have a default.
      \item It added the derived constants to the notation table.
      \item It expanded the two items on future work of the rainflow model, with pointers to the Route-I refinements of this document (Section~\ref{sec:routeI_refinements}).
      \item It recorded two questions on the convention of the $\text{ARA}_1$ anchor as open items: whether the anchor stores the state before or after the repair, and the missing reset of the anchor at a replacement.
    \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Version 1.0 (merged package).}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item The specification describes the package that merges the branches \texttt{lore} and \texttt{main}, on the code base of \texttt{main}.
    It holds only the codebase and its mathematical constructions; the reasons, the proofs and the sources are in this scoping document.
  \item The five degradation models are cell builders in one registry; a cell is component~$\ell$ of member~$i$.
  \item The repair laws are $\text{ARD}_\infty$, which reduces the whole degradation, and $\text{ARD}_1$, which reduces the degradation since the last intervention.
  \item The horizon has a transitory phase and an operating phase, $H = [H_1, H_2]$, and the loop row bounds the full tracked state, anchors included.
  \item Every dynamics row is one-sided, in big-M or indicator form.
  \item The rainflow model offers five tail bounds, and the quadratic reliability rows have an exact, a tangent and a piecewise-linear form.
  \item The inverse Gaussian row is a cap from a conditional tail mean.
    The Gamma row is a cap on the tracked mean that holds for any mix of rates.
  \item The reliability level, the tangent point, the repair cost and the replacement cost are set per cell.
  \item The specification covers the horizon sweeps, the warm starts, the schedule plot and the validators.
  \item The input keys follow one table, and unknown or old keys raise an error.
  \item The input checks give every non-strict inequality the relative tolerance $10^{-12}\max(1, |a|, |b|)$, and they compare selector values with their case.
    At a key without a default, \texttt{null} means that the key is not given at that cell.
    YAML and JSON result files write every number that is not finite as \texttt{null}.
  \item The specification fixes the numerical methods of the two caps: a root search in $\ln q$ for the inverse Gaussian quantile, and the grid, the golden-section search and the two bisections of the Gamma cap~$A_1$.
  \item One function, \texttt{read\_result}, reads the result files for every tool.
    The greedy heuristic tries a repair and a replacement on every free day of a Wiener cell.
\end{itemize}

\appendix

\section{Proofs and derivations}
\label{app:proofs}

\subsection{Overview of the problem}

\phantomsection\label{drv:down_set}
\begin{proof}[Derivation that $\mathcal{C}_{i\ell}$ is a closed down-set]
Every reliability row, written in chance-bound form, reads ``a continuous function of the tracked statistics, non-decreasing in each of them, is at most a constant'' (Table~\ref{tab:chance_rows}).
So the set $\mathcal{C}_{i\ell}$ is closed, and it is a down-set.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{prf:lem:monotone}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:monotone}]
Let $\xi \le \xi'$ componentwise.
Each coordinate of $\pi_k$ has one of the forms $s + \delta s_k$, $g$, $s$, $a\,s$, $a\,s + (1-a)\,g$ or $s^{\mathrm{new}}$.
By (M1), $a \ge 0$ and $1 - a \ge 0$.
Therefore each coordinate of $\pi_k(\xi)$ is at most the same coordinate of $\pi_k(\xi')$.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{prf:prop:domination}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:domination}]
At binary points the rows of day~$k$ read: $s_k \ge \pi_k(\xi_{k-1})_s$ for every tracked statistic $s$, with equality on a replacement day;
\begin{equation}
  g_k = s_k \ge \pi_k(\xi_{k-1})_g
\end{equation}
on an intervention day;
\begin{equation}
  g_k = g_{k-1} = \pi_k(\xi_{k-1})_g
\end{equation}
on any other day.
So $\xi_k \ge \pi_k(\xi_{k-1})$.
Let
\begin{equation}
  \theta_{k-1} \le \zeta_{k-1} \le \xi_{k-1}
\end{equation}
(true for $k = 1$ by (M2), since $\zeta_0 = \xi_0$).
Then, by (M2) and Lemma~\ref{lem:monotone},
\begin{equation}
  \theta_k \le \pi_k(\theta_{k-1}) \le \pi_k(\zeta_{k-1}) = \zeta_k \le \pi_k(\xi_{k-1}) \le \xi_k .
\end{equation}
Therefore
\begin{equation}
  \theta_k \le \zeta_k \le \xi_k
\end{equation}
for every $k$, by induction.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:horizon_safety}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the reliability on the days of the horizon]
On every day $k = 1, \ldots, T$ the tracked values satisfy the reliability row in the form chosen for the component.
Every form lies inside $\mathcal{C}_{i\ell}$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:tangent_safe}), so $\xi_k \in \mathcal{C}_{i\ell}$.
By Proposition~\ref{prop:domination}, $\theta_k \le \xi_k$, so (M3) gives $\Pr(D_{i\ell k} > \tau) \le \varepsilon$ on the days of the horizon.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{prf:thm:perpetual}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:perpetual}]
Fix a component.
Let $\xi_k$ be its full tracked state, and $\theta_k$ and $\zeta_k$ the true statistics and the reference trajectory of the endless schedule.
By (M2) and Lemma~\ref{lem:monotone}, the induction of Proposition~\ref{prop:domination} gives $\theta_k \le \zeta_k$ on every day $k \ge 1$.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item Days $k \le T$: $\zeta_k \le \xi_k$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:domination}) and $\xi_k \in \mathcal{C}_{i\ell}$ (Section~\ref{sec:encoding}).
  \item By Proposition~\ref{prop:domination} and Lemma~\ref{lem:monotone},
    \begin{equation}
      \xi_{H_1+h} \ge \pi_{H_1+h}(\xi_{H_1+h-1}) \ge \cdots \ge \Pi_h(\xi_{H_1})
    \end{equation}
    for $h = 1, \ldots, H_2$.
    With~\eqref{eq:loop},
    \begin{equation}
      \Pi(\xi_{H_1}) \le \xi_T \le \xi_{H_1}.
    \end{equation}
  \item Let $\Pi^n(\xi_{H_1}) \le \xi_{H_1}$ (true for $n = 0$).
    Then
    \begin{equation}
      \Pi^{n+1}(\xi_{H_1}) = \Pi(\Pi^n(\xi_{H_1})) \le \Pi(\xi_{H_1}) \le \xi_{H_1}
    \end{equation}
    by Lemma~\ref{lem:monotone}.
    So $\Pi^n(\xi_{H_1}) \le \xi_{H_1}$ for every $n \ge 0$.
  \item Day $k = H_1 + n H_2 + h$ with $n \ge 0$ and $1 \le h \le H_2$:
    \begin{equation}
      \zeta_k = \Pi_h\bigl(\Pi^n(\zeta_{H_1})\bigr)
      \le \Pi_h\bigl(\Pi^n(\xi_{H_1})\bigr)
      \le \Pi_h(\xi_{H_1})
      \le \xi_{H_1+h} \in \mathcal{C}_{i\ell}.
    \end{equation}
\end{itemize}
Therefore, on every day $k \ge 1$,
\begin{equation}
  \theta_k \le \zeta_k \le s
\end{equation}
for a tracked value $s \in \mathcal{C}_{i\ell}$.
Since $\zeta_k \ge 0$ and $\mathcal{C}_{i\ell}$ is a down-set, $\zeta_k \in \mathcal{C}_{i\ell}$, and (M3) gives $\Pr(D_{i\ell k} > \tau) \le \varepsilon$.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:anchor_loop}
\begin{proof}[Derivation that the loop constraint needs the anchors]
The third step of the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:perpetual} needs $\Pi(\xi_{H_1}) \le \xi_{H_1}$ in every coordinate.
Under $\text{ARD}_1$ a larger anchor gives a weaker repair, so an anchor that grows over one repetition weakens the repairs of the next one.
Example:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Data.} $\rho = 0.5$; the transitory phase ends at mean $0.4$ and anchor $0.1$; the operating phase is a mission ($+0.1$), a repair, and a mission ($+0.05$).
  \item \textbf{First repetition.} The phase ends at mean $0.35$ and anchor $0.3$, because the repair gives $0.5 \cdot 0.5 + 0.5 \cdot 0.1 = 0.3$.
    The row on the mean holds ($0.35 \le 0.4$), and the row on the anchor fails ($0.3 > 0.1$).
  \item \textbf{Later repetitions.} Each repetition raises the mean after the repair by $0.075$, so the mean grows without bound: the third repetition already peaks at $0.525$, above the checked peak $0.5$.
\end{itemize}
A loop row on the mean alone would accept this unsafe schedule.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{prf:prop:acceptance}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:acceptance}]
(a) The values $\xi_k = \zeta_k$ satisfy every row of day~$k$ with equality at binary points.
They lie in $\mathcal{C}_{i\ell}$ because the schedule is safe.
The loop row reads
\begin{equation}
  \zeta_T = \Pi(\bar\zeta) \le \bar\zeta = \zeta_{H_1}.
\end{equation}

(b) Let $y_n = \Pi^n(\bar\zeta)$, the reference state at the end of the $n$-th repetition.
By Lemma~\ref{lem:monotone} and $\Pi(\bar\zeta) \ge \bar\zeta$,
\begin{equation}
  y_{n+1} = \Pi(y_n) \ge \Pi(y_{n-1}) = y_n.
\end{equation}
The sequence is non-decreasing and lies in the natural ranges, so it converges to some $y^*$ with $y^* \ge \bar\zeta$.
Each day map is affine or constant, so $\Pi$ and its prefixes $\Pi_h$ are continuous:
\begin{equation}
  \Pi(y^*) = \lim_n y_{n+1} = y^*
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \Pi_h(y^*) = \lim_n \Pi_h(y_n).
\end{equation}
Each $\Pi_h(y_n)$ is a reference value of a later day, so it lies in the closed set $\mathcal{C}_{i\ell}$; hence $\Pi_h(y^*) \in \mathcal{C}_{i\ell}$ for $h = 1, \ldots, H_2$, and
\begin{equation}
  y^* = \Pi_{H_2}(y^*) \in \mathcal{C}_{i\ell}.
\end{equation}
Take $\xi_k = \zeta_k$ for $k < H_1$, $\xi_{H_1} = y^*$ and $\xi_{H_1+h} = \Pi_h(y^*)$.
Day~$H_1$ is not a replacement day and the component has no anchor, so
\begin{equation}
  \xi_{H_1} = y^* \ge \pi_{H_1}(\xi_{H_1-1})
\end{equation}
satisfies the rows of day~$H_1$.
The later days satisfy their rows with equality, and the loop row reads
\begin{equation}
  \xi_T = \Pi(y^*) = y^* = \xi_{H_1}.
\end{equation}
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:scalar_acceptance}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the acceptance of every safe schedule for one tracked statistic]
A scalar state $y$ is always comparable with its image: $\Pi(y) \le y$ or $\Pi(y) \ge y$.
So a component in the exact form with one tracked statistic under $\text{ARD}_\infty$, not replaced on day~$H_1$, accepts every safe schedule.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{prf:prop:ard1_replacement}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:ard1_replacement}]
Suppose that the component is not replaced in the operating phase.
Let $\mu_k$ be its tracked mean, $g_k$ the anchor of the mean, and $\delta\mu_k$ the mean increment of day~$k$.
Then $\mu_k \ge g_k$ on every day: with equality on a repair day, and on other days because $\mu$ does not decrease while $g$ stays constant, with $g_0 \le \mu_0$.
On a repair day~$k$ of the operating phase,
\begin{equation}
  g_k = \mu_k \;\ge\; (1-\rho)\,\mu_{k-1} + \rho\, g_{k-1} \;=\; g_{k-1} + (1-\rho)\,(\mu_{k-1} - g_{k-1}).
\end{equation}
So
\begin{equation}
  g_T \ge g_{H_1} + (1-\rho)\sum (\mu_{k-1} - g_{k-1}),
\end{equation}
the sum over the repair days of the operating phase, with non-negative terms.
The loop row $g_T \le g_{H_1}$ and $\rho < 1$ force every term to~$0$.
Then every repair day gives $\mu_k \ge \mu_{k-1}$, every other day gives $\mu_k \ge \mu_{k-1} + \delta\mu_k$, and
\begin{equation}
  \mu_T \;\ge\; \mu_{H_1} + \sum_{k=H_1+1}^{T} \delta\mu_k \;>\; \mu_{H_1},
\end{equation}
which violates the loop row $\mu_T \le \mu_{H_1}$.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{prf:prop:repair_cost}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:repair_cost}]
The first inequality is~\eqref{eq:z_row} with $x^m_{i\ell k} = 1$.
Under $\text{ARD}_\infty$,
\begin{equation}
  g^{\mu} = \zeta^{g} = 0,
\end{equation}
and $\mu_{k-1} \ge \zeta^{\mu}_{k-1}$ by Proposition~\ref{prop:domination}.
Under $\text{ARD}_1$, let $t < k$ be the last intervention day before~$k$, or $t = 0$ if there is none.
Then $g^{\mu}_{k-1} = g^{\mu}_t$ and $\zeta^{g}_{k-1} = \zeta^{g}_t$.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item If $t = 0$: $g^{\mu}_0 = \zeta^{g}_0$ (the starting anchor), so
    \begin{equation}
      g^{\mu}_{k-1} - \zeta^{g}_{k-1} = 0 \le \mu_{k-1} - \zeta^{\mu}_{k-1}.
    \end{equation}
  \item If $t \ge 1$: $g^{\mu}_t = \mu_t$ and $\zeta^{g}_t = \zeta^{\mu}_t$.
    On the days $j = t+1, \ldots, k-1$ there is no intervention:
    \begin{equation}
      \mu_j \ge \mu_{j-1} + \delta\mu_j
    \end{equation}
    and
    \begin{equation}
      \zeta^{\mu}_j = \zeta^{\mu}_{j-1} + \delta\mu_j.
    \end{equation}
    So $\mu_j - \zeta^{\mu}_j$ does not decrease, and
    \begin{equation}
      \mu_{k-1} - \zeta^{\mu}_{k-1} \ge \mu_t - \zeta^{\mu}_t = g^{\mu}_{k-1} - \zeta^{g}_{k-1}.
    \end{equation}
\end{itemize}
Rearranging gives
\begin{equation}
  \mu_{k-1} - g^{\mu}_{k-1} \ge \zeta^{\mu}_{k-1} - \zeta^{g}_{k-1}.
\end{equation}
The mean recursion is linear (Section~\ref{sec:repair_connection}); with an exact starting anchor, $\zeta^{\mu}$ and $\zeta^{g}$ are the true means, so
\begin{equation}
  \rho\,(\zeta^{\mu}_{k-1} - \zeta^{g}_{k-1}) = \mathbb{E}[D^- - D^+].
\end{equation}
\end{proof}

\subsection{Stochastic degradation and fatigue models}

\phantomsection\label{prf:prop:st_closure}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:st_closure}]
By the coupling characterisation of $\le_{\mathrm{st}}$ (Definition~\ref{def:st}), for each $t$ there exist $\hat X_t \stackrel{d}{=} \Delta D_t$ and $\hat Y_t \stackrel{d}{=} \widetilde{\Delta D}_t$ on a common probability space with $\hat X_t \le \hat Y_t$ almost surely.
Realise these couplings independently across $t$.
Then
\begin{equation}
  \sum_t \hat X_t \le \sum_t \hat Y_t
\end{equation}
almost surely.
Independence gives
\begin{equation}
  \sum_t \hat X_t \stackrel{d}{=} \sum_t \Delta D_t
  \quad\text{and}\quad
  \sum_t \hat Y_t \stackrel{d}{=} \sum_t \widetilde{\Delta D}_t.
\end{equation}
Hence
\begin{equation}
  \sum_t \Delta D_t \le_{\mathrm{st}} \sum_t \widetilde{\Delta D}_t.
\end{equation}
Any non-decreasing $g$ preserves the almost-sure inequality, which gives the general statement.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:routeI_bounds}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the Markov, Cantelli and Chernoff bounds]
With $D \ge 0$ and $\tau > 0$, Markov's inequality~\eqref{eq:markov_surrogate} follows from
\begin{equation}
  \tau\,\mathbf{1}\{D > \tau\} \le D.
\end{equation}
Applying Markov to $(D - \mathbb{E}[D] + c)^2$ and optimising over $c > 0$ gives the one-sided Chebyshev (Cantelli) bound~\eqref{eq:cantelli_surrogate}, for $\tau > \mathbb{E}[D]$.
The exponential (Cram\'er--Chernoff) bound~\eqref{eq:chernoff_surrogate} follows from Markov's inequality applied to $e^{sD}$, and it is valid for every fixed $s > 0$.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:bernstein_curvature}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the properties of the Bernstein constraint~\eqref{eq:bernstein_constraint}]
By~\eqref{eq:additive_functionals} the mean and the variance are \emph{affine} in the counts.
So the square root is concave, and the feasible set is reverse-convex (its complement is convex); it cannot be written as a second-order cone.

Every tangent line to the concave square root lies \emph{above} it.
So the replacement of the square root by a fixed tangent, or by the pointwise minimum of several tangents, gives constraints in the counts that imply~\eqref{eq:bernstein_constraint}: one linear constraint for a fixed tangent, and linear constraints with an SOS2 set for the minimum.

For an increment whose range has width $b_{\ell j}$, Bernstein's bound of its cumulant generating function reads, for $0 < s < 3/b_{\ell j}$,
\begin{equation}
  \psi_{\ell j}(s) \;\le\; s\,\mu_{\ell j} + \frac{s^2 v_{\ell j}/2}{1 - s\,b_{\ell j}/3}.
\end{equation}
Used in place of $\psi_{\ell j}$ in~\eqref{eq:chernoff_permission}, it keeps a support per mission, and the bound still needs the minimisation over~$s$.
With one constant $b_\ell \ge \max_j b_{\ell j}$ in every term, the choice $s = a/(\operatorname{Var}[D] + b_\ell\, a/3)$, with $a = \tau - \mathbb{E}[D]$, gives the closed form~\eqref{eq:bernstein_constraint}, which has no~$s$.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:width}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the properties of the width of a range]
Let the increment~$X$ lie in an interval $[l, l + b]$ almost surely.
Bernstein's inequality needs an upper bound of the centred term, and the width is one:
\begin{equation}
  X - \mathbb{E}X \le l + b - l = b,
\end{equation}
since $\mathbb{E}X \ge l$.

For Popoviciu's inequality, let $X$ take values in an interval of width~$w$ with midpoint~$m_c$; then
\begin{equation}
  \operatorname{Var} X \le \mathbb{E}\,(X - m_c)^2 \le (w/2)^2.
\end{equation}
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{prf:prop:rf_maps}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:rf_maps}]
Item~1: the mean and $R$ are additive by definition, the variance and the cumulant generating function by independence.
Item~2: $m$ and $V$ scale by $1-\rho$ and $(1-\rho)^2$; every weight is multiplied by $1-\rho$, so $R$ scales by $(1-\rho)^2$.
A cumulant generating function is convex with value~$0$ at~$0$, so
\begin{equation}
  K\bigl((1-\rho)D\bigr) = K_D\bigl((1-\rho)s\bigr) \le (1-\rho)\,K_D(s) + \rho\,K_D(0) = (1-\rho)\,K(D),
\end{equation}
with $K_D$ the cumulant generating function of~$D$ as a function of its argument.
Item~3: by independence,
\begin{equation}
  \theta(D^-) = \theta(G) + \theta(\Delta);
\end{equation}
for $R$ the weights of the terms of~$G$ are unchanged and those of~$\Delta$ are multiplied by $1-\rho$.
Hence
\begin{align}
  \theta(D^+) &= \theta(G) + a_\theta\,\theta(\Delta) \notag\\
  &= \theta(G) + a_\theta\bigl(\theta(D^-) - \theta(G)\bigr) \notag\\
  &= a_\theta\,\theta(D^-) + (1 - a_\theta)\,\theta(G),
\end{align}
and, with item~2 applied to~$\Delta$,
\begin{align}
  K(D^+) &= K(G) + K_\Delta\bigl((1-\rho)s\bigr) \notag\\
  &\le K(G) + (1-\rho)\bigl(K(D^-) - K(G)\bigr) \notag\\
  &= (1-\rho)\,K(D^-) + \rho\,K(G).
\end{align}
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:five_rows}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the rows of Table~\ref{tab:five_bounds}]
Write $d_k = \tau - \mu_k$.
\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Markov.} By A0 and Markov's inequality,
    \begin{equation}
      \Pr(D_k > \tau) \le m/\tau \le \mu_k/\tau,
    \end{equation}
    so the row $\mu_k \le \varepsilon\,\tau$ suffices.
    It uses the mean only and does not need A1 or A2.
  \item \textbf{Cantelli.} Cantelli's inequality gives
    \begin{equation}
      \Pr(D - m \ge a) \le V/(V + a^2) \quad\text{for } a > 0;
    \end{equation}
    the row is equivalent to
    \begin{equation}
      \mu_k + \kappa_C\sqrt{v_k} \le \tau
      \quad\text{with}\quad
      \kappa_C = \sqrt{(1-\varepsilon)/\varepsilon}.
    \end{equation}
    If $d_k > 0$,
    \begin{equation}
      \Pr(D_k > \tau) \le \Pr(D_k - m \ge \tau - m) \le \frac{V}{V + (\tau - m)^2} \le \frac{v_k}{v_k + d_k^2} \le \varepsilon,
    \end{equation}
    because $v/(v + d^2)$ increases in~$v$ and decreases in~$d$, and $\tau - m \ge d_k$.
    If $d_k = 0$, the row gives $v_k = 0$, so $V = 0$ and
    \begin{equation}
      D_k = m \le \tau.
    \end{equation}
  \item \textbf{Hoeffding.} $D_k - m$ is a sum of independent centred terms $w_t(X_t - \mathbb{E}X_t)$ with ranges of widths $w_t\sqrt{r_t}$, and the sum of their squares is~$R^{\mathrm{t}}$.
    Hoeffding's inequality gives
    \begin{equation}
      \Pr(D_k - m \ge a) \le \exp(-2a^2/R^{\mathrm{t}}),
    \end{equation}
    and $\exp(-2d^2/R) \le \varepsilon$ exactly when $d^2 \ge (\kappa/2)\,R$.
    If $R^{\mathrm{t}} = 0$, every term is constant and
    \begin{equation}
      D_k = m \le \tau;
    \end{equation}
    this covers $d_k = 0$, where the row gives $R_k = 0$.
    Otherwise $d_k > 0$, and
    \begin{equation}
      \Pr(D_k > \tau) \le \exp\bigl(-2(\tau - m)^2/R^{\mathrm{t}}\bigr) \le \exp(-2d_k^2/R_k) \le \varepsilon,
    \end{equation}
    because $\exp(-2d^2/R)$ increases in~$R$ and decreases in~$d$.
  \item \textbf{Bernstein.} The support constant $b_\ell$ of~\eqref{eq:bernstein_constraint} is a constant, not a decision variable; the replacement support enters it only when replacements are allowed.
    Every weighted term satisfies
    \begin{equation}
      w_t(X_t - \mathbb{E}X_t) \le w_t\, b_t \le b_\ell,
    \end{equation}
    with $b_t$ the width of the range of~$X_t$, whatever the schedule, because $w_t \le 1$.
    Bernstein's inequality for independent terms bounded above by~$b_\ell$ gives
    \begin{equation}
      \Pr(D - m \ge a) \le \exp\bigl(-a^2/(2(V + b_\ell\,a/3))\bigr) \quad\text{for } a > 0;
    \end{equation}
    the right side is at most~$\varepsilon$ exactly when $a^2 - 2c\,a - 2\kappa V \ge 0$.
    With $v_k \ge 0$, the row and its side row give $d_k \ge 2c$.
    The function
    \begin{equation}
      a^2/(2(V + b_\ell\,a/3))
    \end{equation}
    increases in $a > 0$ and decreases in~$V$, and
    \begin{equation}
      \tau - m \ge d_k \ge 2c;
    \end{equation}
    so $\Pr(D_k > \tau) \le \varepsilon$ when $b_\ell > 0$.
    When $b_\ell = 0$, every term is constant and
    \begin{equation}
      D_k = m \le \tau.
    \end{equation}
  \item \textbf{Chernoff.} For $s > 0$, Markov's inequality applied to $e^{sD}$ gives
    \begin{equation}
      \Pr(D_k \ge \tau) \le \exp(K^{\mathrm{t}} - s\tau).
    \end{equation}
    Since $K^{\mathrm{t}} \le K_k$, the row gives
    \begin{equation}
      \Pr(D_k > \tau) \le \exp(K_k - s\tau) \le \varepsilon.
    \end{equation}
    The row bounds $K$ by $s\tau - \kappa$, its natural range, and $K \ge 0$, because
    \begin{equation}
      K(X) \ge s\,\mathbb{E}X \ge 0
    \end{equation}
    by Jensen's inequality and A0.
    So a known zero damage satisfies the row only if $s\tau \ge \kappa$, and a replacement day satisfies it only if the replacement value of~$K$ is at most $s\tau - \kappa$.
\end{itemize}
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:chernoff_initial}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the initial and replacement values of the statistic~$K$]
By Jensen's inequality,
\begin{equation}
  \ln\mathbb{E}[e^{sD}] \ge s\,\mathbb{E}[D],
\end{equation}
with equality exactly when $D$ is almost surely constant.
So a known initial damage $\mu_0$ gives the initial value $K_0 = s\,\mu_0$, and a known replacement damage $\mu^{\mathrm{new}}$ gives the reset value $s\,\mu^{\mathrm{new}}$.
A random initial or replacement damage needs its own cumulant generating function at~$s$, which is larger.

For the same reason, the cumulant generating function of every increment at~$s$ is at least $s$ times its mean.
Also the initial value and the replacement value of~$K$ are at least $s$ times the corresponding mean.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:loopI_no_chain}
\begin{proof}[Derivation that $U^{(1)} \le U^{(0)}$ does not chain (Section~\ref{sec:loop_routeI})]
The bound $U$ is a many-to-one function of the parameters of the state, and its level sets are not invariant under the dynamics.
Bernstein's bound, for example, depends on $(\mathbb{E}[D],\operatorname{Var}[D])$ through a single scalar.
So $U^{(1)} = U^{(0)}$ can hold with
\begin{equation}
  \big(\mathbb{E}[D_T],\operatorname{Var}[D_T]\big) \;\ne\; \big(\mathbb{E}[D_{H_1}],\operatorname{Var}[D_{H_1}]\big),
\end{equation}
for example with a larger mean balanced by a smaller variance.
One more repetition of the dynamics can map these two parameter points to different bound values, the larger one at the end of the second repetition.
The Cantelli instance of Section~\ref{sec:loop_routeI} with $\sum_j n_{ij} v_{\ell j} = 4$ gives $U^{(1)} = U^{(0)} = 0.2000$ and $U^{(2)} = 0.2155$.
So the relation~\eqref{eq:loopI_U_rise} is possible.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:loopI_moments}
\begin{proof}[Derivation that the moment loop~\eqref{eq:loop_moments} chains]
Under the dynamics, the mean and the variance change by one of three operations.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Increment.} The moments grow as
    \begin{equation}
      \mathbb{E} \mathrel{+}= \sum_j n_{ij}\mu_{\ell j}
      \qquad\text{and}\qquad
      \operatorname{Var} \mathrel{+}= \sum_j n_{ij} v_{\ell j}.
    \end{equation}
  \item \textbf{Imperfect repair.} A repair maps the moments as follows under $\text{ARD}_\infty$ and under $\text{ARD}_1$:
    \begin{align}
      \text{$\text{ARD}_\infty$:}\quad \mathbb{E} &\mapsto (1-\rho)\,\mathbb{E}, &
      \operatorname{Var} &\mapsto (1-\rho)^2\operatorname{Var},\\
      \text{$\text{ARD}_1$:}\quad \mathbb{E} &\mapsto (1-\rho)\,\mathbb{E} + \rho\, g^{\mu}, &
      \operatorname{Var} &\mapsto (1-\rho)^2\operatorname{Var} + \bigl(1-(1-\rho)^2\bigr)\, g^{v}.
    \end{align}
  \item \textbf{Replacement.} A reset to the constants of $D^{\mathrm{new}}$.
\end{itemize}
Each operation is non-decreasing in every moment and anchor separately, so the moments evolve as monotone recursions.

Componentwise domination therefore chains.
Write $\Pi$ for the map of one repetition of the operating phase.
Then~\eqref{eq:loop_moments}, with the rows on the anchors under $\text{ARD}_1$, states that $\Pi$ does not increase either moment, nor either anchor, at the state of day~$H_1$.
Hence the moments at the end of each repetition are at most those at the end of the previous one (Theorem~\ref{thm:perpetual}).

The Bernstein bound~\eqref{eq:bernstein_constraint}, like the Cantelli bound~\eqref{eq:cantelli_surrogate}, is increasing in both $\mathbb{E}[D]$ and $\operatorname{Var}[D]$.
So the certified risk does not increase across repetitions, as in~\eqref{eq:loopI_risk}, and the schedule is repeatable.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:loopI_cumulant}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the loop~\eqref{eq:loop_cumulant} on the cumulant generating function]
Without imperfect repair, $K_k$ is its value at the last replacement (or on day~$0$) plus the count-weighted sum
\begin{equation}
  \sum_j n^{(k)}_{ij}\,\psi_{\ell j}(s).
\end{equation}
Here $n^{(k)}_{ij}$ is the number of days on which mission~$j$ is served after the last replacement and up to day~$k$.
Then $M_{D}(s)$ propagates multiplicatively,
\begin{equation}
  M_{D + \Delta D}(s) \;=\; M_D(s)\,M_{\Delta D}(s).
\end{equation}
So the value of $K$ on a day depends only on its value on the day before, and~\eqref{eq:loop_cumulant} carries over by induction.

An imperfect (ARD) repair does not keep this scalar exact. With $D^+ = (1-\rho)D^-$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mgf_rescale}
  M_{D^+}(s) \;=\; \mathbb{E}\big[e^{s(1-\rho)D^-}\big] \;=\; M_{D^-}\big((1-\rho)s\big):
\end{equation}
the transform of the repaired state at $s$ is the old transform at the \emph{shifted} argument $(1-\rho)s$.
The value $M_{D^-}(s)$ does not determine it: the repair moves the state along the transform curve.
However, $M_{D^-}(s)$ determines an upper bound: the cumulant generating function is convex with value~$0$ at~$0$, so (Proposition~\ref{prop:rf_maps})
\begin{align}
  K_{D^+}(s) &\;\le\; (1-\rho)\,K_{D^-}(s) && \text{under $\text{ARD}_\infty$, and}\\
  K_{D^+}(s) &\;\le\; (1-\rho)\,K_{D^-}(s) + \rho\,K_G(s) && \text{under $\text{ARD}_1$.}
\end{align}
So the tracked statistic follows the maps~\eqref{eq:loopI_cgf_maps}, with the anchor~$g^K$.
These maps are non-decreasing in $(K, g^K)$, and the tracked $K$ stays an upper bound on the true cumulant generating function, which is all that the Chernoff certificate needs.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:sched_support}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the validity of the support $b_\ell(\zeta)$ and of its rows (Section~\ref{sec:routeI_refinements})]\leavevmode
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Validity of $b_\ell(\zeta)$.} Between resets, the state is a sum, with weights in $[0,1]$, of one reset draw and of increments of \emph{served} missions only.
    So $b_\ell(\zeta)$ bounds every summand, and Bernstein's inequality applies without change, on every day and in every repetition of the schedule.
  \item \textbf{Equivalence of the rows.} The certified value $\mu + c + \sqrt{c^2 + 2\kappa v}$ has the derivative
    \begin{equation}
      1 + c/\sqrt{c^2 + 2\kappa v} \;\in\; [1,2]
    \end{equation}
    in~$c$.
    So it increases with the support constant, and the Bernstein row written for a larger constant implies every row written for a smaller one.
    The active row with the largest constant dominates the others, and that largest constant is exactly $\kappa b_\ell(\zeta)/3$.
    So the conditional rows at $c_j$ and the unconditional row at $c_0$ are exactly equivalent to the single row with the schedule-dependent maximum.
\end{itemize}
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:pwl_recovery}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the remaining gap~\eqref{eq:pwl_union_gap} of the union of tangents]
The gap of tangent~$r$ is
\begin{equation}
  f(\mu) - T_r(\mu) \;=\; (\mu - \mu_r)^2,
\end{equation}
so the remaining gap of the union is~\eqref{eq:pwl_union_gap} where the union admits $v \ge 0$.
This gap is quadratic in the distance to the nearest tangent point, with worst case $(\Delta/2)^2$ for a spacing~$\Delta$.
Where the union admits no $v \ge 0$, the gap is $f(\mu) \le (\Delta/2)^2$.
So doubling the number of tangents divides it by four.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{prf:prop:tangent_safe}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:tangent_safe}]
$f$ is a polynomial of degree~$2$ in~$\mu$ with leading coefficient~$c_2$, so its Taylor expansion at~$\mu_r$ is exact:
\begin{equation}
  f(\mu) = f(\mu_r) + f'(\mu_r)(\mu - \mu_r) + c_2(\mu - \mu_r)^2.
\end{equation}
Since $c_2 > 0$, the tangent lies below~$f$.
Each linear form caps $\chi$ by one such tangent and keeps the side row.
Therefore it implies~\eqref{eq:quad_row}.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:bernstein_tangent_point}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the conditions on the tangent point of a Bernstein row (Section~\ref{sec:linear_forms})]
A tangent is linear in~$\mu$, so its largest value on $[0, \tau - c]$ is at an end point.
At $\tau - c$ it is at most
\begin{equation}
  f(\tau - c) \;=\; -c^2/(2\kappa) \;<\; 0.
\end{equation}
At~$0$ it equals
\begin{equation}
  f(0) - \mu_r^2/(2\kappa) \;=\; \bigl(\tau(\tau - 2c) - \mu_r^2\bigr)/(2\kappa),
\end{equation}
by Proposition~\ref{prop:tangent_safe} with $c_2 = 1/(2\kappa)$.
So the tangent row admits a point with $v \ge 0$ exactly when $\mu_r^2 \le \tau(\tau - 2c)$, and at equality only $\mu = 0$ and $v = 0$.
Since $\sqrt{\tau(\tau - 2c)} < \tau - c$, this covers the case $\mu_r \ge \tau - c$, in which the tangent has a non-negative slope.

The \texttt{pwl} form accepts the points below the largest of its tangents, a convex function.
At $\mu = 0$ the largest is the tangent at the midpoint $\tau/(2P)$ of the first segment, so the same argument applies with $\mu_r = \tau/(2P)$.
Otherwise no segment admits a point with $v > 0$: the first segment fails as above, and on every later segment
\begin{equation}
  \mu \;\ge\; \tau/P \;>\; \tau - 2c,
\end{equation}
where $f(\mu) < 0$.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:miner_invmom}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the inverse moments~\eqref{eq:miner_invmom}]\leavevmode
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Log-normal.} The first line is the normal moment generating function at the argument $-r$.
  \item \textbf{Weibull.} The second line follows from the substitution $u = (x/\lambda)^k$, which turns $\mathbb{E}[N_f^{-r}]$ into
    \begin{equation}
      \lambda^{-r}\int_0^\infty u^{-r/k}e^{-u}\,\mathrm{d}u;
    \end{equation}
    this integral diverges \emph{at the origin} when $r \ge k$.
  \item \textbf{Gumbel-minimum.} The third line writes $\ln N_f = \mu + \beta G$ with $\Pr(G \le g) = 1 - e^{-e^{g}}$.
    The transform
    \begin{equation}
      \mathbb{E}[e^{tG}] \;=\; \int_0^\infty u^{t}e^{-u}\,\mathrm{d}u \;=\; \Gamma(1+t)
    \end{equation}
    (substitute $u = e^g$) is finite if and only if $t > -1$.
\end{itemize}
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:miner_loop}
\begin{proof}[Derivation that the loop condition~\eqref{eq:miner_loop_det} chains]
Unlike the scalar bound of Section~\ref{sec:loop_routeI}, which loses information, the condition~\eqref{eq:miner_loop_det} \emph{chains}, for two reasons.
First, $M$ is a sufficient statistic: the failure probability is the fixed increasing function $F_A$ of the scalar state.
Second, the map $\Pi$ of the operating phase is a composition of $m \mapsto m + c$ ($c \ge 0$), $m \mapsto (1-\rho)m$ and $m \mapsto 0$, and each of these maps is non-decreasing.
So, from
\begin{equation}
  M_T \;=\; \Pi(M_{H_1}) \;\le\; M_{H_1},
\end{equation}
induction gives
\begin{equation}
  \Pi^{n+1}(M_{H_1}) \;\le\; \Pi^{n}(M_{H_1}) \qquad \text{for all } n.
\end{equation}
The same monotonicity, applied to the partial maps, bounds every trajectory within a repetition by the trajectory of the operating phase on the horizon.
So imposing~\eqref{eq:miner_quantile} on all $k = 1,\dots,T$ extends the per-day reliability to every repetition, replacements (which draw a new~$A$) included.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Stochastic orderings and maintenance strategies}

\phantomsection\label{drv:order_gamma}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the Gamma rows of Tables~\ref{tab:ordering_lr}--\ref{tab:ordering_st}]
The log-likelihood ratio of the law $\mathrm{Ga}(\alpha_2,\beta_2)$ over the law $\mathrm{Ga}(\alpha_1,\beta_1)$ has derivative
\begin{equation}
  (\alpha_2 - \alpha_1)/x - (\beta_2 - \beta_1),
\end{equation}
