which is non-negative for all $x > 0$ if and only if $\alpha_2 \ge \alpha_1$ and $\beta_2 \le \beta_1$.
Conversely, if $\alpha_1 > \alpha_2$, then
\begin{equation}
  F_X(t) \sim (\beta_1 t)^{\alpha_1}/(\alpha_1\,\Gamma(\alpha_1)) \ll (\beta_2 t)^{\alpha_2}/(\alpha_2\,\Gamma(\alpha_2)) \sim F_Y(t)
\end{equation}
as $t \to 0^+$, which violates $\le_{\mathrm{st}}$.
If $\beta_1 < \beta_2$, then $\bar F_X(t)/\bar F_Y(t) \to \infty$ as $t \to \infty$, which again violates $\le_{\mathrm{st}}$.
Hence all three orders coincide for the Gamma family~\cite[Sec.~1.C]{ShakedShanthikumar2007}.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:order_gauss}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the Gaussian rows of Tables~\ref{tab:ordering_lr}--\ref{tab:ordering_st}]
The CDF of $\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^2)$ is $\Phi\bigl((t-\mu)/\sigma\bigr)$.
The inequality
\begin{equation}
  \Phi\bigl((t-\mu_1)/\sigma_1\bigr) \ge \Phi\bigl((t-\mu_2)/\sigma_2\bigr)
\end{equation}
for all~$t$ requires
\begin{equation}
  (t-\mu_1)/\sigma_1 \ge (t-\mu_2)/\sigma_2
\end{equation}
for all~$t$.
This condition is linear in~$t$, and it can hold for all~$t$ only if $\sigma_1 = \sigma_2$.
It then reduces to $\mu_1 \le \mu_2$.
The likelihood ratio is then
\begin{equation}
  \exp\bigl((\mu_2-\mu_1)\,x/\sigma^2 + \text{const}\bigr),
\end{equation}
non-decreasing in~$x$.
Thus all three orders coincide: $\sigma_1 = \sigma_2$ and $\mu_1 \le \mu_2$.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:order_ig}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the inverse Gaussian row of Table~\ref{tab:ordering_lr}]
The log-likelihood ratio of $\mathrm{IG}(\mu_2,\lambda_2)$ over $\mathrm{IG}(\mu_1,\lambda_1)$ has derivative
\begin{equation}
  \frac{\lambda_1}{2\mu_1^2} - \frac{\lambda_2}{2\mu_2^2} + \frac{\lambda_2 - \lambda_1}{2x^2}.
\end{equation}
This is non-negative for all $x > 0$ if and only if $\lambda_2 \ge \lambda_1$ (from $x \to 0^+$) and $\lambda_1/\mu_1^2 \ge \lambda_2/\mu_2^2$ (from $x \to \infty$).
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:order_cp}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the compound Poisson row of Table~\ref{tab:ordering_st}]
Sufficiency follows from the superposition coupling
\begin{equation}
  \mathrm{CP}(\nu_2, F_Y) \stackrel{d}{=} \mathrm{CP}(\nu_1, F_Y) + \mathrm{CP}(\nu_2 - \nu_1, F_Y),
\end{equation}
with independent non-negative summands.
Necessity follows from the atom at zero, $\Pr(X = 0) = e^{-\nu \Pr(Y > 0)}$.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{prf:prop:ig_order}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:ig_order}]
Let $c_t = w_t a_t$ and $A = \sum_t c_t$.
If $A = 0$, then $D = 0$.
Otherwise, the map $x \mapsto (x-d)_+$ is convex and non-decreasing, and $(A - d)_+ \le \mathbb{E}(AZ - d)_+$ by Jensen's inequality, since $\mathbb{E}Z = 1$.
Hence, for every $d \ge 0$,
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb{E}(D - d)_+
  = \mathbb{E}\Bigl(\sum_t \tfrac{c_t}{A}\,(A Z_t) - d\Bigr)_+
  \le \sum_t \tfrac{c_t}{A}\,\mathbb{E}(A Z_t - d)_+
  \le \mathbb{E}(A Z - d)_+
  \le \mathbb{E}(\mu Z - d)_+ . \qedhere
\end{equation}
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{prf:thm:ig_cap}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:ig_cap}]
If $\mu = 0$, then $\mathbb{E}D \le 0$ and $D = 0$.
Let $\mu > 0$ and $d = \mu q$; then
\begin{equation}
  d < \mu\bar q \le \tau.
\end{equation}
For every $x$,
\begin{equation}
  \mathbf{1}\{x > \tau\} \le (x-d)_+/(\tau - d),
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb{E}(Z - q)_+ = \varepsilon\,(\bar q - q).
\end{equation}
Therefore
\begin{equation}
  \Pr(D > \tau)
  \le \frac{\mathbb{E}(D-d)_+}{\tau - d}
  \le \frac{\mu\,\mathbb{E}(Z-q)_+}{\tau - \mu q}
  = \frac{\varepsilon\,\mu\,(\bar q - q)}{\tau - \mu q}
  \le \varepsilon,
\end{equation}
where the last step is $\mu\bar q \le \tau$.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:ig_tailmean}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the tail mean of the inverse Gaussian law]
Substituting into the density $f_Z$ of~$Z$ gives $z\,f_Z(z) = z^{-2} f_Z(1/z)$, and the change of variable $u = 1/z$ gives
\begin{equation}
  \int_q^\infty z\, f_Z(z)\, \mathrm{d}z = \int_0^{1/q} f_Z(u)\, \mathrm{d}u = F_Z(1/q). \qedhere
\end{equation}
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:ig_sharp}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the sharpness of the CVaR cap]
Take $\mu > \bar\mu$ and a level $\varepsilon' > \varepsilon$ close to~$\varepsilon$, with quantile~$q'$; then still $\mu\,\mathbb{E}[Z \mid Z > q'] > \tau$.
The two-point law
\begin{equation}
  \mu\,\mathbb{E}\bigl[Z \mid \mathbf{1}\{Z > q'\}\bigr]
\end{equation}
lies below $\mu Z$ in the order of Definition~\ref{def:icx}, by Jensen's inequality, and exceeds~$\tau$ with probability at least~$\varepsilon'$.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:ig_bracket}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the search interval of the inverse Gaussian quantile]
The right end of the interval in $u = \ln q$ is $\ln b$, with
\begin{equation}
  b = 1 + \sqrt{\eta\,(2/\varepsilon - 1)} > 1.
\end{equation}
$Z$ has the mean~$1$ and the variance~$\eta$, so Cantelli's inequality~\eqref{eq:cantelli_surrogate} at the level~$b$ gives
\begin{equation}
  \Pr(Z > b) \le \frac{\eta}{\eta + (b-1)^2} = \frac{\eta}{\eta + \eta\,(2/\varepsilon - 1)} = \frac{\varepsilon}{2} < \varepsilon.
\end{equation}
Therefore $\ln\Pr(Z > e^u) - \ln\varepsilon < 0$ at $u = \ln b$, and the quantile satisfies $q < b$.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{prf:lem:gamma_class}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:gamma_class}]
Since the last replacement (or day~$0$),
\begin{equation}
  D_k = \delta + \sum_t w_t X_t.
\end{equation}
The $X_t$ are the independent Gamma terms added since then, $\delta \ge 0$ collects the known constants, and each weight $w_t \in [0,1]$ is a product of factors $1-\rho$.
A term of weight~$0$, possible when $\rho = 1$, is dropped.
A mission adds a term with weight~$1$.
$\text{ARD}_\infty$ multiplies every weight, constants included, by $1-\rho$.
$\text{ARD}_1$ multiplies by $1-\rho$ the weights of the terms and constants added since the last intervention, and keeps the others.
A replacement starts a new sum with the replacement draw.
When the anchor before the first intervention is below the initial mean, the initial damage is read as two independent terms of its rate, with means equal to the anchor and to the rest.
Their sum has the law of the initial damage.
A known initial damage is read as two constants in the same way.
Write $X_t = Y_t/\beta_t$ with $Y_t \sim \mathrm{Ga}(a_t, 1)$ and $\beta_t \ge \beta^*$.
Then
\begin{equation}
  \beta^* D_k = \beta^*\delta + \sum_t (w_t\beta^*/\beta_t)\, Y_t,
\end{equation}
and every scale $w_t\beta^*/\beta_t$ lies in $(0,1]$.
The $Y_t$ are independent, so the law of $\beta^* D_k$ is in $\mathcal{G}(\beta^*\,\mathbb{E}[D_k])$.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{prf:lem:gamma_rates}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:gamma_rates}]
$S$ is infinitely divisible, with drift~$\delta$ and L\'evy density
\begin{equation}
  \Lambda(y) = \sum_i (m_i/c_i)\, e^{-y/c_i}/y
\end{equation}
on $y > 0$.
A change of the parameters at the rates $(\dot\delta, \dot\Lambda)$ changes the Laplace transform
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb{E}e^{-\vartheta S} = \exp\bigl(-\vartheta\delta - \int_0^\infty (1 - e^{-\vartheta y})\,\Lambda(y)\, \mathrm{d}y\bigr)
\end{equation}
at the rate
\begin{equation}
  \int_0^\infty \mathbb{E}\bigl[e^{-\vartheta(S+y)} - e^{-\vartheta S}\bigr]\,\dot\Lambda(y)\, \mathrm{d}y - \vartheta\dot\delta\,\mathbb{E}e^{-\vartheta S}.
\end{equation}
Since $\int_0^\infty e^{-\vartheta t}\,\Pr(S > t)\,\mathrm{d}t = (1 - \mathbb{E}e^{-\vartheta S})/\vartheta$, this rate times $-1/\vartheta$ is the transform of the rate
\begin{equation}
  \int_0^\infty \Pr(t - y < S \le t)\,\dot\Lambda(y)\, \mathrm{d}y + \dot\delta\, f_S(t)
\end{equation}
of $\Pr(S > t)$.
Case~(a) is $\dot\Lambda(y) = c^{-1}e^{-y/c}/y$, or $\dot\delta = 1$ when $c = 0$.
Case~(b) is
\begin{equation}
  \dot\Lambda(y) = m_i\,\partial_c\bigl(c^{-1}e^{-y/c}/y\bigr)
\end{equation}
at $c = c_i$.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{prf:thm:gamma_oneterm}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:gamma_oneterm}]
Let $\mathcal{G}_N(m)$ be the elements with at most $N$ atoms, the constant included, and let $N \ge 3$.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{A maximiser exists.} $\mathcal{G}_N(m)$ is the image of the compact set of atoms $(m_i, c_i)$ with $m_i \ge 0$, $\sum_i m_i = m$, $c_i \in [0, 1]$.
    The law of~$S$ depends continuously on the atoms, and only the constant element $S = m$ has an atom of probability, at $m \ne t$.
    So $\Pr(S > t)$ is continuous and attains its maximum at some~$S^*$.
    Since
    \begin{equation}
      \Pr(m > t) = 0 < Q(m, t),
    \end{equation}
    $S^*$ has at least one term.
    Every element has finitely many atoms, so
    \begin{equation}
      W(m, t) = \sup_N \max_{\mathcal{G}_N(m)} \Pr(S > t).
    \end{equation}
  \item \emph{First-order conditions.} Write $\omega^\circ = \omega_{S^*}$.
    Lemma~\ref{lem:gamma_rates} gives three conditions.
    \begin{enumerate}[nosep,label=(\roman*)]
      \item Moving mean between two atoms of positive mean must not raise the tail, so $\omega^\circ$ takes one value $\omega^*$ at every atom of positive mean.
      \item At an atom with $c \in (0, 1)$, $\partial\omega^\circ/\partial c = 0$.
      \item If $S^*$ has fewer than $N$ atoms of positive mean, a new atom can be opened at any $c \in [0, 1]$, so $\omega^\circ(c) \le \omega^*$ on $[0, 1]$.
    \end{enumerate}
  \item \emph{A single-peak function.} Put $\gamma = 1/c$ and
    \begin{equation}
      h(u) = \Pr(t - u < S^* \le t)/u.
    \end{equation}
    Then
    \begin{equation}
      \omega^\circ(\gamma) = \gamma\int_0^\infty h(u)\, e^{-\gamma u}\, \mathrm{d}u,
    \end{equation}
    and
    \begin{equation}
      \omega^\circ(\infty) = h(0^+) = f_{S^*}(t)
    \end{equation}
    is the value at the constant atom.
    $S^*$ is a constant plus a sum of independent Gamma variables, so its density is unimodal~\cite[Theorem~4, p.~11]{RoostaKhorasaniSzekely2015}, proved in~\cite[proof of Theorem~4, pp.~194--195]{SzekelyBakirov2003}; the constant shifts the mode only.
    Then $p(v) = f_{S^*}(t - v)$ for $v < t$ and $p(v) = 0$ for $v \ge t$ is unimodal on $[0, \infty)$, and so is its running average
    \begin{equation}
      h(u) = u^{-1}\int_0^u p:
    \end{equation}
    before the mode of~$p$, $p \ge h$; after it,
    \begin{equation}
      u\,(p(u) - h(u)) = u\,p(u) - \int_0^u p
    \end{equation}
    is non-increasing.
    Since $h' = (p - h)/u$, $h$ rises and then falls.
  \item \emph{Zero count.} For every constant $\omega'$, $h - \omega'$ changes sign at most twice.
    The Laplace transform of a function with at most $n$ sign changes has at most $n$ zeros in $\gamma > 0$, counted with multiplicity.
    Indeed, at a sign change~$u_1$, multiply the transform by $e^{\gamma u_1}$.
    Its derivative in~$\gamma$ is the transform of a function with one sign change fewer, and Rolle's theorem and induction conclude.
    So
    \begin{equation}
      \omega^\circ - \omega' = \gamma\int_0^\infty (h - \omega')\, e^{-\gamma u}\, \mathrm{d}u
    \end{equation}
    has at most two zeros in $\gamma > 0$.
    Also $\omega^\circ(\gamma) \to 0$ as $\gamma \to 0^+$.
    Hence $\omega^\circ$ cannot rise above a level $\omega' > 0$, fall below it and rise above it again: with $\omega^\circ(0^+) = 0$ that would give three zeros.
    So $\omega^\circ$ is quasi-concave.
    It is analytic in $\gamma > 0$ and not constant, so its maximum over $\gamma \in [1, \infty]$ is attained at one point only: two maximisers would make $\omega^\circ$ constant between them.
  \item \emph{One term.} By (i) and (ii), an atom with $c \in (0, 1)$ is a zero of $\omega^\circ - \omega^*$ of multiplicity at least~$2$, and an atom at $c = 1$ a zero of multiplicity at least~$1$.
    The constant atom is at $\gamma = \infty$.
    By step~4, $S^*$ has at most two atoms of positive mean.
    As $N \ge 3$, (iii) applies, so $\omega^*$ is the maximum of $\omega^\circ$ over $[1, \infty]$, attained at every atom of positive mean.
    By step~4 there is only one such atom, and by step~1 it is not the constant.
    So $S^* = c^*\,\mathrm{Ga}(m/c^*, 1)$ with $c^* \in (0, 1]$.
\end{enumerate}
Therefore
\begin{equation}
  \max_{\mathcal{G}_N(m)} \Pr(S > t) = \max_{c \in (0,1]} Q(m/c, t/c)
\end{equation}
for every $N \ge 3$, and the claim follows from step~1.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{prf:lem:gamma_monotone}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:gamma_monotone}]
Let $m < m' < t$ and let the law of $S$ be in $\mathcal{G}(m)$.
Then the law of $S + (m' - m)$ is in $\mathcal{G}(m')$, and $\Pr(S + m' - m > t) \ge \Pr(S > t)$.
Therefore $W(m, t) \le W(m', t)$.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:gamma_w}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the properties of the function~$w$]
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item $w$ is non-decreasing in~$A$, because $Q(a, x)$ increases in~$a$.
    It is continuous on $[0, A']$ for every $A' < t$.
    By Chebyshev's inequality $Q(A/c, t/c) \le cA/(t - A)^2$, so $w(A) \le A/(t - A)^2 \to 0 = w(0)$ as $A \to 0$.
    On $[\delta, A']$, with $\delta > 0$, the maximum can therefore be taken over a compact interval $[c_0, 1]$, on which $Q(A/c, t/c)$ is continuous in $(A, c)$.
  \item $w(A) < 1/2$ for every $A < t$: with $r = A/t < 1$ and $s = t/c$,
    \begin{equation}
      Q(rs, s) \le Q(rs, rs) < 1/2,
    \end{equation}
    because the median of a Gamma law lies below its mean.
  \item $w(A) \to 1/2$ as $A \to t$, because $Q(s, s) \to 1/2$ as $s \to \infty$.
\end{itemize}
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{prf:prop:gamma_safe}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:gamma_safe}]
Let $m = \beta^*\,\mathbb{E}[D_k]$ and $A = A_1(\beta^*\tau, \varepsilon)$, so
\begin{equation}
  m \le \beta^*\mu_k \le A < t.
\end{equation}
If $m = 0$, then $D_k = 0$.
Otherwise
\begin{equation}
  \Pr(D_k > \tau) = \Pr(\beta^* D_k > t) \le W(m, t) \le W(A, t) = w(A) \le \varepsilon,
\end{equation}
by Lemma~\ref{lem:gamma_class}, Lemma~\ref{lem:gamma_monotone}, Theorem~\ref{thm:gamma_oneterm} and the choice of~$A$: in both cases of the definition, $A < t$ and $w(A) \le \varepsilon$.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:gamma_computation}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the computation of the Gamma cap]
Three facts justify the computation.
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
  \item A bisection on the first argument of~$Q$ finds the exact one-rate cap $\hat A$, the root of $Q(\hat A, t) = \varepsilon$, because $Q$ increases in its first argument.
    The inverse routine \texttt{gammaincinv} solves for the second argument and does not apply.
  \item The function $c \mapsto Q(A/c, t/c)$ tends to~$0$ as $c \to 0$ and has a single peak on $(0, 1]$.
    With $r = A/t$ and $s = t/c$, the function is $s \mapsto Q(rs, s)$ on $s \ge t$. A single peak of this function on $s \in [10^{-12}, 10^{8}]$ was checked numerically for $r$ from $10^{-5}$ to $0.999$, which covers the bisection for $\varepsilon \ge 10^{-5}$.
    A second check, on $200$ values of~$r$ from $10^{-10}$ to $10^{-5}$, found no second peak either.
    So a logarithmic grid of values of~$c$, refined by a golden-section search between the neighbours of the best grid point, finds the maximum.
  \item The bisection on~$A$ starts from the lower end $\varepsilon t$, which satisfies $w(\varepsilon t) \le \varepsilon$ by Markov's inequality $Q(a, x) \le a/x$.
    It returns the lower end of its last interval, so $w(A_1) \le \varepsilon$ holds at the returned value.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:gamma_below}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the sharp cap below the threshold~$t_1$]
With $r = A/t$ and $s = t/c$, $Q(A/c, t/c) = Q(rs, s)$, and $c \in (0, 1]$ means $s \ge t$.
Let $r^*(\varepsilon)$ be the value of~$r$ at which the peak value of $s \mapsto Q(rs, s)$ equals~$\varepsilon$. This peak lies at $s = t_1(\varepsilon)$, so $r^*(\varepsilon) = \hat A(t_1, \varepsilon)/t_1$. For $t \le t_1(\varepsilon)$ the peak lies at $s \ge t$, so the maximum does not depend on~$t$, and
\begin{equation}
  A_1(t, \varepsilon) = r^*(\varepsilon)\, t. \qedhere
\end{equation}
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:gamma_beta}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the monotonicity of the Gamma mean cap in~$\beta^*$]
A larger~$t$ shrinks the range $s \ge t$, so $A_1(t, \varepsilon)/t$ is non-decreasing in~$t$, and the mean cap
\begin{equation}
  A_1(\beta^*\tau, \varepsilon)/\beta^* = \tau\, A_1(t, \varepsilon)/t
\end{equation}
is non-decreasing in~$\beta^*$.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:mfacts_gamma}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of facts (M1)--(M3) for the Gamma model]
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item[(M1)] The repair factor $1-\rho$ lies in $[0, 1]$, and the mean increments are non-negative.
  \item[(M2)] The true mean equals the day map applied to the true statistics of the day before, and $\theta_0 \le \xi_0$ holds as for the Gaussian model below.
  \item[(M3)] If a value $\mu \le A_1/\beta^*$ is at least the true mean of day~$k$, then $\Pr(D_k > \tau) \le \varepsilon$, because the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:gamma_safe} uses only these two facts.
\end{itemize}
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{prf:prop:gauss_exact}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:gauss_exact}]
A mission adds an independent Gaussian term, and $\text{ARD}_\infty$ scales the damage by $1-\rho$; both keep the law Gaussian and give the first two cases.
Under $\text{ARD}_1$, $D^- = G + \Delta$, with $G$ the damage just after the last intervention and $\Delta$ the increments added since; $G$ and $\Delta$ are independent Gaussian variables by N1.
Then $D^+ = G + (1-\rho)\Delta$ is Gaussian, with mean $m_G + (1-\rho)(m^- - m_G)$ and variance
\begin{equation}
  V_G + (1-\rho)^2\, V_\Delta = V_G + (1-\rho)^2\,(V^- - V_G) = (1-\rho)^2\, V^- + \bigl(1 - (1-\rho)^2\bigr)\, V_G .
\end{equation}
A replacement starts from the replacement draw.
Therefore the law is Gaussian on every day, with the moments of the maps.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:gauss_validity}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the validity of the Gaussian row]
Let $(m_k, V_k) \le (\mu_k, v_k)$ be the true moments (Proposition~\ref{prop:gauss_exact} and Proposition~\ref{prop:domination}).
If $V_k = 0$, then
\begin{equation}
  D_k = m_k \le \tau.
\end{equation}
Otherwise $\mu_k < \tau$, because $\mu_k = \tau$ forces $v_k = 0$, and
\begin{equation}
  \Pr(D_k > \tau) = 1 - \Phi\bigl((\tau - m_k)/\sqrt{V_k}\bigr) \le 1 - \Phi\bigl((\tau - \mu_k)/\sqrt{v_k}\bigr) \le \varepsilon,
\end{equation}
because $(\tau - \mu)/\sqrt{v}$ decreases in~$\mu$ and in~$v$.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:mfacts_gauss}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of facts (M1)--(M3) for the Gaussian model]
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item[(M1)] The repair factors $1-\rho$ and $(1-\rho)^2$ lie in $[0, 1]$, and the mean and variance increments are non-negative.
  \item[(M2)] $\theta_0 \le \xi_0$: the initial values are the moments of the initial damage, and each starting anchor is at least the moment of the part of the damage present just after the last intervention before day~$1$ (Section~\ref{sec:repair_connection}).
    On every later day, the true moments equal the maps applied to the true statistics of the day before (Proposition~\ref{prop:gauss_exact}).
  \item[(M3)] If values $(\mu, v)$ that satisfy the row are at least the true moments of day~$k$, then $\Pr(D_k > \tau) \le \varepsilon$ (the derivation of the validity of the Gaussian row, Appendix~\ref{drv:gauss_validity}, page~\pageref{drv:gauss_validity}).
\end{itemize}
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:wiener_path}
\begin{proof}[Derivation for a repair of the Wiener damage path]
A repair of the damage path, as in the Gaussian model, would give the same mean and the variance
\begin{equation}
  (1-\rho)^2 V \quad \text{under } \text{ARD}_\infty,
  \qquad
  (1-\rho)^2 V + \bigl(1 - (1-\rho)^2\bigr)\,V_G \quad \text{under } \text{ARD}_1,
\end{equation}
with $V_G \le V$ the variance just after the last intervention.
Both are at most~$V$, and the reliability row increases in~$v$, so the rows of the Wiener model are safe for that reading as well.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:mfacts_ig}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of facts (M1)--(M3) for the inverse Gaussian model]
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item[(M1)] The repair factor $1-\rho$ lies in $[0, 1]$, and the mean increments are non-negative.
  \item[(M2)] $\theta_0 \le \xi_0$ holds as for the Gaussian model, and the true mean equals the day map applied to the true statistics of the day before.
  \item[(M3)] If a value $\mu \le \bar\mu$ is at least the true mean of day~$k$, then $\Pr(D_k > \tau) \le \varepsilon$, by Proposition~\ref{prop:ig_order} with this~$\mu$ and Theorem~\ref{thm:ig_cap}.
\end{itemize}
Each day of each repetition has its own set of terms.
The argument holds for each of them, because it needs neither independence nor a fixed set of terms.
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:mfacts_rf}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of facts (M1)--(M3) for the rainflow model]
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item[(M1)] The repair factors $1-\rho$ (for the mean and the cumulant generating function) and $(1-\rho)^2$ (for the variance and the Hoeffding budget) lie in $[0, 1]$, and the increments of the four statistics are non-negative.
    The cumulant generating function of an increment is at least $s$ times its mean, which is non-negative, by Jensen's inequality and A0.
  \item[(M2)] $\theta_0 \le \xi_0$ holds as for the Gaussian model.
    On every later day the true statistics are at most the day maps applied to the true statistics of the day before, with equality for the mean, the variance and the Hoeffding budget, and an inequality for the cumulant generating function (Proposition~\ref{prop:rf_maps}).
  \item[(M3)] If values that satisfy the row of the bound are at least the true statistics of day~$k$, then $\Pr(D_k > \tau) \le \varepsilon$ (the derivations of Section~\ref{sec:cons_surrogates}).
\end{itemize}
\end{proof}

\phantomsection\label{drv:repair_maps}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the repair maps of the mean and the variance]
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Mean.} $a = 1-\rho$, because expectation is linear: Eq.~\eqref{eq:ard1} gives
    \begin{equation}
      \mathbb{E}[D^+] = (1-\rho)\,\mathbb{E}[D^-] + \rho\,\mathbb{E}[D^{\mathrm{last}}].
    \end{equation}
  \item \textbf{Variance and Hoeffding budget.} $a = (1-\rho)^2$.
    Under $\text{ARD}_1$ the reason is that $D^+ = G + (1-\rho)\Delta$, where $G$ is the damage just after the last intervention and $\Delta$ the damage added since, independent of~$G$.
    So
    \begin{equation}
      \operatorname{Var}[D^+] = \operatorname{Var}[G] + (1-\rho)^2\operatorname{Var}[\Delta] = (1-\rho)^2\operatorname{Var}[D^-] + \bigl(1-(1-\rho)^2\bigr)\operatorname{Var}[G],
    \end{equation}
    because $\operatorname{Var}[D^-] = \operatorname{Var}[G] + \operatorname{Var}[\Delta]$.
    Under $\text{ARD}_\infty$, $\operatorname{Var}[(1-\rho)D^-] = (1-\rho)^2\operatorname{Var}[D^-]$.
    The Hoeffding budget $R = \sum_t w_t^2 r_t$ follows the same map, because a repair multiplies by $1-\rho$ the weight $w_t$ of each term that it repairs (Proposition~\ref{prop:rf_maps}).
\end{itemize}
\end{proof}

\subsection{Electric vehicles and implementation}

\phantomsection\label{drv:bigM_validity}
\begin{proof}[Derivation of the validity of the big-M constants of Section~\ref{sec:impl_encoding}]
An intervention day is a depot day, so $\delta s_k = 0$ on it.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item The carry row with an intervention reads $s_k \ge s_{k-1} - M_s$, whose right-hand side is at most~$0$.
  \item The repair row without a repair has a right-hand side
    \begin{equation}
      a\,s_{k-1} + (1-a)\,g_{k-1} - M_s \le 0,
    \end{equation}
    because the repair map is a convex combination of values in $[0, M_s]$.
  \item The replacement rows without a replacement allow
    \begin{equation}
      s_k \in [s^{\mathrm{new}} - M_s,\, s^{\mathrm{new}} + M_s] \supseteq [0, M_s].
    \end{equation}
  \item The relaxed anchor rows allow every difference in $[-M_s, M_s]$.
\end{itemize}
\end{proof}
